Dr. Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel,

Professor of Philosophy at the University

Heidelberg,

at August Oswald’s University Bookshop.

1817.

[p. III]

The need to put into the hands of my listeners a guide to my philosophical lectures is the immediate occasion for my letting this survey of the entire range of philosophy see the light earlier than would otherwise have been my thought.

The nature of an outline not only excludes a more exhaustive elaboration of the ideas as regards their *content*, but in particular also constrains the elaboration of their systematic

[p. IV]

derivation, which must contain what used to be understood by *proof*, and what is indispensable to a scientific philosophy. The title was meant to indicate partly the range of a whole, partly the intention of reserving the details for oral delivery.

With an outline, however, what then comes into consideration is more merely an *external purposiveness* of arrangement and organization, when it is an already presupposed and familiar content that is to be presented with deliberate brevity. Since the present exposition is not in this case, but sets out a new treatment of philosophy according to a method which, I hope, will yet be recognized as the only true one, the one identical with the content,

[p. V]

I could have held it to be more advantageous for it, with respect to the public, had circumstances permitted me to let a more detailed work on the other parts of philosophy precede it, like the one I have delivered to the public on the first part of the whole, the *Logic*. For the rest, although in the present exposition the side by which the content lies closer to *representation* and to empirical acquaintance had to be restricted, I believe that, with respect to the transitions, which can only be a mediation to be accomplished through the *Concept*, I have made it noticeable enough that the method of the progression is sufficiently distinguished both from the merely *external order* which the other sciences seek out, and from what has become a customary

[p. VI]

*manner* with philosophical objects, one that *presupposes* a *schema* and with it parallels the materials just as externally as the first way does, and still more arbitrarily, and, through the strangest misunderstanding, claims to have done justice to the necessity of the Concept with the contingency and arbitrariness of the connections.

We have seen the same arbitrariness also take possession of the content of philosophy and set out on adventures of thought, for a time impose upon genuine-minded and honest striving, but otherwise also be taken for an absurdity heightened even to madness. Instead of the impressive or the mad, the import more properly and more often let well-known

[p. VII]

trivialities be recognized, just as the form let the mere manner of a deliberate, methodical and easily acquired wit of baroque connections and of a forced eccentricity be recognized, and, in general, behind the mien of earnestness, deception of oneself and of the public. On the other side, by contrast, we saw shallowness stamp *the lack of thoughts* into a skepticism clever in its own eyes and a criticism modest about reason, and, with the emptiness of ideas, increase its conceit and vanity in the same degree. — These two tendencies of spirit have for a considerable time aped German earnestness, wearied its deeper philosophical need, and had as their consequence an indifference, indeed even such contempt for the science of philosophy, that now even a self-styled

[p. VIII]

modesty believes it may take the liberty of joining in the talk about what is deepest in philosophy and pronouncing against it, and of denying it the rational cognition whose form was formerly understood under *proving*.

The first of the phenomena mentioned can in part be regarded as the youthful zest of the new epoch that has dawned in the realm of science as in the political realm. If this zest greeted the dawn of the rejuvenated spirit with giddy rapture, and without deeper labor went straight to the enjoyment of the Idea and reveled for a time in the hopes and prospects it offered, it more easily reconciles us to its excesses, because a kernel lies at its basis, and the superficial haze which

[p. IX]

it has spread around it must dissipate of itself. The other phenomenon, however, is more repugnant, because it betrays exhaustion and impotence, and strives to cover them with a conceit that schoolmasters the philosophical minds of all centuries and misjudges them, but most of all misjudges itself.

All the more gratifying is it, however, to perceive, and still to mention, how against both the philosophical interest and the earnest love of *higher cognition* have preserved themselves, unprejudiced and without vanity. If this interest has at times thrown itself rather upon the form of an *immediate* knowing and of *feeling*, it attests on the other hand the inner, further-reaching drive of rational insight, which alone gives

[p. X]

man his dignity — most highly in this, that for it itself that standpoint becomes only a *result* of philosophical knowledge, so that what it seems to disdain is at least acknowledged by it as a *condition*. — To this interest in the *cognition of truth* I dedicate this attempt to furnish an introduction or contribution to its satisfaction; may such an aim win it a favorable reception.

Heidelberg, May 1817.

[p. XI]

#### Encyclopaedia

#### of the

#### Philosophical Sciences.

[scan 24]

### Introduction.

§. 1.

All sciences other than philosophy have objects of a kind that, as immediately granted by representation, are therefore also presupposed as accepted at the *beginning* of the science, just as the determinations held to be required in the further progression are likewise taken up from representation.

Such a science does not have to justify itself concerning the *necessity* of the object itself that it treats; mathematics in general, geometry, arithmetic, jurisprudence, medicine, zoology, botany, etc., are granted the right to presuppose that there *is* magnitude, space, number, a right, diseases, animals, plants, etc., i.e., these are assumed by representation as *present*; it occurs to no one to doubt the being of such objects and to demand that it be proved from the Concept that there must in and for itself be magnitude, space, etc., disease, the animal, the plant. — Of such an object it is first the *familiar name* that is stated. This is what is fixed, but at first it gives only

[p. 4]

the representation of the matter. But further determinations of it are also to be stated. These too can, indeed, be taken up from immediate representation. Here, however, the difficulty easily arises already that determinations are picked up of which it is just as *immediately granted* that they are present in the object, and likewise that they are the essential ones. As regards the formal side of this, logic, the doctrine of definitions, divisions, etc., is presupposed for it; as regards the content, however, it is permitted to proceed in an empirical way, seeking within oneself and others what sort of marks of this kind are *factually* found in the representation of the universal object; and this fact can then already be something very much subject to dispute.

§. 2.

The *beginning* of *philosophy*, on the other hand, has the inconvenience that its *object* is already, at once, necessarily subject to doubt and dispute, 1) as to its *import*, since, if it is to be stated not merely as an object of representation but as the *object of philosophy*, it is not found in representation, indeed is opposed to it as to its *manner of cognition*, and representing is rather to be brought beyond itself by philosophy.

§. 3.

2) as to its *form* it is exposed to the same embarrassment, because, in that a *beginning is made*, it is an *immediate* one, but is by its nature of such a kind that it is to present itself as something mediated, to be cognized through

[p. 5]

the *Concept* as *necessary*, and at the same time the manner of cognition and *method* cannot be presupposed, since their consideration falls within philosophy itself.

If it were a matter of nothing more than pointing out to *representation*, within representation itself, the entirely indeterminate object of philosophy, one could take refuge in the usual appeal: that the human being, who begins with *sensuous perception* and *desire*, soon feels himself driven beyond them, to the feeling and intimation of something *higher* than he is, of an *infinite* being and *infinite* will, — to the universal interest that the questions have: *what is the soul, what is the world, what is God? — what can I know, according to what ought I to act, what can I hope*, etc. More precisely, one could point to *religion* and its object. Leaving aside the fact that such questions and such objects can themselves at once be met with doubt and negation, immediate consciousness already contains in part, and religion still more, in its own way, the resolution of those questions and a doctrine about those objects. But what is peculiar to them, by which they are the *content of philosophy*, is not expressed by this. — For this reason, too, one cannot appeal, already *concerning the object*, to authority and universal agreement as to what is understood by philosophy. Already the demand made in the § for the cognition *of necessity through the Concept* is not granted, since there are many who think they have philosophy although they abstract precisely from the cognition of necessity, and their objects rather

[p. 6]

take from immediate feeling and intuition, and even call such immediacy of perceiving reason. Thus in this sense Newton and the English also call *experimental physics* philosophy, and hence also call electrical machines, magnetic apparatus, air pumps, etc., *philosophical instruments*, whereas surely not a construction of wood, iron, etc., but *thinking* alone could be called the instrument of philosophy.<sup>∗)</sup>

§. 4.

Since the object of philosophy is not an immediate one, its Concept, and the Concept of philosophy itself, can be grasped only within philosophy; what is said here of the former as well as of philosophy, properly *before* philosophy itself, is therefore something anticipated, as yet ungrounded for itself, but for that very reason also incontestable, and is to be taken with the purpose of providing an indeterminate, merely preliminary, historical acquaintance.

§. 5.

Philosophy is hereby presented here as the science of *reason*, and specifically insofar as reason becomes conscious of itself as *all being*.

∗) The journal currently published by Thomson, too, has the title: *Annals of Philosophy; or, Magazine of Chemistry, Mineralogy, Mechanics, Natural History, Agriculture, and the Arts*. — From this one can imagine for oneself what the materials are like that are here called *philosophical*.

[p. 7]

All other knowing than the philosophical is *knowing of the finite*, or *a finite knowing*, because in it reason in general, as something subjective, presupposes a given object, and thus does not cognize itself in it. Even if the objects are found in self-consciousness, like right, *duty*, etc., they are single objects, *beside* and *outside* of which, and thus also outside self-consciousness, the remaining wealth of the universe is to be found. The object of *religion* is indeed, for itself, the infinite object, which is to encompass everything within itself; but its representations do not remain true to themselves, in that for it the world outside the infinite also remains again independent, and what it gives out as the highest truth is supposed to remain at the same time unfathomable, a mystery and unknowable, something given, and only in the form of something *given* and *external* for the distinguishing consciousness. In it the true is in feeling, intuition, in intimation, in representation, in devotion in general, also interwoven with thoughts, but the truth not in the *form of truth*. In general, it constitutes a region *of its own*, *separated* from the rest of *consciousness*, even if its heart is all-embracing. — Philosophy can also be regarded as the science *of freedom*; since in it the foreignness of the objects, and with it the finitude of consciousness, disappears, there falls away in it alone contingency, natural necessity, and the relationship to an externality in general, hence dependence, longing and fear; only in philosophy is reason entirely *with itself*. — For the same reason, in this science reason also does not have the one-sidedness

[p. 8]

of a subjective rationality, neither as if it were the property of a peculiar talent or the gift of a special divine good fortune — or also misfortune — like the possession of artistic skill, but, since it is nothing but reason in the consciousness of itself, it is by its nature capable of being universal science. Nor is it *idealism*, in which the content of knowing has only the determination of a product *posited by the I*, of a *subjective* one enclosed within self-consciousness; since reason is conscious of itself as of *being*, subjectivity, the I that knows itself as a *particular* against the objects, and its determinations as in it and as distinct from something else located outside of or above it, is sublated and sunk into rational universality.

§. 6.

Philosophy is *encyclopaedia of the philosophical sciences* insofar as its whole scope is presented with the determinate indication of the parts, and it is *philosophical* encyclopaedia insofar as the separation and the connection of its parts are presented according to the necessity of the Concept.

Since philosophy is through and through rational knowing, each of its parts is a philosophical whole, a circle of totality closing itself within itself, but the philosophical Idea is therein in a particular determinacy or element. The single circle, for the very reason that it is totality in itself, also breaks through the limitation of its element and grounds a further sphere; the whole therefore presents itself as a circle of circles, each of which

[p. 9]

is a necessary moment, so that the system of their peculiar elements constitutes the whole Idea, which equally appears in each single one.

§. 7.

Philosophy is also *essentially* encyclopaedia, in that the true can be, only as totality, and only through differentiation and determination of its differences, the necessity of these and the freedom of the whole; it is therefore necessarily *system*.

A philosophizing *without system* can be nothing scientific; besides the fact that for itself it expresses rather a subjective frame of mind, it is contingent as to its content, in that the latter has its justification only as a moment of the whole, but outside it has an unfounded presupposition or subjective certainty.

§. 8.

By a *system* of philosophy there is falsely understood only a philosophy of a determinate *principle*, distinguished from others; on the contrary, it is the principle of true philosophy to contain within itself all particular principles. It shows this both in itself and in that its *history*, in the differently appearing philosophies, shows partly only one philosophy at different stages of development, partly that the particular principles, one of which lay at the basis of a system, are only branches of one and the same whole.

In this connection the *universal* and the *particular* must be distinguished according to their proper determination; the universal, taken formally and placed *beside* the particular, itself also becomes something

[p. 10]

particular. Such a placement would of itself strike one, in objects of common life, as inappropriate and awkward, as if, e.g., someone who demanded fruit were to refuse cherries, pears, grapes, etc., because they were cherries, pears, grapes, but *not* fruit. — With regard to philosophy, however, one permits oneself, partly to justify the disdain for it by the fact that there are so many different philosophies, and each is only *a* philosophy, not *the* philosophy, — as if cherries too were not fruit; — partly to place one whose principle is the universal *alongside* those whose principle is a particular one, indeed even alongside doctrines that assert that there is no philosophy at all, and use this name for a movement of thought that presupposes the true as given and immediate, and carries out reflections on it.

§. 9.

As *encyclopaedia*, however, the science is not to be presented in the detailed development of its particularization, but is to be restricted to the beginnings and basic concepts of the particular sciences.

How much of the particular parts is required to constitute a particular science is entirely indeterminate, insofar as the part must only be, not an isolated moment, but a totality, in order to be something true. The whole of philosophy therefore constitutes truly *one* science, but it can also be regarded as a whole of several particular sciences.

[p. 11]

§. 19.

Whatever is true in a science is so through and by virtue of philosophy, whose encyclopaedia therefore embraces all genuine sciences.

The philosophical encyclopaedia differs from another, ordinary encyclopaedia in that the latter is an *aggregate* of the sciences, which are taken up in a contingent and empirical way, and among which there are also such as bear only the name of sciences but otherwise are themselves a mere collection of items of knowledge. The unity into which the sciences are brought together in such an aggregate is, because they are taken up externally, likewise an *external* one, — *an order*. This must, for the same reason, and moreover because the materials too are of a contingent nature, remain an *attempt*, and always show unsuitable sides. — Besides, then, that the philosophical encyclopaedia excludes 1) such mere *aggregates* of items of knowledge — as, e.g., philology is —, so also, in any case, 2) such as have mere arbitrary will for their ground, as, e.g., heraldry; sciences of this kind are the *through and through positive* ones. 3) Other sciences are also called *positive*, which, however, have a rational ground and beginning; this part of them belongs to philosophy; the *positive side*, however, remains peculiar to them. Of this kind are the sciences existing for themselves outside philosophy, in general. The positive element of these is, however, of different kinds. 1) Their beginning, the truly true, has in them the contingent for its *end*, by the fact that they have to bring the universal down into *empirical singularity and actuality*.

[p. 12]

In this field of changeability and contingency, not the *Concept* but only *grounds* can be asserted. Jurisprudence, e.g., or the system of direct and indirect taxes, require *final, exact* decisions, which lie outside the *in-and-for-itself being-determined of the Concept*, and therefore admit a latitude for determination which can be conceived one way on one ground and another way on another, and is capable of no secure ultimate. Likewise the Idea of *nature* loses itself, in its individuation, in contingencies, and *natural history*, *geography*, *medicine* get into determinations of actuality, into species and differences that are determined by external chance and by play, not by reason; *history* too belongs here, insofar as the Idea is its essence, the appearance of which, however, is in contingency and in the field of arbitrary will. 2) Such sciences are also *positive* insofar as they do not cognize their concepts as *finite*, nor show the transition of these and of their whole sphere into a higher one, but accept them as *absolutely valid*. With this finitude of *form*, as the first is the finitude of the *material*, is connected 3) that of the *ground of cognition*, partly in that sciences conduct themselves *ratiocinatively*, but partly insofar as feeling, belief, the authority of others, in general of inner or outer intuition, are the *ground of cognition*, to which belong religion as well as philosophy, which wants to ground itself on anthropology, facts of consciousness, inner intuition or outer experience, — as also natural history, etc. 4) It can further be that merely the *form of scientific presentation*

[p. 13]

is empirical and conceptless, but otherwise meaningful intuition arranges what are only appearances in such a way as the inner sequence of the Concept is. For this there is further required that through the opposition and manifoldness of the appearances assembled, the *external, contingent circumstances* of the conditions sublate themselves, whereby the *universal* then steps before the sense. — A discerning experimental physics, history, etc., would in this way present the rational science of nature and of human events and deeds in an external image that mirrors the Concept.

§. 11.

The whole of the science is the exposition of the Idea; its *division* can therefore first be comprehended only out of this. Because the Idea now is the reason equal to itself, which, in order to be for itself, places itself over against itself and is an other to itself, but in this other is equal to itself, the science falls into the 3 parts: 1) *logic*, the science of the Idea in and for itself; 2) the *philosophy of nature* as the science of the Idea in its otherness; 3) the *philosophy of spirit*, as of the Idea that returns into itself out of its otherness.

1) The division of a science, which is sent ahead of the science itself, is at first an external reflection on its object, for the differences of its Concept can result only through the cognition of it, which however is precisely the science itself. Thus the division of philosophy is an anticipation of what produces itself through the Idea's own necessity itself. 2) Above, § 6, it has already been remarked that the differences of the particular

[p. 14]

sciences are only determinations of the Idea itself, and that it is only the Idea that presents itself in these different elements. In nature it is not an other than the Idea that would be cognized, but it is in the form of *externalization*; just as, in spirit, the very same *as being for itself* and *becoming in and for itself*. Such a determination, in which the Idea appears, is, further, a fluid moment; therefore the single science is just as much this, to cognize its content as an object that *is*, as also this, at the same time and immediately therein to cognize its transition into its higher circle. The *representation of the division* therefore has this incorrectness, that it places the particular parts or sciences *beside one another*, as if they were only resting and, in their differentiation, substantial ones, like *species*.

[p. 15]

A.

### The Science of Logic.

#### Preliminary Concept.

§. 12.

Logic is the science of the *pure Idea*, that is, of the Idea in the abstract element of *thinking*.

What holds of the determinations contained in this Preliminary Concept is the same as what holds of the Concepts sent ahead concerning philosophy in general: that they are likewise anticipations, or, what is the same, determinations stated from and according to the survey of the whole. — One can indeed say that logic is the science of *thinking*, of its *determinations* and *laws*; but thinking is at first the *pure identity* of knowing with itself, and therefore constitutes only the universal determinacy or the element in which the Idea is as logical. The Idea is indeed thinking, but not as formal thinking; rather as the totality of its own peculiar determinations, which it gives itself. — Logic is in this respect the *most difficult* science, in that it has to do not with intuitions, — not even, like geometry, with abstract —

[p. 16]

or otherwise with sensuous representations, but with pure abstractions, and requires a power to withdraw into pure thought, to hold it fast, and to move in such thoughts. On the other hand it could be regarded as the *easiest*, because the content is nothing but one’s own thinking and its familiar determinations, and these are at the same time the *simplest*. — The *utility* of logic concerns the relationship to the subject, insofar as it gives itself a certain formation for other ends. The formation through logic consists in this, that it is exercised in thinking, because this science is thinking of thinking. But insofar as the logical is the absolute form of truth and, still more than this, also pure truth itself, it is something quite different from merely something *useful*.

§. 13.

The logical has, in respect of form, three sides: α) the *abstract or of the understanding*, β) the *dialectical* or *negatively rational*, γ) the *speculative* or *positively rational*.

These three sides do not make up three *parts* of logic, but are *moments of every logical-real*, that is, of every Concept, or of everything true in general. They can again be posited under the first moment, that *of the understanding*, and thereby be held apart from one another in isolation, but in that case they are then not considered in their truth.

§. 14.

α) Thinking as *understanding* stops at the fixed determinacy and the distinctness of it from others, and such a limited abstract counts for it as subsisting for itself and as being.

[p. 17]

§. 15.

β) The *dialectical* moment is the self-sublation proper to such determinations, and their passing over into their opposite.

1) The dialectical, taken apart by the understanding for itself, constitutes, in particular when shown in scientific concepts, *skepticism*; it contains mere negation as the result of the dialectical. 2) Dialectic is usually regarded as an external art, which by arbitrary will produces a confusion in determinate concepts and a mere *semblance* of *contradictions* in them, so that not these determinations but this semblance is a nullity, and what is of the understanding, on the contrary, is rather the true. Dialectic is, however, rather to be regarded as the proper, true nature of the determinations of the understanding, of things, and of the finite in general. Reflection is at first the going beyond the isolated determinacy and a relating, whereby it is put into relationship but is otherwise retained in its isolated validity. Dialectic, on the other hand, is this *immanent* going beyond, in which the one-sidedness and limitedness of the determinations of the understanding present themselves as what they are, namely as their negation. The dialectical therefore constitutes the moving soul of progression, and is the principle through which alone *immanent connection and necessity* comes into the content of science, just as in it in general lies the true, not external, elevation above the finite:

[p. 18]

§. 16.

γ) The *speculative* or *positively rational* grasps the unity of the determinations in their opposition, the positive that is contained in their dissolution and in their passing over.

1) Dialectic has a *positive* result, because it has a *determinate content*, or because its result is truly not the *empty, abstract nothing*, but the negation of *certain determinations*, which are contained in the result precisely for that reason, because this is not an *immediate nothing* but a result. 2) This rational is therefore, although it is something thought and also something abstract, at the same time a *concrete*, because it is not *simple, formal* unity, but *unity of differentiated determinations*. With mere abstractions or formal thoughts, therefore, philosophy in general has absolutely nothing to do, but solely with concrete thoughts. 3) In speculative logic the mere *logic of the understanding* is contained, and can be made out of the former at once; nothing is needed for that except to leave out of it the dialectical and the rational; thus it becomes what *ordinary logic* is, a *history* of sundry assembled thought-determinations, which in their finitude count as something infinite.

§. 17.

As to *content*, in logic the determinations of thinking are considered *in and for themselves*. — In this way they are the concrete pure thoughts, i.e., *Concepts*, with the worth and significance of the *in-and-for-itself being*

[p. 19]

*ground of everything*. Logic is therefore essentially *speculative philosophy*.

In the speculative, *form* and *content* are in general not so separated as they were set apart in this and the preceding §; the *forms* of the Idea are its *determinations*, and it could not be said where another, true content should come from other than these its determinations themselves. The forms of the mere logic of the understanding, by contrast, are not only not something true for themselves, but cannot even be merely forms of the true; rather, since as merely formal they are burdened with the essential opposition to content, they are hereby nothing further than forms of the finite, of the untrue. — But because logic, as the *purely speculative* philosophy, is at first the Idea in *thinking*, or the absolute still shut up within its eternity, it is on the one hand the *subjective* and hence the *first* science; it still lacks the side of the complete *objectivity* of the Idea. But not only does it remain as the *absolute ground* of the real, but by showing itself as this it proves itself equally to be the *real-universal* and *objective* science. In the first universality of its Concepts it appears for itself and as the subjective, particular business, outside which the whole wealth of the sensuous, as well as of the more concrete, the intellectual, world would carry on its being. But since this too has been cognized in the philosophy of the real part, and the same has shown itself to be returning into the pure Idea and having therein its ultimate ground and truth, the logical universality thereby presents itself no longer as a particularity over against that real wealth, but rather as containing the same, as true universality;

[p. 20]

it then acquires the significance of *speculative theology*.

§. 18.

Logic, in the essential meaning of speculative philosophy, takes the place of what was otherwise called *metaphysics* and treated as a science separated from it. The nature of the logical, and the standpoint on which scientific cognition has placed itself, receives its closer, preliminary clarification from the nature of metaphysics and then of critical philosophy, through which metaphysics has reached its end. To this end the Concept of these sciences and the relationship of the logical to it are to be set forth in more detail. — Metaphysics, for the rest, is something *former* only in relation to the history of philosophy; for itself it is, as it has namely become in recent times, in general the mere *view of the understanding* of the objects of reason.

§. 14.

This science considered, then, the thought-determinations as *the fundamental determinations of things*; by this presupposition, that what is, by the very fact that it is *thought*, is cognized *in itself*, it stood higher than the more recent critical philosophizing; but 1) it took those determinations, in their abstraction, as valid for themselves and capable of being *predicates of the true*. It presupposed in general that the cognition of the absolute could take place in such a way that *predicates are attributed to it*, and investigated neither the determinations of the understanding according to their peculiar content and worth, nor also

[p. 21]

this form, of determining the absolute by the attribution of predicates.

Such predicates are, e.g., *determinate being*, as in the proposition: *God has determinate being*; *finitude* or *infinity*, in the question whether the world is finite or infinite; *simple*, *composite*, in the proposition: the soul is *simple*; — further, the thing is *one*, a *whole*, etc.

§. 20.

2) Their *objects* were indeed totalities, which belong in and for themselves to *reason*; — *soul, world, God* — but metaphysics took them up from *representation*, laid them down as *ready-made given subjects* as the basis in applying the determinations of the understanding to them, and had only in that representation the *standard* of whether the predicates are suitable and sufficient or not.

§. 21.

3) It thereby became *dogmatism*, because, by the nature of the finite determinations, it had to assume that of *two opposed assertions*, such as those propositions were, the one must be *true*, the other, however, *false*.

§. 22.

The *first part* of this metaphysics, after it had given itself an ordered shape, was constituted by *ontology*, the doctrine of the *abstract determinations of essence*. For the determinations, in their manifoldness and finite validity, there is a lack of a principle; they must therefore be taken up *empirically* and *contingently*, and their closer *content* can only be founded on *representation*, on the

[p. 22]

*assurance* that one thinks precisely this by a word, possibly also on etymology. Here it can only be a matter of the *correctness* of the analysis, agreeing with linguistic usage, and of empirical *completeness*, not of the *truth* and *necessity* of such determinations in and for themselves.

The question whether being, determinate being, or finitude, simplicity, composition, etc., are *true Concepts in and for themselves*, can then be striking if one supposes that there could be talk only of the *truth of a proposition*, and that the only question could be whether a *Concept is to be attributed to a subject* with truth (as it was called) or not; that untruth depends on the contradiction that would be found between the subject and the Concept to be predicated of it. In such a representation the Concept is taken as a simple determinacy. But the Concept is in general something concrete, and even every determinacy is essentially a unity of differentiated determinations. If truth, then, were nothing further than the lack of contradiction, then in the case of every Concept it would first have to be considered whether it does not contain such an inner contradiction.

§. 23.

The *second part* was *rational psychology*, or *pneumatology*, which concerns the metaphysical nature of the *soul*, namely of spirit as a *thing*.

Immortality was sought in the sphere where *composition, time, qualitative alteration, quantitative increase or decrease* have their place.

[p. 23]

§. 24.

The *third part*, *cosmology*, dealt with the *world*, its contingency, necessity, eternity, being-limited in space and time; the formal laws in its alterations; further with the freedom of the human being, and the origin of evil.

As absolute oppositions there count here chiefly: contingency and necessity; external and internal necessity; efficient and final causes, or causality in general and end; essence or substance and appearance; form and matter; freedom and necessity; happiness and pain; good and evil.

§. 25.

The *fourth part*, *natural* or *rational theology*, considered the Concept of God or its possibility, the proofs of his determinate being, and his properties.

a) In this consideration of God by the understanding, what matters chiefly is which predicates fit or do not fit what *we represent to ourselves by* God. The opposition of reality and negation, or of the positive and the negative, appears here as absolute. For the *Concept*, as the understanding takes it, there remains in the end only the empty abstraction of indeterminate essence, of pure reality or positivity. b) The *proving* of finite cognition contains either the inverted position that an objective ground of God’s being is to be given, and that this being thus presents itself as something *mediated*, or, insofar as the ground is to be only a subjective one for our cognition, then this proving, which

[p. 24]

proceeds along the identity of determinacies belonging to the understanding, cannot make the transition from the finite to the infinite, and thus cannot free God from the positively remaining finitude of the world that has determinate being, so that he would have to determine himself as the immediate substance of the same; — or else he remains as an Object over against the subject, and thus in this way *a finite*. c) The *properties* have actually perished in the abstract Concept just cited; but insofar as the finite world as a true being, and God over against it, still remain in representation, there also arises the representation of various relationships of the latter to the former, which, determined as properties, must be, on the one hand, as relationships to finite states, themselves of a finite kind (e.g., just, kind, powerful, wise, etc.), but on the other hand are at the same time to be infinite. This contradiction admits, on this standpoint, only the meager and at the same time nebulous resolution by quantitative heightening, namely by driving them into the determinationless, the *sensum eminentiorem* [the more eminent sense]. Hereby, however, the property is in fact reduced to nothing for representation, and only a name is left to it.

§. 26.

This metaphysics has succumbed to two attacks, which came from opposite sides; I) the one is the philosophizing that grounds itself on *empiricism*, which takes not only all content of representing, but also all content and determination of thinking as it finds the same in sensuous perception, in feeling and in intuition, as an external or inner fact of consciousness, or believes it can derive them from this, and takes this empirical fact

[p. 25]

in general and its analysis for the source of truth, but denies the supersensuous either altogether or at least all cognition of it, and admits to thinking only the form of abstraction, or of identical positing.

§. 27.

II.) The view standing immediately opposed to this one is contained in the *Kantian philosophy*. It has more closely subjected to examination the worth of the *concepts of the understanding* used in metaphysics, and has asserted of them that they do not stem from sensibility, but belong to the spontaneity of *thinking*, and contain *relations* that have universality and necessity, i.e., *objectivity*; synthetic propositions *a priori*.

§. 28.

As the determinate *ground* of the concepts of the understanding this philosophy gives the *original identity* of the I in thinking — (transcendental unity of self-consciousness); the representations given through feeling and intuition are, as to their *content*, a *manifold*, and just as much through their form, through the *being outside one another* of sensibility, in its two forms, space and time. This manifold, in that the I relates the same to itself and unites it in itself as in one consciousness (pure apperception), is hereby brought into identity, into an original connection. The determinate ways of this relating are now the pure concepts of the understanding, the *categories*.

§. 29.

On the one hand, mere perception is raised to *experience* through the objectivity of the categories,

[p. 26]

on the other hand, however, these concepts, as unities merely of subjective consciousness, are conditioned by the given material, empty for themselves, and have their application and use solely in experience.

§. 30.

On account of this finitude they are incapable of being determinations of the absolute, which is not given in a perception, and the understanding, or cognition through the categories, is therefore incapable of cognizing the *things in themselves*.

§. 31.

It is now *reason*, as the faculty of the *unconditioned*, which sees the conditionedness of this cognition of experience, and with it that it gives only *appearances*. But if reason, by its nature, wants to make the *infinite* or the *thing-in-itself* the object of cognition, and to apply the categories to it, since it would have nothing else for this, it becomes *overreaching* (transcendent), commits *paralogisms*, and falls into *antinomies*. It therefore supplies nothing but the *formal unity* for the simplification and systematization of experiences, is a *canon*, not an *organon* of *truth*, and is unable to supply a *doctrine* of the infinite, but only a *critique* of cognition.

§. 32.

This philosophy now rightly recognizes the determinations of the understanding as *finite* and thereby as incapable of grasping the true; but it is one-sided in that it does not consider them in and for themselves, and therefore sees their finitude not in the nature of their content, but in the opposition that they belong to the thinking

[p. 27]

self-consciousness, and retains them in this opposition.

A particular defect in Kant’s elaboration of the above propositions is to be seen in this, that the categories, apart from the fact that, taken even only from the side of enumeration, they are wholly incomplete, have been taken up empirically from common logic, without showing how the so-called transcendental unity of self-consciousness in general *determines itself*, and likewise how it proceeds to the *manifoldness* of the *determinations* that the categories are; — or, the categories are not *deduced* according to their determinacy. — In the so-called paralogisms and antinomies of reason, that table of categories is again likewise presupposed, and the manner, since become so fashionable, of bringing an object, instead of a derivation, merely under an otherwise ready-made schema, has been applied. The other deficiency in the elaboration of the antinomies I have pointed out on occasion in my *Science of Logic* (Nuremberg, by Schrag, 1812 — 1815). — In and for itself, moreover, the thought that the contradiction which is posited in the rational by the determinations of the understanding is *essential and necessary* is to be esteemed as one of the most important and deepest advances of philosophy in recent times; although in the Critique of Pure Reason it is presented as if this contradiction did not lie in these Concepts themselves, but came in only with their application to the unconditioned. Likewise the high merit is to be acknowledged that the Kantian philosophy has brought out the I as pure apperception, and has placed the cognition of it no longer upon *soul-thing*, upon the metaphysical predicates, whether it is material

[p. 28]

or not, but upon its true essence, namely *upon the pure identity of self-consciousness with itself, freedom*; thereby, in that this has been grasped as the essence and substance of the so-called soul, the absolute ground for philosophical cognition has been laid.

§. 33.

Kantian criticism is therefore only a philosophy of subjectivity, a subjective idealism; it departs from empiricism merely with regard to what constitutes *experience*, but agrees with it entirely in this, that reason cognizes nothing supersensible, nothing rational and divine. It remains standing in the finite and untrue, namely in a *cognition* that is only subjective, that has an externality and a *thing-in-itself* as its condition, which is the abstraction of the formless, an empty beyond.

§. 34.

At the same time it goes beyond these two sides of the opposition, which it accepts as an ultimate, precisely in this, that it cognizes the cognitions of that understanding only as *appearances*, and the product of reason only as a one-sided, formal unity, but the *thing-in-itself* as a determinationless void, which, as the *in-itself*, is supposed at the same time to be the true, and thus to contain the Concept.

It is the greatest inconsistency to concede, on the one hand, that the understanding cognizes only appearances, and, on the other hand, to assert this cognition as something absolute, in that one says: cognition *cannot* go further, this is the natural, absolute *limitation* of human knowledge. As *limitation*,

[p. 29]

*deficiency*, anything at all is cognized only through *comparison with the present Idea of the whole and the perfected*; it is therefore a lack of consciousness not to see that precisely the designation of something as a finite or limited contains the *proof of the actuality and presence* of the infinite, the limitless. — One need also merely be reminded of *the religious* and *ethical*, as that in which there is a knowing of the absolute, — a knowing, to be sure not developed, but yet always a knowing, which immediately does not relate itself as a this-side toward the *in-itself*, as toward an unknown and indeterminate beyond, but has given up that opposition, through whose being held fast cognition remains subjective and the absolute a negative. — One is, to be sure, accustomed, as regards the ethical in particular, but also in part the religious, to regard the *theoretical* and the *practical* as two particular faculties or powers, as it were as two kinds of dwellings; which is connected, in general, with the representation of the soul as a thing that is, within itself, just as originally manifold as atomistic matter is thought to be. But this separation also belongs among the presuppositions and assurances that have once become fixed in representation and pass for true without further criticism; although it is easy to see that they contradict the likewise presupposed unity of self-consciousness, and it would not be possible to say what a practical faculty without a theoretical one, without cognition, is supposed to be.

§. 35.

In order now to place oneself upon the standpoint of science, it is necessary to give up the presuppositions,

[p. 30]

which are contained in the cited subjective and finite modes of philosophical cognition, 1) that of the fixed *validity* of limited and opposed *determinations of the understanding* in general, 2) the presupposition of a *given*, represented, *already finished substratum*, which is to be the standard for whether one of those determinations of thought is adequate to it or not, 3) of cognition as a mere *relating* of such finished and fixed predicates to some given substratum, 4) of the opposition of the cognizing subject and its Object, not to be united with it, of which, as with the opposition just named, each side for itself is likewise supposed to be something fixed and true.

§. 36.

To give up these presuppositions can as yet be demanded not so much on the ground that they are false, for this the science, in which the cited determinations must occur, has first to show in them themselves; but on the ground that they belong to *representation* and to the immediate, i.e., to the thinking entangled with *the given*, to *opinion*, and in general because they are *givens* and *presuppositions*, whereas the science presupposes nothing except that it wants to be pure thinking.

I earlier treated the *Phenomenology of Spirit*, the scientific history of *consciousness*, as the first part of philosophy, in the sense that it is to precede pure science, since it is the production of its Concept. But at the same time consciousness, and its history, like any other philosophical science, is not an absolute beginning but a member in the circle of philosophy. *Skepticism*, as a negative science carried through all the forms of finite cognition,

[p. 31]

would likewise present itself as such an introduction. But it would be not only a joyless path, but also for this reason something superfluous, because the dialectical itself is an essential moment of positive science, as was said above; moreover, it would have to find the finite forms only empirically and unscientifically, and to take them up as given. The demand for such a completed skepticism is the same as the demand that *doubting of everything*, or rather *despair of everything*, i.e., the complete *absence of presuppositions* as to everything, should precede science. It is properly, in the resolve *to want to think purely*, accomplished through freedom, which abstracts from everything and grasps its pure abstraction, the simplicity of thinking. — The demand, become current through the Kantian philosophy, that before actual cognition the *faculty of cognition* be *critically examined*, presents itself at first glance as something plausible. But this examination is itself a cognition; that it should be undertaken without cognition is senseless; moreover, the very assumption of a *faculty of cognition* before actual cognition is a presupposition both of the unjustified category or determination of *faculty* or *force*, and also the presupposition of a *subjective cognition*; — a presupposition that belongs to what was mentioned earlier. Incidentally, logic too is that demanded examination, but in a more genuine manner than the critical procedure, which would first of all have had to examine its own presuppositions, which it makes outright, and the nature of its doing.

[p. 32]

§. 37.

The *pure science* or *logic* falls into three parts: into the logic of *being*, of *essence*, and of the *Concept* or of the *Idea*; — of *immediate* thought, of *reflecting* thought, and of thought that has gone back into itself out of reflection and is *with itself in its reality*.

[p. 33]

#### Part One.

### The Doctrine of Being.

#### A.) Quality.

§. 39.

##### a) Being.

*Pure being* makes the beginning, because it is pure thought as much as it is the simple immediate; but the first beginning cannot be anything mediated and further determined.

The truly *first definition of the absolute* is therefore: *it is pure being*.

This definition is the same as the familiar one that *God is the sum total of all realities* — namely insofar as abstraction is to be made from the determinacy which each reality contains — or that God is only the *real* in all reality, the *most real*; since reality contains a reflection within itself, this has been expressed more immediately thus: *God is the being in all determinate being*. — All doubts and objections that could be raised against beginning science with abstract empty being

[p. 34]

are settled through the simple consciousness of what the nature of the beginning brings with it. Being can be determined as I=I, as *absolute indifference* or *identity*, etc.; these forms can, with regard to the necessity either of beginning with something absolutely *certain*, i.e., the certainty of itself, or with the *absolute true*, be taken for those that must be the first. But since in each of them there is already *mediation*, they are not truly the first; mediation is a proceeding forth from distinct things. If I=I, or also intellectual intuition, is truly taken as only the first, then in this pure immediacy it is nothing other than *being*, just as, conversely, pure being, as no longer this abstract being but containing mediation within itself, is pure thinking or intuiting. Moreover, the form of *definition* arises: The *absolute is being*, or is absolute indifference, entirely and solely through the fact that a *substratum* of *representation*, here under the name of the absolute, hovers before one, — a substratum whose *thought*, with which alone it is concerned, is contained solely in the *predicate*; that subject, as well as the form of a proposition, is therefore something wholly superfluous.

§. 40.

This pure being is now the *pure abstraction*, hence the *absolutely negative*, which, likewise taken immediately, is *nothing*.

1) From this there followed the second definition of the absolute, that it is *nothing*; in fact it is contained in it when it is said that the thing-in-itself is the indeterminate, absolutely formless

[p. 35]

and hence contentless; — or also that God is only the *highest being*, for as such he is expressed as precisely the same negativity. One can perhaps take this negativity as the *indeterminacy* of a positive; but the positive is itself a determinacy, which is thus likewise supposed to be sublated; — furthermore, *indeterminacy* itself is again sublated, in that *in itself* the thing-in-itself and God are supposed not to be this void but to have an import and content, thus neither determinacy nor its opposite, indeterminacy, is supposed to belong to them. 2) If the opposition in this immediacy is expressed as *being* and *nothing*, it seems too striking that being should be nothing for one not to try to fix being, and to safeguard it against this transition. Reflective thinking must in this respect hit upon seeking a fixed determination for being by which it would be distinguished from nothing; one takes it, e.g., as what persists in all change, the infinitely determinable *matter*, etc., or even, without reflective thinking, as some *singular* being. But all such further and more concrete determinations no longer leave being as the *pure being*, as it is here immediately at the beginning. On account of its pure indeterminacy it is *nothing*; — something *unsayable*; its difference from nothing is a mere *opinion*. — It is just the consciousness about these beginnings that is to be held fast, that they are nothing but these empty abstractions, and each of the two is just as empty as the other; the *drive* to find a fixed meaning in being or in both is itself precisely this *necessity* that leads them further and gives them a true meaning. This progression is precisely the logical

[p. 36]

elaboration, and the course that presents itself in what follows. *Reflective thinking*, which *finds* deeper determinations for them, is logical thinking, through which such determinations produce themselves, only not in a contingent but in a necessary way. — Every subsequent meaning that they receive is therefore to be regarded only as a *closer determination and truer definition* of the *absolute*; such a one is then no longer an empty abstraction like being and nothing, but rather a concrete, in which both, being and nothing, are moments. Insofar as in such concretes the difference emerges, this is likewise something further determined within itself. — The highest form of nothing for itself is *freedom*, but it is negativity insofar as it deepens itself within itself to the highest intensity, and is itself also affirmation.

§. 41.

*Nothing*, as this immediate, self-equal, is just so, conversely, the *same* as what *being* is. The truth of being, as well as of nothing, is therefore the *unity* of both; this unity is *becoming*.

1) The proposition: *Being and nothing are the same*, appears to *representation* as such a paradoxical proposition that it perhaps does not take it to be seriously meant. In fact it is also among the hardest things that thinking demands of itself, for being and nothing are the opposition in its whole *immediacy*, i.e., without there being *posited* in the one already a determination that would contain its relation to the other. — They *contain*, however, this determination, as was shown in the preceding §, — the determination that is precisely the same in both; and the deduction of their unity is to that extent wholly *analytic*; just as in general

[p. 37]

the whole progression of philosophizing, as methodical, i.e., as *necessary*, is nothing other than only the mere *positing* of that which is already contained in a Concept. — Just as correct as the unity of being and nothing is it, however, *also* that *they* are *absolutely different*, that the one is *not* what the other is. But because the difference has here not yet *determined* itself, for being and nothing are precisely still the immediate, — it is, as it is in them, only as yet something *unsayable*, a mere *opinion*. — 2) It requires no great expenditure of wit to make ridiculous the proposition that being and nothing are the same, or rather to put forward absurdities with the untrue assurance that they are consequences and applications of that proposition; e.g., that it would accordingly be the same whether my house, my fortune, the air for breathing, this city, the sun, right, spirit, God is or not. Here *particular ends*, the *usefulness* that something has for me, are in part foisted in, and it is asked whether it is a matter of indifference to *me* whether the useful matter is or is not. In fact philosophy is precisely this teaching, to free the human being from an infinite multitude of finite ends and intentions, and to make him indifferent to them, so that it is indeed the same to him whether such matters are or are not. — Furthermore, as regards, be it air, sun, or right, God, it is thoughtlessness to consider such essential ends, absolute existences and Ideas merely under the determination of *being*. Such concrete objects are something quite other than merely *beings*, or also *non-beings*; meager abstractions like being and nothing, — and they are, because they are precisely only the determinations

[p. 38]

of the beginning, the most meager there are, — do not suffice to express the nature of those objects. If, therefore, such a concrete is foisted in, the usual thing happens to thoughtlessness, that it gets before representation, and speaks of, something wholly other than that of which the discussion is, and here the discussion is merely of abstract being and nothing. — 3) It can easily be said that one does not *comprehend* the unity of being and nothing. The Concept of it, however, is given in the preceding §§, and it is nothing further than this which has been given; to comprehend it means nothing other than this, to grasp it. But by that comprehending one actually understands more, namely to obtain a more manifold, richer consciousness of it, so that such a Concept might be presented as a concrete case with which thinking in its ordinary practice would be more familiar. As already recalled, the whole of philosophy is nothing other than this more concrete elaboration of the same. — Insofar, however, as the not-being-able-to-comprehend expresses the unaccustomedness to hold fast abstract thoughts without any sensuous admixture, to grasp speculative propositions, there is nothing further to say than that the manner of philosophical knowing is indeed different from the manner of knowing to which one is accustomed in common life, as also from that which prevails in other sciences. — Not-comprehending here often probably means also merely that one cannot *represent* to oneself the unity of being and nothing. But this is in fact not the case; everyone has, rather, infinitely many representations of it, and a lack of this representation can only mean that one does not recognize the Concept at hand in any one of its representations and does not know it as an example of it.

[p. 39]

The example of it that lies nearest is *becoming*. Everyone has a representation of becoming and will likewise concede that it is *one* representation; further, that if one analyzes it, the determination of *being*, but also of the absolute other of the same, *nothing*, is contained in it; further, that these two determinations are inseparably in this *one* representation; so that becoming is thus the unity of being and nothing. — An example that likewise lies near is the *beginning*; the matter *is not* yet in its beginning, but the beginning is not merely its *nothing*, but its *being* is also already in it. The beginning already expresses the regard to further progression; but *becoming* is in fact also only a beginning, one must go further; it becomes, because it is the contradiction within itself, something *become*, *determinate being*.

##### b.) Determinate Being.

§. 42.

Being in becoming, as one with nothing, and nothing in turn one with being, are only vanishing ones; becoming collapses, through its contradiction within itself, into the unity in which both are sublated; its *result* is thus *determinate being*.

What alone can ground a progression and a development in knowing is to hold fast the results in their truth; if in any object or Concept the contradiction is pointed out (— and there is absolutely nothing in which the contradiction, i.e., opposed determinations, does not have to be pointed out; — the abstracting of the understanding is the violent holding fast

[p. 40]

to one determinacy, an effort to obscure and to remove the consciousness of the other that lies in it) — if now such contradiction is cognized, one is accustomed to draw the conclusion: *Therefore* this object is *nothing*; as *Zeno* first showed of motion that it contradicts itself, that it therefore is not, or as the ancients recognized *coming-to-be* and *passing-away*, the two kinds of becoming, as untrue determinations with the expression that the *one*, i.e., the absolute, neither comes to be nor passes away. This dialectic thus stops merely at the negative side of the result, and abstracts from what is at the same time actually present, a *determinate* result, here a pure *nothing*, but a *nothing* that includes *being*, and likewise a being that includes nothing within itself. Thus 1) determinate being is the unity of being and nothing, in which the immediacy of these determinations, and thereby, in their relation, their contradiction has vanished, — a unity in which they are only still *moments*, 2) since the result is the sublated contradiction, it is in the form of simple unity with itself or itself as a being, but a being with negation or determinacy,

§. 43.

*Determinate being* is being with a *determinacy*, which, as immediate or being determinacy, is *quality*. But because nothing constitutes its ground, there is thereby also posited a non-being of determinate being, as likewise something immediate, — an *otherness*; quality is therefore in itself relation to an other, because it is its own moment. In this *being-for-other*, at the same time as *being*, relation to itself, it is *reality*,

[p. 41]

§. 44.

Reality, as pure relation to itself, immediate and indifferent toward otherness, is *something*, which has qualities or realities that are distinguished from it as a breadth of its determinate being, namely as *relations to an other*.

§. 45.

In something, however, determinacy is one with its being; hence otherness too is not something indifferent outside it, but its own moment; it is thereby, through its quality, first *finite*, and second *alterable*, so that alterability belongs to its being.

§. 46.

Something becomes an other, but the other is itself a something, so it likewise becomes an other, and so on into the *infinite*.

§. 47.

This infinity is the bad or negative infinity, in that it is nothing but the sublation of the finite, which, however, equally arises again, and is thus just as much not sublated, — or this infinity expresses only the *ought* of the sublation of the finite; the progress into the infinite remains standing only at the stating of the contradiction that the finite contains, that it is both *something* as well as its *other*, and is the perennial continuing of the alternation of these determinations that bring on one another.

§. 48.

What is in fact present is that something becomes an other, and the other in general becomes an other.

[p. 42]

(Something, in relationship to an other, is itself already an other as against it) thus, since that into which it passes is wholly the same as that which passes, — both have no further determination than one and the same, namely to be an *other*, — something therewith in its passing over merges only *with itself*, and this relation to itself in the passing over and in the other is the *true infinity*. Or, considered negatively: what is altered is the *other*, it becomes the *other of the other*. Thus being is restored, but as negation of the negation, and is *being-for-itself*.

##### c. Being-for-itself.

§. 49.

Being-for-itself, as relation to itself, is *immediacy*, and this, as relation of the negative to itself, is that which is for itself, or the *one*.

§. 50.

The relation of the negative to itself is *negative* relation, the absolute *repulsion* of the one, i.e., the positing of *many ones*. According to the *immediacy* of being-for-itself, these many are *beings*, and the repulsion of the ones is to that extent their repulsion *toward one another* as of things present; or reciprocal *exclusion*.

§. 51.

The *many* are, however, the one that the other is, they are therefore one and the same. Or, the repulsion considered in itself, it is, as the negative *comportment* of the many ones toward one another, just as essentially their *relation* to one another; since those to which the one relates in its repelling are ones, so

[p. 43]

it relates itself in them to itself. The repulsion is therefore just as essentially *attraction*; and the excluding one or being-for-itself sublates itself. The qualitative determinacy, which in the one has attained its being-determined in and for itself, has thereby passed over into determinacy *as sublated*, i.e., into being as *quantity*.

The *atomistic* philosophy is this standpoint, on which the absolute determines itself as being-for-itself, as one, and as many ones. As its fundamental force there has also been assumed the repulsion that shows itself in the Concept of the one; but not so attraction; rather *chance*, i.e., the thoughtless, is supposed to bring them together. Since the one is fixed as one, its coming together with others is indeed to be regarded as something wholly external. — The *void*, which is assumed as the other principle alongside the atoms, is repulsion itself, represented as the nothing that *is*, between the atoms. — Modern atomistics, — and physics still retains this principle, — has in part given up the atoms, and keeps to small parts, molecules; it has thereby brought itself closer to sensuous representing, but has given up thinking consideration. Since, further, an attractive force is set alongside the repulsive force, the opposition is indeed made complete, and one has prided oneself greatly on the discovery of this so-called force of nature; but the relation of the two to one another, which constitutes the concrete and the real of them, has remained up to now only a murky confusion.

[p. 44]

B.

#### Quantity.

##### a.) Pure Quantity.

§. 52.

*Quantity* is pure being, in which determinacy is no longer posited as one with it itself, but as *sublated* or *indifferent*.

1) The expression *magnitude* is not suitable for quantity insofar as it designates chiefly the quantum. 2) Mathematics is accustomed to define magnitude as that which can be *increased* or *diminished*; however faulty this definition is, in that it contains the definitum itself again, there nevertheless lies in it this, that the determination of magnitude is one that is posited as *alterable* and *indifferent*, so that, without prejudice to an alteration in it — an increased extension or intension — the matter, e.g., a house, red, does not cease to be a house, red. 3) The absolute is pure quantity, — this standpoint has been taken for itself insofar as the absolute has the determination of *matter*, in which form is indeed present, but is an indifferent determination; for in quantity determinacy is not wholly absent, but is rather one of the moments from which quantity results. — Quantity also constitutes the basic determination of the absolute in the Concept that in it, the absolutely indifferent, all difference is supposed to be only quantitative. — Otherwise, pure space, light, etc., can be taken as examples of quantity, insofar as in the former the real, as indifferent filling of space, or in the latter the configuration

[p. 45]

as well as clouding, is to be grasped as an external difference.

§. 53.

The moments in quantity are sublated in it; thereby they are, as determinations of it, however, only as of its unity; in the determination of the equality with itself posited through attraction it is *continuous*, in the determination of the one it is *discrete* magnitude; the former, however, is just as much discrete, for it is only continuity *of the many*; the latter is likewise continuous; its continuity is the one as the same of the many ones, the *unity*.

1) Continuous and discrete magnitude must therefore not be regarded as *species* in the sense that the determination of the one does not belong to the other, but they differ only in that *the same whole* is posited now under the one, now under the other of its determinations. 2) The antinomy of space, time, or matter, with regard to their divisibility into the infinite, or else their consisting of indivisibles, is nothing other than the assertion of quantity now as continuous, now as discrete. If space, time, etc., are posited only with the determination of a continuous quantity, they are *divisible* into the infinite; but with the determination of discrete magnitude they are in themselves *divided* and consist of indivisible ones.

##### b.) Quantum.

§. 54.

In the immediate simplicity of quantity the negative of the one is therefore *limit*, and quantity is essentially *quantum*.

[p. 46]

§. 5.

Quantum has its complete determinacy in *number*, because the one constitutes its element; according to the moment of discreteness it is *amount*, according to that of continuity *unity*; this its qualitative difference is sublated in the *one*, which is the whole number, the amount and the unity, the essence of the quantum, identical with its limit.

##### c.) Degree.

§. 56.

This limit, as multiple within itself, is the *extensive*, but, as determinacy *simple* within itself, the *intensive* magnitude or the *degree*.

The difference of continuous and discrete magnitude from the extensive and intensive therefore consists in this, that the former concern *quantity in general*, the latter, however, the *limit* or determinacy of it as such. — Likewise, extensive and intensive magnitude, too, are not two species, of which each would contain a determinacy that the other would not have.

§. 57.

In degree the *Concept* of the quantum is *posited*; it is magnitude as *for itself* and simple, but so that it has its determinacy absolutely *outside it* in other magnitudes. Through this indifference of the quantum as of the determinacy whose *being-for-itself* is absolute *externality*, the *infinite* quantitative *progress* is posited.

Number is indeed thought, as the *identity* of the determinacy that is for itself *with itself*, but thought as a being perfectly external to itself.

[p. 47]

It does not belong to intuition, because it is thought, but is the thought that has within itself the externality of intuition. — The quantum, therefore, not only *can* be increased or diminished to infinity; rather, by its Concept it is this *sending-out* beyond itself. The infinite quantitative progress is likewise again nothing but the thoughtless repetition of one and the same contradiction, which is the quantum in general and, posited in its determinacy, the degree. Concerning the superfluity of expressing this contradiction in the form of the infinite progress, *Zeno* says rightly in Aristotle: it is the same to say something *once* and to say it *always*.

§. 58.

This *being-external* to itself of the quantum, in its determinacy *that is for itself*, makes up its *quality*; in it the quantum is precisely itself and related to itself. Or it is precisely the externality, i.e., the quantitative, and being-for-itself, the qualitative, united therein. Thus posited, it is at first not something immediate, but a quantitative *relationship*.

§. 59.

The *sides* of the quantitative relationship, however, are themselves still only immediate quanta, and their relation therefore itself an indifferent one, or a quantum (the exponent), or the qualitative and the quantitative determination are still external to one another. According to their truth, however, that the quantitative is itself relation to itself in its externality, or that being-for-itself and the indifference of the determinacy are united, it is *measure*.

[p. 48]

C.

#### Measure.

§. 60.

Measure is the qualitative quantum, at first as *immediate*, a quantum to which a determinate being or a quality is bound.

*Modality* or *manner of being* could appear as the third to quality and quantity insofar as the qualitative, through its unity with the quantitative, has become an external, indifferent being. But the mode expresses only *in general* this indifference or contingency. This external manner, however, is at the same time also an expression of qualitative being, as one says of conduct in a matter that everything depends on the *manner*. But the mode, according to this qualitative side, is likewise only the indeterminate manner; in its true determinacy it is measure.

§. 61.

Since in measure quality and quantity are only in *immediate* unity, their difference emerges in them in such an immediate manner, and the specific quantum is in this respect, on the one hand, a mere quantum and capable of an increase and decrease without the measure, which in this respect is a *rule*, being thereby sublated; on the other hand, however, the alteration of the quantum is also an alteration of the quality.

§. 62.

The *measureless* is at first this going-out of a measure, through its quantitative nature, beyond its

[p. 49]

qualitative determinacy; but since the other quantitative relationship, the measureless of the first, is just as much qualitative, the measureless is likewise a measure; which two transitions, from quality into quantum and from this into that, can again be represented as an *infinite progress*.

§. 63.

What is in fact posited therein is that *passing over* in general sublates itself. Since the qualitative and the quantitative are themselves qualitatively different, while quality, in the indifferent determinacy of the quantum, sublates itself just as much as it is posited therein, and in this its externalization merges only with itself, there is hereby posited the negativity, in its otherness, of sublating itself as well as this otherness. The being that in this way relates itself to itself is *essence*.

[p. 50]

#### Part Two.

### The Doctrine of Essence.

§. 64.

Essence, as the being that mediates itself with itself through the negativity of itself, contains the negative as immediately sublated determinacy, as *semblance*, and is *reflection* — relation to itself only insofar as it is relation to another, which is immediately only as something *posited and mediated*.

The absolute is *essence*. — This determination is the same, to that extent, as the one that it is *being*, insofar as being likewise is the simple relation to itself. But it is at the same time higher, because essence is the being that has gone *into itself*, i.e., its simple relation to itself is this relation through the negation of the negative, or as pure negativity. When the absolute is determined as *essence*, however, negativity is frequently taken only in the sense *of an abstraction* from all determinate predicates. This negative doing then falls outside essence, and essence itself is thus only as a result *without this its premise*, the *caput mortuum* [dead head] of abstraction.

[p. 51]

But since this negativity is not external to being but is its own dialectic, its truth is essence as the being that has gone *into itself*, or that is *within itself*, and just that *reflection* alone makes up its difference from immediate being, and is the proper determination of essence itself.

§. 65.

In the sphere of essence, *relativity* constitutes the dominant determination. In the sphere of being, identity is *immediate* relation to itself, and the negative the mere *otherness*; in the present sphere, by contrast, everything is posited as being only in such a way that at the same time it has been gone beyond; it is a *being of reflection, relationship*.

A.

#### The Pure Determinations of Reflection.

##### a.) Identity.

§. 66.

Essence *shines within itself*, or is pure reflection; thus it is *identity with itself*, — the relation to itself, though not as *immediate* but as reflected.

1) *Formal* identity or *identity of the understanding* is this identity insofar as it is held fast to, and the difference is *abstracted* from. Or *abstraction* is rather the positing of this formal identity, the transformation of something concrete in itself into the form of simplicity, — be it that a part of the manifold present in the concrete is *left out* and only one of them is taken,

[p. 52]

or that the differentiated manifolds are *drawn together* into one determinacy, so that here nothing is changed as regards the content. Considered according to truth, both are the same; for every being, or every universal determination, is, as Concept, concrete in itself; it is therefore the same contingency or arbitrariness to leave as it is that which appears to representation or to thinking as a simple identical, or to draw together what appears as concrete into the form of simple determinacy. 2) Identity, combined with the absolute as the subject of a proposition — thus it reads: *the absolute is identical with itself*. — As true as this proposition is, it is still ambiguous whether it is meant in its truth; it is therefore, in its expression at least, incomplete: for it is undecided whether abstract *identity* of the understanding, i.e., in opposition to the other determinations of essence, — or else identity as concrete in itself, is meant; in that case it is, as will emerge, the *ground*, or in higher truth the *Concept*. Even the word *absolute* itself often has no other meaning than that of *abstract*; thus *absolute* space, *absolute* time, means nothing more than abstract space and abstract time. 3) The determinations of essence can also be taken as *essential* determinations; then they become predicates of a presupposed subject; and since they are determinations of *essence itself*, they are the essential ones *in themselves*, the *universally-essential* ones. Hence there is also given to them the subject: *everything*, and the propositions that arise thereby have been pronounced as *the universal laws of thought*. The *proposition of identity* accordingly reads: *Everything is identical with itself*; A = A;

[p. 53]

and negatively: *A cannot at the same time be A and not be A*. — This proposition, instead of being a true law of thought, is nothing but the law of the *abstract understanding*. The *form of the proposition* already contradicts it, since a proposition also promises a difference between subject and predicate, but this one does not deliver what its form demands. In particular, however, it is sublated by the following so-called laws of thought, which are placed *next to* that first one with equal validity.

##### b.) Difference.

§. 67.

Essence is only pure identity and semblance within itself, insofar as it is the negativity of being, or the negativity that relates itself to itself, hence a repelling of itself from itself; it therefore contains essentially the determination of *difference*.

Otherness is here no longer the *qualitative* one, determinacy, negation, limit, but the same as in essence, which relates itself to itself; negation is therefore as relation, *difference, being-posited, being-mediated*.

§. 68.

Difference is α) *immediate* difference, or, since immediacy and being are sublated in essence and are only posited, a merely *posited* difference; *the diversity*, in which the differentiated, each *for itself*, is what it is, and indifferent to its relation to the other, which is thus one external to it.

It can also be said that diversity is *being-posited as being-posited*, i.e., as yet only as semblance; as the difference, as in essence, is only

[p. 54]

a semblance. — Since now *being-posited as being-posited* is the negative as negative, the diverse is *that which is for itself*, and so rather *the opposite of semblance*.
