That which is for itself, in that it disdains relativity and does not want to be merely in difference, which, however, makes up its essence, is for that very reason not posited as it is in truth, and it is only as yet the semblance of difference.

§. 69.

On account of the indifference of the diverse toward their difference, it falls outside them into a third, a *comparing* one. This external difference is, as identity of the related, *likeness*, as non-identity, *unlikeness*.

1) Likeness and unlikeness are the ways in which identity and difference exist for the understanding; *both* lie in the Concept of difference; for it is *relation*, which constitutes the sides of likeness, just as difference itself as such constitutes the side of unlikeness. But because likeness and unlikeness are nothing further than difference external to itself, what is posited in it is indifferent to whether it is like or unlike, and these determinations themselves fall outside one another, and the like is only like, the unlike only unlike. — Even though the comparison has one and the same substrate for likeness and unlikeness, the *sides and respects* according to which it is like are different from those according to which it is unlike. — 2) Diversity has likewise been transformed into a proposition, namely into this: that *everything is diverse*, or that *there are not two things that are completely like one another*. Here there is given to everything the *opposite*

[p. 55]

predicate to the *identity* ascribed to it in the *first* proposition. But insofar as by diversity only that belonging to external comparison is understood, then something *is supposed* to be, *for itself*, only *identical* with itself, and this second proposition thus not to be contradictory to the first. Then, however, diversity too does *not belong* to the something or to everything itself; it makes up no essential determination of this subject; this second proposition therefore cannot properly be stated at all. Insofar as diversity is the entirely indeterminate difference, mere *multiplicity*, the proposition that everything, i.e., *the* somethings in their complete *multiplicity*, are *many*, is in any case tautological. But if the something itself is diverse, then it is so through *its own* determinacy; hereby what is meant is no longer diversity as such but *determinate* difference.

§. 70.

Likeness is an identity only of such as are *not the same*, not identical with one another, and unlikeness is *relation* of the unlike. Both therefore do not fall indifferently apart into different sides or respects, but one is a shining into the other. Diversity is therefore difference of reflection, or *difference in itself*.

§. 71.

β) Difference *in itself* is the *essential* one, *the positive and the negative*, such that the former is the identical relation to itself in such a way that it is *not* the negative, and the latter the differentiated for itself in such a way that it is *not* the positive. Since each is thus for itself insofar as it is *not the other*, each *shines* in the other, and is only insofar as the other is. The difference

[p. 56]

of essence is therefore *opposition*, according to which the differentiated has over against itself *not an other in general*, but *its* other; i.e., each has its own determination only in its relation to the other, is reflected into itself only insofar as it is reflected into the other.

Difference in itself yields the following proposition: *Everything is something essentially differentiated* — or, as it has also been expressed, of two *opposed* predicates *only the one* belongs to the something, and there is no *third*. — This proposition of opposition now contradicts the proposition of identity, in that something, according to the one, is supposed to be only *relation to itself*, but according to the other *relation to another*. — It is the peculiar thoughtlessness of abstraction to place two such contradictory propositions next to one another without even comparing them. — *The proposition of the excluded third* is the proposition of the determinate understanding, which wants to keep contradiction away from itself, and in doing so commits it; for the predicate, precisely in being an opposed one, is the *third*, in which it itself, but also its opposite, is contained; A is supposed to be either +A or −A; thereby the third, the A, is already stated, which is *neither* + *nor* −, and which is posited *just as well also* as +A and as −A.

§. 72.

The *positive* now is that *diverse* which is supposed to be for itself and at the same time not indifferent toward its relation to its other; the *negative* is supposed to be just as independent, hence the negative relation to itself, to be for itself, but, as negative, absolutely to have this its relation to itself, its positive, only in the other

[p. 57]

Both are thus the posited contradiction, both are *in themselves* the same, but both are so also *for themselves*, since each is the sublating of the other and of itself. They thereby go to the ground. — Or immediately the essential difference, as difference in and for itself, is only the difference of itself, and therefore contains the identical. — *As* difference *relating itself to itself*, it is likewise already stated *as the identical with itself*, and the *opposed* is in general that which contains the *one and its other*, itself and its very opposite.

##### c.) Ground.

§. 73.

c.) *Ground* is the unity of identity and of difference; the truth of that which difference and identity have turned out to be, — the reflection-into-itself that is just as much reflection-into-another and conversely. It is *essence* posited *as totality*.

*The proposition of ground* reads: Everything has its sufficient *reason*, i.e., not the determination of something as identical with itself, nor as diverse, nor as merely positive or as merely negative, is its true essentiality, but that it has its being in an other, which, as the identical with itself, is essence. This, however, is just as much not abstract reflection *into itself*, but *into another*. Ground is the essence that is *within itself*, but it is essentially ground, and it is ground only insofar as it is ground of something, of an other.

[p. 58]

§. 74.

Essence is at first shining and mediation within itself, the determination of reflection is the determinacy of mediating and therefore essentially the mediated. Since this mediation sublates itself in itself, this is the restoration of *immediacy* or of *being*, but of being insofar as it is *mediated through the sublating of mediation*; — *existence*.

Ground has as yet no *content*, nor *end*, therefore it is not yet *active*, nor *producing*; rather, an existence only *proceeds* from the ground. The *determinate* ground is therefore something formal, because the *content* of the existence and of its ground is not one with its form, and the ground is not that which is determined in and for itself. A ground can therefore be found and given for everything, and a *good ground* (e.g., a good motive for acting) can bring something about *or also not*, have a consequence or *also not*. A motive that brings something about, it becomes so, e.g., through the taking up into a will, which first makes it into something active and into a cause. — Ground as such therefore also does not remain behind, for itself, in the interior, over against the existence that proceeds from it, but passes wholly into this; — it is the reflection-into-itself that is immediately reflection-into-another; and existence is this *immediate* unity of both, in which the mediation of the ground has sublated itself.

[p. 59]

B.

#### Appearance.

##### a.) Existence.

§. 75.

That which *exists* is the immediate unity of reflection-into-itself and of reflection-into-another, therefore it is not only as unity or as reflection-into-itself, but differentiated into these two determinations. As the former it is the *thing* and, fixed in its abstraction, the *thing-in-itself*.

The *thing-in-itself*, which has become so famous in the Kantian philosophy, shows itself here in its origin, namely as the abstract reflection-into-itself, which is held fast to, over against the differentiated determinations, as their empty *foundation*. — Ground is thus posited as *thing-in-itself*, as it is for itself in its truth, as the *indeterminate and inactive*, since it is only the sublated mediation without content and end.

§. 76.

The *thing*, as *reflection-into-another*, has the differences, in it, according to which it is a *determinate* thing. These determinations are *different from one another*; in the thing, not in themselves, they have reflection-into-itself. They are *properties* of the thing, and their relation to it is *having*.

*Having* enters as relation into the place of *being*. *Something*, to be sure, also has *qualities*, but this transference of having onto that which is, is inexact, because determinacy as quality is immediately

[p. 60]

one with the something, and something *ceases to be* when it loses its quality. The *thing*, however, is reflection-into-itself, as the identity differentiated from difference. — *Having* is used in many languages to designate the *past*, — rightly, since the past is the *sublated being*, and spirit is its reflection-into-itself, in which alone it still has subsistence, spirit that, however, also distinguishes from itself this being sublated in it.

§. 77.

Reflection-into-another is, in its truth, namely in the *ground*, also reflection-into-itself; hence the properties of the thing are just as much *independent* and freed from their being bound to the thing. But because they are only the determinacies of the thing, *differentiated from one another*, as reflected-into-itself, they are not so much things, such as are in the relation of *having* with reflection-into-another, but things as abstract determinacies, *matters*.

The matters, e.g., magnetic, electric matters, are also not called *things*. — They are the proper qualities, one with their being, determinacy that has attained immediacy, but an immediacy that is existence.

§. 78.

*Matter* is the abstract or indeterminate reflection-into-another, or reflection-into-itself at the same time as determinate; it is therefore the *thinghood that has determinate being*, the *foundation* of the thing; over against it, however, is the determinate difference, which is in that respect *form*.

[p. 61]

§. 79.

*Form* and *matter*, *thing-in-itself* and the *matters* of which the thing consists, are one and the same opposition of *unessential* and *essential* existence, with the difference that form for itself is the abstraction of reflection-into-another, whereas the thing-in-itself is the abstraction of reflection-into-itself. Matter, however, is, over against form, the *essential existence*, in that it is reflection-into-itself, but at the same time has determinacy within itself; the many matters of which the thing consists are likewise its essential existence, in that they are reflection-into-another, but at the same time *reflection-into-itself*.

§. 80.

The thing, which has its essential existence both as one matter and as many independent matters, which, however, insofar as the former is the essential existence, sink into the form, but in that they are just as much the essential existence, reduce the one matter to abstract empty thinghood, is in this way *appearance*.

##### b.) Appearance.

§. 81.

Essence must *appear*. Its semblance in it itself is the sublating of itself into the immediacy of *existence*, which, however, is not the immediacy of being but has reflection as its ground. It is therefore such a thing as is immediately sublated and has its ground in a non-being identity-with-itself, which inwardness, however, is immediately just as much reflection-into-another in itself, hence existence, but another existence than the first. In that something

[p. 62]

that exists rather exists in another than in itself, and is something mediated, *essence* is in appearance. Essence is therefore not *behind* or *beyond* appearance, but by the fact that *essence* is what exists, existence is appearance. —

§. 82.

That which exists is therefore, according to its truth, something subsisting for itself, which exists immediately as an other; it exists immediately as mediation. It is therefore *one and the same* that is the differentiation and relation of these doubled ways of existence. These ways have, further, as the difference of reflection, the determinacy over against one another that the one is *reflection-into-itself*, the other reflection-into-another.

§. 83.

That which exists, or appearance in its determinacy, is therefore the *relationship*, that one and the same is the *opposition* of independent existences, and their *identical* relation, in which the differentiated are alone what they are.

##### c.) Relationship.

§. 84.

1) The immediate relationship is that of the *whole* and the *parts*; the whole *consists* of the parts, the opposite of itself. The parts are the independent diverse; but they are parts only in their identical relation to one another, or insofar as taken together they make up the whole; but the *together* is the opposite of the part.

[p. 63]

§. 85.

2) The one and the same of this relationship is thus immediately the negative relation to itself, and specifically such that it is posited as the mediation, namely as one and the same to be *indifferent* toward the difference, and to be the *negative* relation *to itself*, which, as reflection-into-itself, repels itself from itself and posits itself as existing reflection-into-another, and conversely; — *force* and its *expression*.

The *relationship of the whole and the parts* is the immediate, hence the thoughtless relationship, and a turning over of identity-with-itself into diversity. One passes from the whole to the parts and from the parts to the whole, and in the one the opposition to the other is forgotten, since each side is taken for itself as an independent existence. Or, since the parts are supposed to subsist in the whole, and this to consist of those, at one time the one, at another time the other, is the *subsisting*, and likewise each time the other of it is an *unessential* one. The *mechanical* relationship consists, in its superficial form in general, in this, that the parts, as independent without their unity, are over against one another and over against the whole. The *progress to the infinite*, which concerns the *divisibility* of *matter*, can also make use of this relationship, and then it is the thoughtless alternation with the two sides of it. A thing is at one time taken as a *whole*, then there is a passage to the *determination of parts*; this determination is now forgotten, and what was part is regarded as whole; then the determination of the part enters again, etc., to infinity. — This infinity, however, as the truly negative

[p. 64]

taken, is the negative relation of the relationship to itself, *force*, the whole identical with itself, as being-within-itself, and as sublating itself and expressing itself, and conversely the expression, which vanishes and goes back into force. — Force, notwithstanding this infinity that it is, is also finite; it requires a solicitation from outside for its expression, is blind in its working, and has only a determinate, finite *content*. It has a content, as does the relationship of *the whole and the parts*, because *one and the same* posits itself in the differentiated determinacy of form, and specifically as unity of these form-determinations and at the same time is indifferent toward this differentiating. But this one and the same is only as yet *in itself* this identity, because the two sides of the relationship are not yet themselves, each for itself, the concrete identity of it, not yet the totality. They are therefore diverse for one another, and the whole relationship a *finite* one. Force therefore requires solicitation from outside, and the determinacy of the content is a contingent one. The content has not yet the infinity of the Concept and of end, which is what is determined in and for itself. — One is therefore also accustomed to say that the *nature* of *force* itself is *unknown*, and only its expression is cognized. On the one hand, the whole *determination of content* of *force* is precisely the same as that of the *expression*; the explanation of an appearance from a force is therefore an empty tautology. What is supposed to be unknown is therefore nothing but the empty form of reflection-into-itself, whereby alone force is differentiated from expression, — a form that, however, is something quite well known. On the other hand, however, the nature of force is indeed an unknown, since

[p. 65]

its relationship is indeed infinite according to its own, though only as yet abstract, negativity, but otherwise its determinacy is finite; the latter therefore demands a necessity of connection and of origin, which is entirely lacking. There is thus present the contradiction of the semblance of independence, which force has, and of its finitude, which must have conditions; these, however, lie outside it, and are therefore not cognized in it.

§. 86.

Force, as the whole, which is in itself the negative relation to itself, is hereby this: to repel itself from itself and to *express* itself; but since this reflection-into-another, the difference of the parts, is just as much reflection-into-itself, the expression is the mediation through which it returns into itself. Its expression therefore consists in this, thereby to *posit* the identity of reflection-into-itself and of reflection-into-another, which as yet only *is in itself*; its truth is therefore the relationship whose two sides are differentiated only as *inner* and *outer*.

§. 87.

3) The *inner* is the ground, as it is in its truth, namely as a *side* of appearance and of the relationship, the empty form of reflection-into-itself, to which existence, likewise as a side of the relationship, stands opposed with the empty determination of reflection-into-another, as *outer*; their identity is a filled one, the *content*, as the unity, posited in the movement of force, of reflection-into-itself and of reflection-into-another.

§. 88.

The outer therefore has, *first of all, the same content* as the inner; what is inward is also outwardly present

[p. 66]

and conversely; appearance shows nothing that is not in essence, and in essence there is nothing that does not manifest itself.

§. 89.

*Secondly*, however, inner and outer are also absolutely *opposed* to one another as the abstractions of identity with itself and of mere reality. But since they are essentially identical, that which is as yet posited only in the one abstraction is *immediately* also *only* in the other. What is therefore only something *inward* is thereby also only something *outward*; and what is *only* something outward is also *only* something inward.

It is the common error of reflection to take *essence* as the merely *inner*. If it is taken merely so, then this consideration too is a wholly *outward* one, and that essence the empty outward abstraction. Into the *inner* of nature, says a poet:

No created spirit penetrates, / Too happy, if it knows only the *outer* shell.

He should rather have said: precisely then, when the essence of nature is determined for him as an *inner*, he knows only the *outer* shell. — Because in *being* in general the *Concept* is as yet only the inner, it is something outer to it, — a merely subjective thinking without truth. — In nature itself, as in spirit, insofar as the Concept, end, law are as yet only *inner* predispositions, pure possibilities, they are as yet only an external inorganic nature, science of a third party, alien power, etc.

§. 90.

The empty abstractions, through which the one identical content is still supposed to be in the relationship, sublate themselves in

[p. 67]

the immediate passing over, the one in the other; they are the semblance, posited as semblance, of essence, or the essentiality that has become wholly inessential. Through the expression of force the inner is *posited* into existence; this *positing* is the *mediating* through empty abstractions; it vanishes within itself into *immediacy*, in which *the inner and the outer* are identical *in and for themselves*. This identity is *actuality*.

C.

#### Actuality.

§. 91.

Actuality is the unity that has become immediate, of essence and existence, or of the inner and the outer. The expression of the actual is the actual itself, so that in it it remains just as much something essential, and is something essential only insofar as it is in immediate external existence.

Earlier, *being and existence* have occurred as forms of the immediate; being is, in general, unreflected immediacy, and *passing over* into an other. *Existence* is the immediate unity of being and reflection; it is therefore *appearance*, comes from the ground, and goes to the ground. The actual is the *positedness* of that unity, the relationship that has become identical with itself; it is therefore removed from *passing over*, and its *externality* is its energy; in it it is reflected into itself; its determinate being is only the *manifestation of itself*, not of an other.

§. 92.

Because actuality is reflection in general, it is 1) also the difference of its immediacy from its

[p. 68]

mediation with itself, *possibility*; — the reflection-into-itself, which, as over against the concrete unity of the actual, is posited as the *abstract and inessential essentiality*.

It is probably the determination of *possibility* that induced *Kant* to regard it, and with it actuality and necessity, as *modalities*, “since these determinations do not in the least augment the Concept as object, but only express the relationship to the faculty of cognition.” In fact possibility is at first the empty abstraction of the reflection-into-itself, so that it belongs only to subjective thinking. It is the same as what the inner was before, only that, since in the actual the inner is sublated, it is now determined as the sublated or merely posited, external inner, and so is indeed *posited* as a mere modality, as an empty abstraction. — Actuality and necessity, however, are truly nothing less than a mere *mode and manner* for an other, rather precisely the opposite. — Since possibility is again, as against the concrete actual, the empty form of *identity-with-itself*, *everything* is *possible*; for this form can be given to any content by abstraction. But *everything* is just as much *impossible*, for in any content, since it is a concrete, the determinacy can be grasped as determinate opposition and thereby as contradiction. — There is therefore no emptier talk than that of such possibility and impossibility. In philosophy in particular there must be no talk of showing *that something is possible, or that something else too is possible*, any more than of the so much favored *probability* elsewhere.

[p. 69]

§. 93.

2) The actual, however, in its difference from possibility as reflection-into-itself, is itself only the external immediate. Or rather, the actual, on account of its immediacy, is itself immediate only in the abstraction of reflection-into-itself; it is thus, as actual, determined as a *merely* possible; in this value of a mere possibility, or inessential actuality, it is something *contingent*.

§. 94.

This semblance posited as semblance, mere possibility and contingency, has, however, in the actual its real reflection-into-itself; therefore here the *content* is *posited*, on which they have their essential determining ground. The finitude of the contingent and the possible therefore consists, more precisely, in the difference of the determination of form, of identity with itself, from the content, and *whether something is contingent and possible depends on the content*.

§. 95.

The *reflection-into-itself* is, however, in the actual no longer the abstract determination, as in the simple essence, but the self-sublating positing or mediating. Contingency therefore, as immediate actuality, is what is identical with itself essentially as positedness, whose positing is likewise sublated; it is a *presupposed*, whose immediacy is likewise a *possibility* and at the same time has the determination of being sublated, — the possibility of an other, the *condition*.

§. 96.

3) Possibility as full of content, so that the condition therefore belongs to it, is at first the *real possibility*. But as distinguished from the content and the immediate

[p. 70]

actuality, it is form for itself, and in the sphere of the actual not the abstract identity, but concrete totality of form for itself, the immediate self-transposition of the inner into the outer, and of the outer into the inner; the *ground* as reflected into itself, *activity*, and indeed of the *real* ground, which sublates itself into actuality, and of the contingent actuality, the condition, its reflection-into-itself and its sublation into another actuality. This identity of possibility and actuality is *necessity*.

§. 97.

Necessity is therefore the *one essence*, identical *with itself* but full of content, that shines within itself in such a way that its differences have the form of *independent actuals*, and this is identical at the same time as absolute form, the *activity* of sublating immediacy into mediation, and mediation into immediacy. — The necessary is through an *other*, which has fallen apart into the *mediating ground* and into an *immediate* actuality, something contingent, which at the same time is again condition. The necessary, as through an other, is not in and for itself, but something merely *posited*. But this mediation is just as immediately the sublating of itself; the ground posits itself as ground, and as contingent condition transposes itself over into immediacy, whereby that positedness is rather sublated into actuality, and it has come together with itself. This *identity* makes the actual into the *necessary*. It is therefore, in its truth, the *relationship of necessity*.

§. 98.

The necessary is a) at first the relationship of *substantiality and accidentality*. The

[p. 71]

absolute identity of this relationship with itself is *substance* as such, which, however, as necessity is the negativity of this inwardness, thus posits itself as *actuality*, but is just as much the *negativity* of this external, according to which the actual, as immediate, is only an *accidental*, which through this its mere possibility passes over into another actuality; a *passing over* which is the substantial identity as the *form-activity* (§ 97).

§. 99.

Substance is hereby the totality of the accidents, in which it reveals itself as *absolute power* and as the *wealth of all content*. This content, however, is *nothing but this manifestation itself*, in that the determinacy reflected into itself is not indifferent to the form, but passes over in the *power* of substance, — or substantiality is rather itself the absolute form-activity and the power of necessity.

§. 100.

b) According to the moment that substance, as absolute power, is the power *relating* itself *to itself* as to inner possibility, it is *acting*, and is *cause*; substantiality is therefore essentially *causality*.

§. 101.

This, however, is likewise a *relationship of causality*, in that substance, which over against its passing over into accidentality reflects itself into itself, and so is the *original matter*, just as much sublates this reflection-into-itself or its mere possibility, posits itself as the negative of itself, and so brings forth an *effect*, an actuality that is at the same time only a *posited* one, but thereby a necessary one.

[p. 72]

Cause and effect are opposed to each other, as substance or original matter, and as mere *positedness*. — The cause has, in necessity, whose identity constitutes its very originality, passed over into the effect. There is no content in the effect that is not in the cause; that identity is content; just as much, however, it is also the determination of form: the originality of the cause is sublated in the effect, in which it *makes* itself into a *positedness*. But this *positedness* is just as immediately sublated; it is rather the reflection of the cause into itself, its originality; it is first in the effect that the cause is actual. — The Concept of *necessity* is one of the most difficult in philosophy, because it is precisely *the Concept* itself, but still in its externality. — Substantiality is the necessity still *taken immediately*; it is, however, at least essentially a *relationship*; *substance* for itself and the *accident* are empty abstractions. In its *reality*, the infinite relationship of substantiality is, however, relationship of causality: for substance it is not an external abstraction to relate itself to itself, but it is this itself, and is thus *cause*, but it is thus itself infinite, and its *acting*, but infinite acting, which returns into itself in the effect and first therein is original and actual. — Only in the immediately self-sublating moment of the opposition is the cause *finite*. — When this opposition is adhered to and the *Concept* of cause is thereby given up, this gives the *finite* cause and the common representation of the relationship of causality. Its finitude also turns over into the opposition of *form* and *content*, and is so taken that the cause is finite because

[p. 73]

it is, according to its content, a *determinate* actuality. On account of this finitude the cause seems to be something *posited* or an *effect*; this then has again an *other* cause; thus here too arises the progress from effects to causes into the infinite. Likewise the *descending* one, in that the effect, according to its identity with the cause, is reflection-into-itself and itself something actual and a cause, and indeed likewise an *other*, which again has *other* effects and so forth into the infinite.

§. 102.

Since the cause in its acting is not only positing, but this mediation is just as much sublated into reflection-into-itself and immediacy, it is at the same time a *presupposing*; there is hereby another substance at hand, upon which its effect occurs.

§. 103.

The presupposed substance is, as *immediate*, hereby determined not as negativity relating itself to itself and as *active*, but as *passive*. But as substance it is just as much active, sublates the presupposed immediacy or, what is the same, the effect posited in it, and *reacts*, i.e. it sublates the activity of the first substance, which, however, is just as much this sublating of its immediacy or of the effect posited in it, hereby sublates the activity of the other, and reacts. Causality has hereby c) passed over into the relationship of *reciprocal action*.

In reciprocal action the progress from causes and effects into the infinite is sublated in a true manner; in that the rectilinear going-forth from causes to effects and from effects to causes is *bent around* and *back* into itself. The cause, which has an effect, is itself

[p. 74]

an effect, but not of a cause lying at its back and beyond its own effect, but of that which arises in the first one’s own effect; likewise the effect is itself again cause, but over against that whose effect it is. — Or the cause is not an effect in *another respect* than that in which it has an effect, but the effect produced is the *positedness* of the cause, — which, however, is immediately just as much reflection-into-itself, actuality, and presupposed substance, — the other cause —; — but the cause is supposed to be, as *immediate*, actual and even original; yet precisely this immediacy is only a *positedness* or effect.

§. 104.

Reciprocal action is the truth of causality, i.e. causality is only as reciprocal action; since the cause is only as acting, but passivity is the non-acting immediacy, the cause assumed as *first* is, on account of its immediacy, determined as *passive*, as *positedness* and *effect*. But in this the difference of the causes still named as *two* has vanished, and there is *in itself* present only one cause, which in its effect sublates itself as substance just as much as it first becomes independent in it:

§. 105.

But this unity is also *for itself*, in that this whole interchange is the cause’s own *positing* and only this its positing is its *being*. The *effect or passivity* which it receives in reciprocal action is rather its *originality* and the immediacy produced through the mediation of the sublating of its mediation. Its *activity* is this positing *of itself* as effect or as something

[p. 75]

*posited*; and this reducing of itself to an effect is, conversely, its originality and *independent being*.

§. 106.

This pure interchange with itself is hereby the *unveiled or posited necessity*, whose *bond* is identity as still *inner*, because it is the identity of *actuals* whose independence, however, is supposed to be precisely necessity. The course of substance through causality and reciprocal action is therefore only the *positing* that *independence* is the *negative relation to itself*, — *negative*, in which distinguishing and mediating becomes an originality of *actuals independent* of one another, — *relation to itself*, in that their independence is only as their identity.

§. 107.

This *truth of necessity* is thus *freedom*, and the *truth of substance* is the *Concept*, — the independence which is the repelling of itself from itself into distinct independents, identical with itself as this repelling, and this reciprocal motion with itself that remains with itself.

§. 108.

The *Concept* is hereby the *truth of being and of essence*, in that the shining of reflection within itself is at the same time independent immediacy, and this *being* of diverse actuality is immediately only a shining within itself.

1) Since the Concept has proved itself to be the truth of being and essence, both of which have *gone back* into it as into their *ground*, it has *conversely developed* itself out of *being* as out of its *ground*. That side of the

[p. 76]

progression can be regarded as a *deepening* into being, whose inner has been unveiled in it, and this side as emergence of *the more perfect from the less perfect*. The more determinate import that the more superficial thoughts of the less perfect and the more perfect have here is the difference that *being*, as *immediate* unity with itself, has from the *Concept*, as *free mediation* with itself. Since *being* has shown itself to be a mere *moment* of the Concept, the Concept has proved itself precisely thereby as the truth of being; as this its reflection-into-itself and sublation of mediation it is the *presupposing of the immediate*, a presupposing which is identical with the return-into-itself, which identity constitutes freedom and the Concept. If, therefore, the *moment* is called the imperfect, then the Concept is the perfect, indeed this: to develop itself out of the imperfect, for it is essentially this sublating of its presupposition. But it is the Concept alone that, as positing itself, makes for itself the presupposition. The dead Concept is, to be sure, without freedom and motion within itself, and therefore also without moments that one may call the imperfect. — If one regards the moment as something *independent*, and the presupposition as an *original* and persisting *prius*, then such a determination, as well as the Concept that would be bound to such a thing, is not Concept but, like the opposition of perfect and imperfect, as if the latter were something in and for itself, an empty abstraction. 2) As for the relation of being and essence to the Concept, the Concept is the essence that has gone back to being as simple immediacy, whose shining

[p. 77]

thereby has actuality, and whose actuality is at the same time free shining within itself. *Being* the Concept has as its simple relation to itself or the immediacy of its unity within itself; it is so poor a determination that it is the least that can be shown in the Concept. — 3) The transition from necessity to freedom, or from the actual into the Concept, is the hardest, because independent actuality is to be thought as having its substantiality solely in the passing over and in the identity with the independent actuality that is other to it; thus the Concept too is the hardest, because it itself is precisely this identity. The actual substance as such, however; the cause, which in its being-for-itself will let nothing penetrate into itself, is already subjected to *necessity* or fate, and this subjection is rather the hardest; the *thinking* of necessity, however, is rather the dissolution of that hardness; for it is the coming-together of itself in the other with itself, — the *liberation*, which is not the flight of abstraction, but, in the other actual, with which the actual is bound together through the power of necessity, to have itself not as an other, but its own being and positing. The great intuition of the Spinozistic substance is *in itself* the *liberation* from finite being-for-itself; but the Concept itself is *for itself* the power of necessity and the substantial freedom.

[p. 78]

#### Part Three.

### The Doctrine of the Concept.

§. 109.

The Concept is the *free*, as the pure negativity of the reflection of essence into itself or the *power* of substance, — and as the *totality* of this negativity, *what is determinate in and for itself*.

§. 110.

The progression of the Concept is *development*, in that what is distinguished is immediately posited as the identical, or determinacy is as a free being of the Concept.

§. 111.

The Doctrine of the Concept divides into the doctrine 1) of the subjective or *formal* Concept, 2) of the Concept as immediate, or of *objectivity*, 3) of the *Idea*, the subject-Object, the unity of the Concept and objectivity, the absolute truth.

The *ordinary logic* comprehends within itself only what occurs here as a *part of the third* part of the whole; besides this, perhaps, the so-called laws of thinking that occurred above, and in applied logic still some things about cognition. To show the poverty of this scope is all the more superfluous, since the latter presents itself

[p. 79]

only as a contingently *picked-up material*, and no thought at all is given to any justification that it should be neither more nor even less. On the other hand, the scope that is given to the logical in this exposition is derived and justified through its own development. In relation to the preceding logical determinations, the determinations of being and essence, this can be remarked: that they are not merely determinations of thought; in their passing over, the dialectical moment, and in their return into themselves and totality, they prove themselves to be *Concepts*. But they are only *determinate* Concepts, Concepts in themselves, or, what is the same, *for us*, in that the *other*, into which every determination *passes over*, or in which it *shines*, is determined neither as *particular*, nor its third as *singular* or *subject*, and the identity of the determination in its opposite, its freedom, is not posited, because it is not *universality*. 2) The logic of the Concept is understood, in its customary treatment, as a merely *formal* science, i.e. that it does not matter at all, in the *form* as such of the Concept, of judgment, and of syllogism, whether something is *true*; but this depends solely on the *content*. If the logical forms of the Concept were really dead, ineffective, and indifferent receptacles of representations or thoughts, then knowledge of them would be a very superfluous and dispensable *history*. In fact, however, they are, conversely, as forms of the Concept, *the living spirit of the actual*, and of the actual only that is true which, *by virtue of these forms*, *through them* and *in them*, is *true*. The validity and truth of these forms themselves

[p. 80]

has, however, hitherto never been considered and investigated, any more than their necessary connection.

A.

#### The Subjective Concept.

##### a.) The Concept as Such.

§. 112.

The *Concept* as such contains the moments of *universality*, as free likeness with itself in its determinacy, of *particularity*, of the determinacy in which the universal remains undimmed, equal to itself, and of *singularity*, as the reflection of determinacy into itself, which negative unity with itself is at the same time *the determinate in and for itself*, and what is identical with itself or universal.

The singular is the same as what the actual is, only that the former has proceeded from the Concept, and is thus *posited* as universal, as the negative identity with itself. The *actual*, because it is only as yet *in itself* or *immediately* the *unity* of essence and existence, *can* effect; the singularity of the Concept, however, is simply what *effects*, and indeed also no longer, like the *cause*, with the semblance of effecting an other, but what effects *itself*.

§. 113.

The Concept is the simply *concrete*, because the negative unity with itself, singularity, constitutes its relation to itself, universality. The moments of the Concept cannot, to this extent, be separated; the determinations of reflection *are supposed* to be grasped and to count for themselves, separated from the opposite one; but since in the Concept their *identity* is *posited*, each of its moments can be grasped immediately only out of and with the others.

[p. 81]

Nothing is more commonly heard said than that the Concept is something *abstract*. This is quite correct insofar as it is not the *Idea*. To this extent the subjective Concept is still *formal*, yet not at all as if it should ever have or receive any other content than itself. — As the absolute form itself it is all determinacy, but as that is in its truth. Although it is therefore abstract, it is the concrete, indeed the simply concrete, the subject as such; insofar as, as Concept, it exists distinguished from its objectivity, it is spirit. Everything else concrete is not so *concrete*, least of all what one commonly understands by the concrete, a manifold held together externally. — What one commonly calls Concepts, and indeed determinate Concepts, e.g. human being, house, animal, etc., are anything but Concepts, but simple determinations and abstract representations, — abstractions that take from the Concept only the moment of universality, and leave out particularity and singularity, and for that very reason abstract precisely from the Concept.

§. 114.

The moment of *singularity* first posits the moments of the Concept as differences, in that it is the Concept’s negative reflection-into-itself, therefore *at first* its free distinguishing, as the *first negation*, with which the determinacy of the Concept is posited, but as *particularity*, i.e. that the distinct ones in the first place have only the determinacy of the moments of the Concept against one another, and likewise their identity, that the one is the other, is posited; this *posited* particularity of the Concept is the *judgment*.

Holding fast the universal, particular and singular as separated Concepts is the business of *abstraction*

[p. 82]

or of the understanding that holds to the determination of reflection of *identity*. Moreover, if *species* of the Concept as Concepts, determinate Concepts, insofar as a content fetched from elsewhere is not supposed to constitute the determinacy, were to be specified, then those moments alone would be the true species. — The ordinary species of *clear, distinct* and *adequate* Concepts belong not to the Concept but to psychology, insofar as by clear and distinct Concepts mere *representations* are meant, by the former an abstract, simply determinate one, by the latter one of the same kind, on which, however, a *mark*, namely a sign for the *subjective* cognition, is in addition singled out. The *adequate* one alludes more to the Concept, indeed even to the Idea, but expresses as yet nothing but the formal element of the agreement of a Concept, or also of a representation, with its Object, — an external thing. — Underlying the difference of *subordinate* and *coordinate* ones are the conceptless difference of the universal and the particular, and their relationship-relation in an external reflection. Further, however, an enumeration of species of *contrary* and *contradictory*, *affirmative*, *negative* Concepts etc. is nothing other than a picking up by chance of determinacies of thought which are Concepts only *in themselves* or only by being treated as such, but are otherwise a content or determinations which have nothing to do with the determinacy of the Concept itself. — The true differences of the Concept, the universal, particular and singular, constitute *species* of it only insofar as they are held apart by an external reflection. The Concept is rather only the *universal*, which

[p. 83]

determines itself and thereby is *particular*, but just as immediately sublates this its particularity as determinacy, returned therein into itself, and thereby is *singular*, and *universal*, in one identity. — The immanent distinguishing and reflection of the Concept itself is posited in the *judgment*.

##### b.) Judgment.

§. 115.

The *judgment* is the Concept in its particularity, as the distinguishing *relation* of its moments, which are at the same time posited as being for themselves and identical with themselves, and thus step forth over against one another as singular and universal.

1) Ordinarily, in the case of the judgment one thinks first of the *independence* of the extremes, of the subject and predicate, that the former is a thing or a determination for itself, and likewise the predicate a universal determination, outside that subject, perhaps in my head, — which are then brought together by me and thereby judged. Since, however, the *copula*, *is*, asserts the predicate of the subject, that external, subjective *subsuming* is sublated again, and the judgment is taken as a determination of the object itself. — The *etymological* meaning of *judgment* [*Urtheil*] in our language is deeper and expresses the unity of the Concept as the first, and its distinction as the *original* division, which is what the judgment is in truth. 2) The abstract judgment expresses at first the proposition: the *singular* is the *universal*, for these are the essential determinations that the subject and predicate have against one another. — This proposition is a contradiction, and precisely this constitutes the necessity that the judgment determines itself further, to *identity*

[p. 84]

of its subject and predicate. It is also immediately evident from this that such abstract judgments have no *truth*; they can well be *correct* through their content, i.e. have a *truth* in the sphere of perception, of finite thinking in general, but truth in and for itself cannot pertain to them; for the subject and the predicate, the abstract singular and universal (which of them is now taken for the Concept, and which for reality, is indifferent) do not agree; rather one is supposed to be what the other is not. It therefore does not depend on the *content* whether e.g. the judgment: *this rose is red*, has truth or not; in such sensuous content it is not to be sought, and the form of such a judgment is not able, as form, to grasp it. — Philosophical truth cannot, for this very reason, be expressed in a single judgment; spirit, life, the Concept in general, is only motion within itself, which in the judgment is precisely killed. It is therefore already on account of the *form* of the judgment alone that such content *does not have the truth*. — 3) The *copula*: *is*, still comes from the nature of the Concept, of being *identical* with itself in its externalization; the singular and the universal are, as moments of the Concept, such determinacies as cannot be isolated; — the earlier determinacies of reflection did, in the relationships too, posit the relation to one another, but their connection is only the *having*, not the *being*, the *identity posited as such*, or *universality*. The judgment is for this very reason the true *particularity* of the Concept, for this is the determinacy or distinction of it, which however remains *universality*.

[p. 85]

§. 116.

The judgment is usually taken in a *subjective* sense, as an *operation* and form that occurs merely in *self-conscious* thinking. Since, however, this distinction is as yet not at all present in the logical, the judgment is quite universal, and *all things are a judgment*, — i.e. *singulars*, which have a *universality* or inner nature within themselves; or a *universal* that is *individuated*; and universality and singularity are distinguished in them, but are at the same time identical.

Subjective judgments are distinguished from *propositions*; in the latter something is asserted of the subjects that does not stand to them in the relationship of universality, — a state, a singular action and the like. It is something quite empty to say that such propositions, e.g. *I slept well last night*, — or even: *Present arms! can* be brought into the form of a judgment. — Only then would a proposition: a carriage is passing by, — be a judgment, if it could be doubtful whether what is moving past is a carriage, or whether the object is moving, and not rather the standpoint from which we observe it. Here, in the subject, the object, which constitutes the subject of the proposition, and the determination that is supposed to pertain to it, are separated from one another, and each is regarded at first as an independent one, the object as an external thing, and the determination as a *universal* representation, still separated from it, in my head, and the connection of the latter with the former then becomes a judgment.

[p. 86]

§. 117.

The *finitude* of things consists, at this standpoint, in this, that their determinate being and their universal nature (their body and their soul) are indeed united; otherwise they would be nothing; but that they are also separable, and have essential independence against one another.

§. 118.

In the abstract judgment: *the singular is the universal*, the subject as the *singular* is immediately *concrete*, the predicate on the other hand *abstract*, the *universal* and rather abstraction itself. But since subject and predicate are connected through: *is*, or the determinacy of the Concept has the whole Concept in it, the predicate too must contain in its universality the determinacy of the subject; thus it is *particularity*. Because it is, further, the posited *identity* of subject and predicate, and thereby what is indifferent to this difference of form, it is the *content*.

§. 119.

Since singularity and universality constitute the universal determinations of form of subject and predicate against one another, the further determinacy of the judgment, whereby it is a particular one against others, falls at first into the *content*, the *particularity*; insofar, however, as this at the same time, as *determination of form*, has a relationship to singularity and universality, this too is further determined with them.

Since the content of the judgment, as it is an immediate one, falls into the particularity of the predicate, the formalism of the judgment emerges from this on the side of the content. The subject has its determinacy and content only in the predicate; for itself it is therefore a mere representation or an empty name. In the judgments: *God* is the

[p. 87]

most real, etc., or the *absolute* is identical with itself, etc., *God*, the *absolute*, is a mere name; what he *is* is first said in the predicate. What he might otherwise be as a concrete, *this* judgment does not concern. — But if, precisely because the subject is the concrete, the predicate is to express only a *single* property, then it does not thereby correspond to its subject. According to the determination of the Concept, however, not only the one side of the judgment, the *subject*, is the *concrete totality*, but also the other, the *predicate*, namely as unity of particularity and universality. To this extent the judgment is identical with itself in its subject and predicate. The formalism of the *immediate* judgment — (and it is usually taken in this sense, in that the *positive* judgment, or the form of judgment in general, counts as the abiding genus) — then consists in this, that the content of the predicate is an immediate one, and the particularity a determination indifferent to the universality; e.g., the universal of the predicate: *Red* is *color*; but color is equally also *blue*, *yellow*, etc.

§. 120.

1) The immediate judgment is the *judgment of determinate being*; the subject posited in a universality, as its predicate, which is an immediate quality, one that therefore does not correspond to the concrete nature of the subject, nor, as immediate particularity, to the universality of the predicate, nor in general to the *unity* of the Concept in its determinations.

It is one of the most essential logical prejudices that such qualitative judgments as: the rose is red, or is not red, can contain truth.

[p. 88]

§. 121.

This untrue judgment therefore falls apart into the doubled relation. Since neither the particularity of the predicate nor the universality of the same, which two determinations are diverse in this immediate judgment, corresponds to the concrete subject, 1) these must be abstracted from, and only the emptily *identical* relation: the singular A is the singular A, be posited — which gives an *identical* judgment. 2) The other relation is the present complete inadequacy of subject and predicate, which gives a so-called *infinite* judgment.

Examples of the latter are: spirit is not an elephant, a lion is not a table, etc. — propositions that are absurd but correct, just like the identical propositions, a lion is a lion, spirit is spirit, — but which, to be sure, are the truth of the immediate, so-called qualitative judgment, yet are in general not judgments, and can occur only in a subjective thinking, which can also hold fast an untrue abstraction. Considered objectively, they express the nature of *that which is* or of *sensuous* things, namely that they are a falling apart into an *empty* identity and into a *filled* relation, which is the *qualitative otherness of the related*, their complete inadequacy. — The different forms of judgment are in general the spheres of *being* and *essence*, which run through the Concept.

§. 122.

2) The sublated judgment of immediacy is the judgment of *reflection*, the subject with a predicate as such an existence, which shows itself not as an immediate one, but as an essential one, one that comprises a relationship.

[p. 89]

§. 123.

The subject, at first still the immediate one, as a *singular* or *this*, is raised in this relation above its singularity. This widening is, in it as immediate, an external one, the subjective reflection, at first the indeterminate particularity, as *particularity*. But, determined through the singularity of the subject, in which it is, it becomes the *allness*.

§. 124.

Through the fact that the subject is likewise determined as a universal, the identity of the same and of the predicate is *posited*, just as thereby the determination of the judgment itself is posited as indifferent. The unity of the *content* as the posited simple Concept makes the judgment-relation, in the distinctness of its determinations of form, into a *necessary* one.

§. 125.

3) The *judgment of necessity* contains partly, in the predicate, the *substance* or *nature* of the subject, the concrete as universal, thus as *genus*, (*categorical* judgment), partly, however, the shape of independent actuality of the two sides, and their identity as an inner one, and the actuality of the one as not its own, but that of the other (*hypothetical* judgment). There now emerges with this, at the externalization of the Concept, the judgment, this externalization and its identity, the real Concept itself; — the universal that is identical with itself in its exclusive singularity; or the judgment which has the same universal for its two sides, once as such, the other time as the totality of its self-excluding particularization, or singularity that has become universal; — *disjunctive* judgment.

[p. 90]

§. 126.

4) The *judgment of the Concept* has for its content the Concept, as which it has appeared through the judgment of necessity, the universal with its determinacy; and since, as judgment, it is also the opposition of the Concept, the latter is, as subject, the singular, as it is *immediately* a universal and external determinate being, and, as predicate, the *reflection* of the particular determinate being on the universal, — the agreement or non-agreement of these two determinations; good, true, correct, etc.

§. 127.

This judgment is thus the judgment of *truth*, (the *apodictic*). All things are a genus and an end in a singular actuality of a particular constitution; their true being is this subjectivity in general, which contains both within itself, yet still as a finite one, in which the particular can be in accordance with the universal, or also not.

§. 128.

Subject and predicate are in this way themselves each the whole judgment; at the same time the particularity of the subject, its immediate constitution, is the *mediating ground* between the singularity of the actual and between its universality, namely that of the judgment about it. Thereby the empty *Is* of the copula is *filled*, and a relation of subject and predicate is posited which is no longer the immediate one, but a mediated one. What mediates, however, is not the *ground* as such, but the mediation is in the determination of the Concept, into whose unity the differences of form of the judgment have gone back; — it is the *syllogism*.

[p. 91]

##### c.) Syllogism.

§. 129.

The syllogism is the unity of the Concept and of the judgment; — it is the Concept as the simple identity of its determinations, and judgment, insofar as it is at the same time posited in its reality, namely in the difference of its determinations. The syllogism is therefore the *rational* and *everything* rational.

The syllogism is usually wont, to be sure, to be given out as the *form of the rational*, but of a subjective one, and without any connection being shown between it and any other rational content, e.g., a rational principle, a rational action, Idea, etc. In fact, formal syllogizing too is the rational in such a reasonless manner that it has nothing to do with a rational import. But since such a one can be rational only through the determinacy through which *thinking* is reason, it can be so only through the form which the syllogism is. — The latter is nothing other than the *posited*, (at first formally) *real Concept*. It is therefore the *essential ground* of everything true; and the *definition of the absolute* is now that it is the syllogism, or, with this determination expressed as a proposition, *Everything is a syllogism*. Everything is Concept, and its determinate being is the difference of the moments of the same, so that its universal nature gives itself external reality through particularity, and thereby makes itself the singular. — Or conversely, the actual is a singular that raises itself, through particularity, into universality. It is one, but just as much the stepping apart of the moments of the Concept, and the

[p. 92]

syllogism is the cycle of the mediation of its moments, through which it posits itself as one.

§. 130.

The *immediate* syllogism is, according to the form of the determinations of the Concept, that they stand in relationship to one another as abstract ones, and indeed that the two extremes are singularity and universality, but the Concept, as the middle term that joins the two together, is likewise only the abstract, simple particularity, as the universal that is at the same time posited in determinacy. Hereby the extremes are posited as just as indifferent toward one another as toward their middle term, as subsisting for themselves. This syllogism is thus the rational as conceptless, — the formal *syllogism of the understanding*. — It is therefore, considered objectively, the nature of external determinate being. In the latter, subjectivity, as thinghood, is separable from its properties, its particularity, and just as separable from its universality, insofar as the latter is both the genus of the thing and its external connection with other things.

§. 131.

1) The first syllogism is the *syllogism of determinate being* or the *qualitative* one, as it was stated in the preceding §. S—P—U, that a subject, as singular, is *joined together* through *a quality* with a *universality*.

§. 132.

This syllogism is entirely *contingent*, in that the middle term, as abstract particularity, is *only any determinacy whatever* of the subject, of which it has several, and can therefore be joined together with just as *many kinds of other* universalities, just as a single particularity too can in turn have various determinacies within itself, and thus the subject through *the same medius*

[p. 93]

*terminus* can be related to *different* universals.

By such syllogisms, therefore, the most diverse can be, — as it is called, *proved*. It is only necessary to take the *medius terminus* from which the transition to the required determination can be made. With another *medius terminus*, however, something else can be *proved*. — The more concrete an object is, the more sides it has that belong to it and can serve as *mediis terminis*. Which among these sides is more essential than the other would again rest on such a syllogizing, which holds to the single determinacy and can therefore likewise easily find for it a side and *respect* according to which it can be made to *count* as *important and necessary*.

§. 132.

This syllogism is equally contingent through the form of the *relation* that is in it. According to the Concept of the syllogism, the true is the relation of different ones through a middle term, which is their unity. The relations of the extremes to the middle term, however, (the so-called *premises*, the *major premise* and *minor premise*) are rather *immediate* relations.

This contradiction of the syllogism expresses itself in turn through an infinite *progress*, as the demand that the premises likewise, each through a syllogism, be proved; but since the latter has precisely such immediate premises, this demand, and indeed one constantly doubling itself, repeats itself *into the infinite*.

§. 133.

This contradiction is in the syllogism itself as its own dialectic. Since its *termini*, on the one hand, in

[p. 94]

their determinacy as immediate ones have stepped apart, they relate to one another not merely as the determinations of reflection do, but are posited as *identical*, because they are moments of the Concept; the singular *is* the particular, and the particular *is* the universal. Since through the immediate syllogism S—P—U the singular has been mediated with the universal, it is posited in this conclusion as universal. The singular as subject, which includes universality within itself, is hereby itself the unity of the two extremes and what mediates.

§. 134.

The *second figure* of the syllogism, U—S—P, expresses the truth of the first, namely that the mediation has taken place in singularity and is hereby something contingent. This figure joins together the universal (as subject, for in the preceding conclusion it has received the determinacy of singularity) with the particular; the *universal* is hereby posited through this conclusion as particular, hence as what mediates the extremes, whose places the others now take; — the *third figure* of the syllogism: P—U—S.

The so-called *figures* of the syllogism (Aristotle rightly knows only *three* of them; the *fourth* is a highly superfluous, indeed even tasteless addition of the moderns) are, in the usual treatment of them, merely placed alongside one another, without the slightest thought being given to showing their necessity, still less their significance and worth. Their necessity rests, as shown, on the fact that each moment, a determination of the Concept, is thereby itself the whole and the *mediating* ground. But what other determinations the propositions of such syllogisms, whether they may be universal, etc., or negative, may have in order to bring out a correct syllogism,

[p. 95]

this is a merely *mechanical* inquiry, for which the fixed rule has once for all been given, and which, on account of its conceptless mechanism and inner meaninglessness, could not but fall into contempt. Least of all can one, for the importance of such an inquiry and of the syllogism of the understanding in general, appeal to Aristotle, who, to be sure, described these, just as almost, one might say, countless other forms of spirit and of nature, and sought out and stated their determinacy. But the syllogism of the understanding appears with him as nothing other than the subsumption of singulars or particulars under something more universal. Not only does he distinguish absolute thinking expressly from the thinking of that which can be true or false, where affirmation or negation takes place, — propositions in general belong for him in the latter sphere, — but in his purely metaphysical Concepts, as well as in the Concepts of the natural and of the spiritual, he was so far from wanting to make the form of the syllogism their foundation and criterion, that one could say that probably not even a single one could have arisen or been left standing, if it were to be subjected to the syllogism of the understanding. Aristotle shows himself far too speculative for such a form. Alongside the much that is descriptive and of the understanding which he has, the ruling element in him is nevertheless the Concept; how could he have got on with the syllogism of the understanding?

§. 135.

Since each moment has run through the place of the middle term and of the extremes, their determinate difference from one another has sublated itself, and the syllogism has at first, in this form of the lack of difference of its moments

[p. 96]

the external identity of the understanding, — *likeness* — for its relation; — the *quantitative*, or *mathematical* syllogism.

§. 136.

2) With regard to the determinacy, however, it has resulted that it does not constitute in its abstraction the essence of the syllogism; it has been gone beyond, and particularity now constitutes, *developed*, as *singularity* determined *as universality*, the mediating element; — *syllogism of reflection*; in which, as syllogism of *allness*, of *induction* and *analogy*, the middle term likewise runs through the three determinations of the Concept.

§. 137.

In the syllogism of reflection the shining Concept is the middle term, but both extremes, the determinations of the Concept in general, are themselves nothing but the semblance of the same. Since now this middle term has likewise run through all determinations of the Concept, or, conversely, the extremes have likewise assumed the determination of the middle term that unites them, semblance has sublated itself in semblance, and the *substantial unity* of the Concept, or the true universality, has been established.

§. 138.

3) The *syllogism of necessity* sets up the *particular* expressly in the significance of the determinate *genus*, (in the *categorical* syllogism) and the *singular* expressly in the significance of immediate being (in the *hypothetical* syllogism), as the mediating determinations, so that now the mediating *universal*, as totality of its *particularizations* and as a *single* particular, is exclusive singularity (in the *disjunctive* syllogism.)

[p. 97]

§. 138.

The syllogism of the understanding has, in this its course, sublated the determinacy and the being-outside-itself, in which the Concept is immediate, in that, *first*, each of the determinations steps into the functions of the other two, *secondly*, the *immediate relations* thereby likewise become mediated ones, and *thirdly* the unity, outside of which its determinations were at first as extremes, *posited* the same ones, which it at first had only *in itself*, in reflected relation, and then itself as their substantial unity, at the same time in their development.

§. 139.

Hereby it has come about, 1) that every mediated relation, for the sake of the two immediate ones which it contains, *presupposes* the two others and so, reciprocally, itself, and every positing is a presupposing, 2) that the Concept in its *single differences* is itself posited as the *totality and whole syllogism*, 3) that the difference of the same, as *unity*, over against itself as that which is dirempted *into extremes*, falls away. Through this the Concept is completely realized, and, as this unity of its differences with itself, is the *Object*.

The relation of the *Concept* to *being*, or of the *subject* to the *Object*, constitutes, down to the most recent times, one of the most interesting, or rather the most interesting, and thereby the most difficult point of philosophy, which had not yet attained its clarification. This point has received its greatest significance in the task of proving, *from the Concept of God*, his *determinate being*. This means, in its proper sense, nothing other than presenting the transition of the Concept *out of itself* into objectivity. — *Anselm*, in whom

[p. 98]

the most remarkable thought of this proof is first found, says briefly thus: *Certe id, quo majus cogitari nequit, non potest esse in intellectu solo. Si enim vel in solo intellectu est, potest cogitari esse et in re: quod majus est. Si ergo id, quo majus cogitari non potest, est in solo intellectu; id ipsum, quo majus cogitari non potest, est, quo majus cogitari potest. Sed certe hoc esse non potest.* [Surely that than which a greater cannot be thought cannot be in the understanding alone. For if it is even in the understanding alone, it can be thought to be *also in reality*: which is greater. If, therefore, that than which a greater cannot be thought is in the understanding alone, that very thing than which a greater cannot be thought is that than which a greater can be thought. But surely this cannot be.] — This argumentation is at first an external one; but granting this, it contains this, that the mere Concept as *subjective*, the merely *thought* highest being, insofar as thinking has an opposition in being, is only a finite, not a *true* one, and that this subjectivity is rather to be sublated. The perfectly thorough thought of this argumentation has, however, received a quite incorrect and shallow position through the fact that it has been forced into the form of the syllogism of the understanding. The *Concept* of the most real being is supposed to contain within itself all realities, thus among them also the reality of existence. Thereby only the *positive* side is expressed, according to which being is a moment of the Concept, but not the *negative* one, according to which the one-sidedness of the *subjective* Concept is to be sublated. — The identity of the Concept and of objectivity has appeared in the philosophy hitherto in the two forms which it could have, either to be grasped as *relationship of reflection*, — namely with the presupposition of the absolute *diversity* and independence of the *Concept for itself* and of *objectivity for itself*, as a merely relative relation of the two; or else as their *absolute identity*. The latter has, after all, lain at the basis of each and every philosophy, — either as an inner, *unexpressed* thought, (with

[p. 99]

Plato and Aristotle, all their predecessors and the ancient philosophy in general) — or else as a *presupposed definition*, axiom, (e.g., in Descartes, Spinoza) as immediate certainty, faith, intellectual intuition. — It has already been stated above that it is the distinguishing mark of critical philosophy, in common with all non-philosophy, to remain fixed at finite cognition and at the subjective Concept as such, as an absolute. Anselm in his time already experienced this contradiction, that even what is non-actual and false can be thought. In fact nothing is falser than the merely subjectively thought being of God; it is therefore to be given up, and just as much to be grasped as objective. — But even if the identity of subjectivity and objectivity has been the first foundation of philosophy, then 1) this foundation, the definition, immediate certainty, intellectual intuition, is assumed as an *immediate* one, since it is, by its nature but also by its express form, a *mediated* one, because it essentially contains not an *abstract*-simple one, but the *identity* as that of *differentiated* ones, thus negativity and dialectic, yet within itself. 2) The demand that it be *proved* is therefore necessary through itself; — i.e., the demand that in these differentiated ones, because they are such, their *identity* be shown as proceeding from them. The *need* for this gave critical philosophy its origin, but the impotence to accomplish what was demanded gave it its result. — An *external dialectic* can indeed demonstrate the contradictions that result from the separation of the subjective and the objective, and from the mere *relationship-relation* of the same. But the result of such

[p. 100]

dialectic is at first only negative, and the transition from it to the positive Idea again only a syllogism of the understanding. — But in any case dialectic is the activity of the Concept, and in the present task it is also the *Concept* itself as such that is the object. The only true kind of proof that the subjective in its truth is just as much the objective is the showing of the determination of the Concept, through itself, to objectivity. This movement, however, is its self-determination to *judgment*, then to *syllogism*, and to the complete sublating of its development, which it has in the same in the form of determinations of the understanding and their relation. Since in this the moments of the Concept determine themselves even as the whole Concept, their difference sublates itself *in itself*, and just so, through their negative relation, in that their being-presupposed is a positing and mediating, *for itself*. The Concept, thus realized through itself, has at first crossed over into the Object. This is thus in and for itself the Concept, and determines itself further, through the re-emergence of the Concept out of it, to the *Idea*, to the absolute unity of both that differentiates itself into Object and Concept.

B.

#### The Object.

§. 140.

As being to determinate being, essence to existence, so the Concept resolves itself to *objectivity*, to *immediacy*, into which the mediation of itself with itself through judgment and syllogism has gone together into the simple unity, and which is therefore only *in itself*, not *for itself*, the totality of the Concept, or the *being in and for itself*.

[p. 101]

The definition: *the absolute is the Object*, is indeed, taken in general, to be found in all those ways of representation, also philosophizing, in which God has a relationship external to the Concept. The Object, however, has in such a relationship then only the *abstract* significance of *true being* over against the subject, and the inner reason of the same is not the Concept, because it is supposed to be an unknowable. More determinately, however, that definition is contained in the *Leibnizian monad*, which is supposed to be an Object, but *in itself* representing, and indeed the totality of the world-representation. Nothing comes into it from outside, but it is in itself the whole Concept, differentiated only by its own greater or lesser development.

§. 141.

The Object, because it is in itself the totality of the Concept, is the *indeterminate*, yet *receptive of all determinations*, but just as much remaining *indifferent* toward all. It is therefore just as much a many diverse in itself as an identity of the diverse, a dependent and an independent, and these conceptless determinations are external to the Object and to one another.

##### a). Mechanism.

§. 142.

Because the Object is the Concept only *in itself*, it has the same at first *outside it*, and all determinacy is in it as an externally posited one. As unity of something differentiated it is therefore a *composite*, an aggregate, and the efficacy on another is an *impressed* one; — *formal mechanism*.

[p. 102]

§. 143.

Indeterminacy is for that very reason, because it stands over against determinacy, *determinacy*. There are therefore Objects, of *determinate* difference, which however is an *external* one in them, in a relationship of reflection to one another. Insofar as they present themselves as indifferent to this external determination, they appear as *independent* and *offering resistance*; but insofar as they at the same time suffer the external determination in this independence, they undergo *violence*.

§. 144.

Through violence the *dependence* of the Objects manifests itself, that is, *negativity* as *proper* to their nature; but with this the relationship of reflection, according to which determinacy, as the negative, is only something external in the Object, sublates itself.

§. 145.

This inner negativity is the *immanent independence* of the Object, which is hereby identical with its externality. The identity as Concept, repelling itself from itself, forms the syllogism that the immanent negativity, as the *central* singularity of an Object, relates itself to dependent Objects as the other extreme, through a middle term which unites in itself the centrality and dependence of the Objects; — *absolute mechanism*.

§. 146.

This syllogism is a threefold of syllogisms. The bad *singularity* of the *dependent* Objects, in which formal mechanism is at home, is, as dependence, just as much the external *universality*. These Objects are therefore the

[p. 103]

*middle term* between the *absolute* and the *relative* center; (the form of the syllogism U—S—P) for it is through this dependence that those two are dirempted and extremes, as well as that they are related to one another. Likewise the *absolute centrality*, as the substantial-universal (— the gravity that remains identical), which as the pure negativity just as much includes singularity within itself, is the mediating element between the *relative center* and the *dependent* Objects, the form of the syllogism P—U—S, and indeed just as essentially according to the immanent singularity as dirempting, as according to universality as identical holding-together and undisturbed being-within-itself.

§. 147.

The negativity or selfhood of the Object in absolute mechanism is still the universal or inner one; its diversity is therefore still the indifferent one, and qualitatively only that of abstract being-for-itself or not-being-for-itself, of independence or dependence. As the Concept, however, it *determines* itself, and the particularization of the Concept gives itself objective difference in the Object.

##### b). Chemism.

§. 148.
