The *differing Object* is the *chemical*. It has an immanent *determinacy*, which constitutes its nature, and in which it has existence. But because its essence is the *Concept*, it is the contradiction of this its totality and of the determinacy of its existence; it is therefore the striving to sublate the contradiction, and to make its determinate being equal to the Concept.

§. 149.

The chemical process therefore has the *neutral* of its tensed extremes, which these are *in themselves*, as the

[p. 104]

product; the Concept, the universal, joins together through the difference of the Objects, the particularization, with singularity, the product. Equally, however, the other syllogisms too are contained in this process; singularity, as activity, is likewise a mediating element, just as the universal is, the essence of the tensed extremes, which comes to determinate being in the product.

§. 150.

In the product the determinate properties which the extremes had against one another are sublated. But because the extremes are the Concept only *in itself*, the neutral product is indeed in accordance with it, but the *inspiriting* principle of their difference does not exist in it and is external to it. The Object is still indifferent to the negative unity of the *Concept* as Concept, or the latter does not yet exist in it for itself, and the neutral is therefore a separable one.

§. 151.

The judging principle, which dirempts the neutral into differing extremes, and likewise gives to the indifferent Object in general its difference and inspiriting against an other, and the process as tensing separation, therefore falls outside that first process and makes up only a particular side of it.

§. 152.

The externality of these processes, which lets them appear as independent over against one another, shows, however, their finitude in the passing over into products, in which they are sublated. The Concept is hereby liberated from this determinacy in which it is in each of them, through their distinctness, in which each sublates the other, and through their extinction in the product, and steps over against the Object for itself, — as *end*.

[p. 105]

##### c.) Teleology.

§. 153.

The *end* is the Concept existing *for itself*, which makes up only the *in-itself* of mechanism and chemism. Because it is the determinate in and for itself, the *concrete universal*, which as the absolute form has the determination in itself, but, as having passed through those immediately preceding stages, in which the determinations of form have an external reality, has become free from them and *universal*, it has the determinacy as *content* within itself. As difference of form, it is the subjective, but as the negativity of this its determinacy of form in itself; — the drive to translate itself into *objectivity*.

The *end*-Concept has rightly been called *Concept of reason*, and has been set over against the understanding, as the abstract-universal in general, and in particular against the relationship of causality. The relation of the abstract-universal to the particular is understood as a *subsuming*, insofar as the former does not itself have particularity in it; but thereby it is an abstract. One may determine the *absolute* as pure being, first cause or ground, and then proceed on these determinations of relationship to further knowing, yet it is not determined as *reason* insofar as its essence is not grasped as *end*. — On the whole it is indeed superfluous to say *Concept of reason*, for the Concept is nothing other than reason, and what one calls the Concept of the understanding is not at all the Concept, but the abstract determination of the *universal*, or any content that is held in the form of this simple abstraction. Yet the expression, *Concept of reason*, can indicate the closer significance, that the Concept

[p. 106]

actually be as Concept and be grasped as such. Concepts of the understanding are then called those such as being, quality, etc., identity, force, causality, etc., which are not yet posited as Concepts in their *content*. But of course that which in its content is Concept, like the Concept itself, also the end and reason, can be conceptless through the form, as the syllogism is in the consideration of ordinary logic, and this consideration itself is something conceptless, irrational, merely of the understanding. — The consideration of the end is constituted in just the same way, when a content is sought from outside, and likewise elsewhere the activity to realize it. It is thus considered purely according to its *finitude*, or in the manner of the understanding, not according to its Concept. It has already been remarked that the end 1) has a content *determined* out of itself as the absolutely first. This made up one side of the *reality* in which the Concept as end is, as the identity of the Concept reflected into itself, thus to be as that which is indifferent toward the determination of form. Hereby it is the *universal* that is *for itself*, differentiated from particularity and singularity; an *extreme* in the whole teleological syllogism, over against the mediating realization, and the end posited, as carried out, in singularity. This universal, however, is at the same time the identity which goes through all the *termini* of the syllogism, maintains itself in them, and is their substance. The end is 2) the disjunctive syllogism. The universal is immediately singularity, from which it is disjoined. On the one hand it is here determined as *content* over against the form, as a particular over against another particular, as over against a merely *diverse* one, but at the same time as the particular subjective over against the objective, *opposed*.

[p. 107]

The disjoining singularity, however, is on the other hand, as negative *unity*, just as much the mediating element of these two and the sublating of this opposition, the *activity*, the translating of the subjective into objectivity. — Through the different forms of the *formal syllogism* the Concept passes (§§ 131–138) and thereby gives itself the first immediate *realization*, without its being as yet itself *posited* as the *moving* or dialectical element; it is this only *in itself*. But after it has, through the sublation of the Object, posited objectivity within itself and given itself the negative relation to itself, it is as the Concept *that is for itself*, — as a subjective that is itself the *ought of realization*, and the dialectic as immanent *activity*.

§. 154.

The teleological relation is initially *external* purposiveness; for the Concept is still immediately over against the Object, it has not yet brought it forth out of itself. The end is therefore *finite*, thus partly in respect of its *content*, partly also in that it has, in an object to be found at hand, as the *material* of its realization, a presupposed external condition. Its self-determination is to this extent only formal, enclosed within the subjective end, and the carried-out end only an external form.

This finite end belongs to an external, finite reason, hence properly to an *external understanding*; — the Concept too in its immediate determinations, the judgment and syllogism of the understanding, have as such only an existence in a subjective understanding. The ordinary representation of an end falls merely on such an understanding and end. With the Concept

[p. 108]

of *inner purposiveness Kant* has awakened the Idea in general, and in particular that of life. *Practical reason* he has freed from external purposiveness only insofar as he has recognized as absolute the *formal* element of the will, self-determination in the form of universality; but the content is indeterminate, and purposive action is conditioned by a material, and for that reason also brings about only the formal good, or what is the same, carries out only *means*. — Already *Aristotle’s* Concept of life contains inner purposiveness, and therefore stands infinitely far above the Concept of modern teleology. —

§. 155.

The teleological relation is the syllogism in which the subjective end joins together with objectivity through a middle term, which is the unity of both, as the *purposive activity* and as the objectivity immediately posited under the end, the *means*.

§. 156.

1) *The subjective* end is the syllogism in which the universal Concept joins together through particularity with singularity in such a way that the latter, as self-determination, particularizes the former and makes it into a determinate content, and at the same time is the return into itself, in that it sublates, as something deficient, the particularity of the Concept presupposed over against objectivity (see Remark to § 153) and thereby at the same time turns itself outward.

§. 157.

2) This *activity turned outward* relates itself, as the *singularity* that is identical, in the subjective end, with the particularity in which *external objectivity* is *enclosed*, *immediately*

[p. 109]

to the Object, and takes possession of it as of a *means*. The Concept is this immediate *power* over mechanism and chemism, because it is their truth and at the same time the negativity identical with itself. The whole *middle term* is now this — inner power of the Concept as *activity*, with which the *Object* as means is immediately united.

§. 158.

3) The purposive activity with its means is still directed outward, because the end too is *not* identical with the Object, and is first to be mediated with it. The means, as Object in this second premise, stands in immediate relation to the other extreme of the syllogism, objectivity as presupposed, the material; — a relation which is the sphere of the mechanism and chemism now *serving* the end. That this, the subjective end, which is the power of these processes in which the objective sublates itself on one another, is itself outside them and is that which maintains itself in them, is the cunning of reason.

§. 159.

The *realized* end is the universal that maintains itself in the objective process, which has precisely thereby given itself objectivity. But since this was, in the finite end, a *presupposed* material, one to be found at hand, the carried-out end too is one as much broken within itself as the middle term was. There has therefore come about only a form posited *externally* on the material, a *means*, just as the attained end is likewise, on account of its content, a contingent determination, and therefore also is again as a material for other ends.

§. 160.

In the *Concept*, however, the end has realized itself and sublated its finitude, which lies in the presupposed

[p. 110]

subjectivity of the end and in the independence of the Object over against it. What happens in the realizing of the end is only that *its own subjectivity* and the mere semblance of objective independence are sublated. In seizing the means the *Concept* posits itself as the essence, being *in itself*, of the Object; in the mechanical and chemical process the independence of the latter has already evaporated *in itself*, and in its course, under the mastery of the end, the semblance of that independence, its negative *against the Concept*, is sublated; but this negative is the particularity and direction outward which the Concept gave itself as self-determination; through this process it has thereby returned into itself, as negative relation to itself, or being-for-itself, which has become for itself just as much as the *objective in-itself*. — This realized end is the *Idea*.

C.

#### The Idea.

§. 161.

The Idea is the true *in and for itself*, *the absolute unity of the Concept and of objectivity*. Its ideal content is none other than the Concept in its determinations; its real content is only the Concept’s exposition, which it gives itself in the form of external determinate being.

The definition *of the absolute*, that it is the *Idea*, is now itself absolute. All previous definitions go back into this one. — Everything actual, insofar as it is something true, is the Idea, and has its truth solely through and by virtue of the Idea. The singular being is some one side of the Idea; for this, therefore, there is need of still other actualities,

[p. 111]

which perhaps likewise appear as subsisting separately for themselves; in them together and in their relation alone is the Concept realized. The singular taken on its own does not correspond to its Concept; this limitedness of its determinate being constitutes its finitude and its demise. — The Idea, furthermore, is not to be taken merely as an Idea of *something or other*, any more than the Concept merely as a determinate Concept. In that the Idea enters into determinate being, it throws its moments apart; but since it remains their ground and essence, it is in them, and as in them it is *determinate Idea*. But the absolute is this universal and One Idea, *the Idea itself*, which is just as much the system of determinate Ideas, and into which these return as into their truth. — Consciousness, which lingers in the sphere of *representing* and has only such thoughts as are still interwoven with representations, is accustomed to start from existing things, and when it ascends to the thought of their Ideas, to take the relationship of the Idea and the represented in such a way as if the existing were the real, but the Idea of it only a subjective abstraction that had its content from the former. Furthermore, the Idea as such, which has no determinate content, and not an existence, as its point of departure and support, is taken for something merely formal, logical. Here there can now no longer be talk of such relationships; the existing thing and all further determinations of it have proved themselves untrue and have gone back into the Idea as their last ground. It is thereby *demonstrated* as the true and real in and for itself; and all content that it has beyond this can be given to it only through itself. — Just as false is the representation, as if the Idea

[p. 112]

were only the *abstract*; — it is so, to be sure, insofar as everything *untrue* is consumed in it; but in its own self it is essentially *concrete*, because it is the free Concept that determines itself, and thereby itself, to reality. It would be the formal-abstract only if the Concept, which is its principle, were taken as the abstract unity, not as it is, as the *negative return into itself and singularity*.

§. 162.

The Idea can also be grasped as *reason*, as the *subject-object*, as the *unity of the ideal and the real, of the finite and the infinite, of the soul and the body*, as the *possibility* that has its *actuality in its own self*, as that whose nature can only be *comprehended as existing*, etc.; for in general all relationships of the understanding are contained in it, but in their *infinite* return and identity within themselves.

The understanding has an easy job of pointing out everything that is said of the Idea as in itself *contradictory*. But this can be turned back upon it just as well, or rather this has already been accomplished in the Idea; — a work which is the work of reason, and indeed not so easy as the understanding’s own. — If, therefore, the understanding shows that the Idea contradicts itself, because, e.g., the subjective is only subjective, and the objective rather opposed to it, being is something quite other than the Concept, and therefore cannot be picked out of it, just as the finite is only finite and precisely the opposite of the infinite, hence not identical with it, and so on through all determinations, then

[p. 113]

logic rather points out the opposite, namely that the subjective, which is supposed to be only subjective, the finite, which is supposed to be only finite, the infinite, which is supposed to be only infinite, and so on, has no truth, contradicts itself and passes over into its opposite, whereby this passing-over, the unity in which the extremes are, as sublated, as a shining or as moments, manifests itself as their truth. The understanding that sets about the Idea is the twofold misunderstanding, in that *first* it takes the extremes of the Idea — let them be expressed as they will — insofar as they are *in their unity*, still in the sense as if they were *not* in their concrete unity but *abstractions* outside it; it overlooks, e.g., already the nature of the *copula* in judgment, which asserts of the singular, the subject, that the singular is just as much not singular but universal. — *Secondly* the understanding holds *its reflection*, that the Idea, identical with itself, contains the *negative* of itself, the contradiction, to be an *external* reflection, which does not fall within the Idea itself. — In fact, however, this is not a wisdom peculiar to the understanding, but because the Idea is this negativity, it is itself the dialectic, which eternally separates what is identical with itself from what is different, the subjective from the objective, the finite from the infinite, the soul from the body, and only insofar is eternal creation, eternal vitality, and eternal spirit. Since it is thus itself the passing-over into the *abstract understanding*, it is just as eternally *reason*, as the dialectic which again brings to understanding this differentiated item of the understanding about its nature and the false semblance of the independence of its productions, and leads it back into unity.

[p. 141]

Since this twofold movement is neither temporal nor in any way separated and distinguished, — otherwise it would again be only abstract understanding, — it is the eternal intuiting of itself in the other; the Concept that has carried *itself* out in its objectivity, the Object that is *inner purposiveness*, that is essential subjectivity. — The *different ways* of grasping the Idea, as unity of the ideal and the real, of the *finite and the infinite*, of *identity and difference*, and so forth, are more or less *formal*, in that they designate some stage of the *determinate Concept*. Only the Concept itself is free, and the truly universal; in the Idea, therefore, its determinacy is likewise only itself; an objectivity into which, as the universal, it continues itself, and in which it has only its own, the total determinacy. The Idea is the *infinite judgment*, which is just as absolutely identical as its sides are each the independent totality, and each, precisely thereby, in that it completes itself to this, has passed over into the other. — None of the other determinate Concepts is this totality completed in its two sides, except the *Concept* itself and *objectivity*.

§. 163.

The Idea is essentially *process*, because its identity is only the absolute and free identity of the Concept, insofar as it is absolute negativity and therefore dialectical. It is the course in which the Concept, as the universality that is singularity, determines itself to objectivity, and this externality, which has the Concept as its substance, leads itself back into subjectivity through its immanent dialectic.

[p. 115]

##### a). Life.

§. 164.

The *immediate* Idea is *life*. The Concept is realized as soul in a *body*, of whose externality the former is the immediate, self-relating *universality*, and just as much its *particularity*, so that the body expresses in itself no other differences than the determinations of the Concept; finally, *singularity* is, on the one hand, the dialectic of objectivity, which is led back from the semblance of its independent subsistence into subjectivity, so that all members are *means* for one another, just as they are, as the determinations of the Concept, momentary ends; — on the other hand, life, through the singularity of the Concept, is a *living being*.

§. 165.

The living being has *individuality*, in that its singularity is the subjectivity of the Concept; since this is an inseparable one, but the objective differences have an indifferent externality, the living being is essentially the *process of itself within itself*, and its parts only as passing over.

The relationship of the *whole and the parts* is therefore the most unsuitable for the living being, or if it is considered according to this relationship, it is taken as *dead*, because the parts are such differences as are supposed to have an independent subsistence for themselves. — *Spirit* is likewise a living being, but is just as much considered as dead if *faculties and forces* operating for themselves are assumed in it, which it is supposed to *have*; it is then the *thing of many properties*, a collection of determinations subsisting indifferently toward one another. — Just as unsuitable is it to let the living being *consist* of soul and body.

[p. 116]

The finitude of the living being consists in this, that soul and body are *separable*; this constitutes its mortality; but only insofar as it is dead are those two sides of the Idea different *constituents*.

§. 166.

2). This process is enclosed in the Concept, or in the immediacy of the living being; in the *judgment* of the real Concept, however, the *objective* is likewise an independent totality, and the negative relation of the living being to itself makes the *presupposition* of an inorganic nature standing over against it. Since this negative is just as much a moment of the Concept of the living being itself, it is in this, the at the same time universal, as a *deficiency*. The dialectic by which the Object, as null *in itself*, sublates itself, is the activity of the living being that is certain of itself, which *in this process against an inorganic nature* thereby *maintains itself*, *develops* and *objectifies* itself.

§. 167.

3). Since the living individual, which in its first process is as subject and Concept, has, through its second, assimilated its external objectivity to itself, it is now *in itself genus*, substantial universality, and the judgment of this Concept is relation of the subject to *another subject*, the *difference of sex*.

§. 168.

The process of the *genus* brings the latter to *being-for-itself*. Its product, because life is still the immediate Idea, falls apart into the two sides, in that according to *the one* the living individual, which was at first presupposed as immediate, now as a

[p. 117]

mediated and *generated* thing emerges; in that according to *the other*, however, the living *singularity*, which on account of its first *immediacy* relates itself *negatively* to universality, *goes under* in it, and the Idea thereby, as *free genus*, for itself enters *into existence*: the death of the singular vitality is the *emergence of spirit*.

##### b). Cognition.

§. 169.

The Idea exists freely for itself, insofar as it has universality as the element of *its existence*, or is objectivity itself as the Concept. Singularity, which is sublated in it, *is the pure distinction within* it, and the intuiting that holds itself in this identical universality. But as this singularity of the totality it is the *judgment*, to repel itself from itself as totality, and to *presuppose* itself as an *external universe*.

§. 170.

The relation of these two Ideas, which are identical *in themselves* or as life, is at first the *relative* one, or the *relationship of reflection*, in that the distinction, the *first* judgment, the *presupposing*, is not yet *a positing*, and for the subjective Idea therefore the objective is the *found* immediate world, or the Idea as life in the appearance of *singular existence*.

§. 171.

A). The subjective Idea, as the Idea in the determination of universality, is *for itself* itself and *its other*; it therefore has the *drive* to realize itself as such a unity. But because that *other* which is in it is only the abstraction of the objective world, and this deficiency in it, this world as one that *is*, is for it,

[p. 118]

so this first drive is directed to sublating this its deficiency *within itself*, and to raising the *certainty* of the identity of the objective with it, through the *taking up* of the world that *is into itself*, to *truth*. The realization of this drive is *cognition* as such.

§. 172.

This cognition is *finite*, because it has the presupposition of a *found* world, and thereby its identity with it is not *for it itself*. The truth to which it can come is therefore likewise only *the finite*, not the infinite truth of the Concept; this, as the goal that is *in itself*, is a *beyond* for it. This cognition is therefore the *understanding*, without *reason*; the taking up of the given Object into the *form*, remaining *external* to it, of the Concept.

§. 173.

Finite cognition, 1) since it presupposes the *distinct* as something found, a being standing over against it, — *facts* of external nature or of consciousness, — has for itself only *formal identity* or *abstraction*. Its activity therefore consists in dissolving the given concrete, isolating its differences, and giving them the form of abstract universality; or in leaving the concrete as *ground*, and by abstraction from the particularities that seem unessential, in lifting out a concrete universal, the *genus* or the force and the law. — *Analytic method.*

§. 174.

2). This *universality* is in general at the same time a *determinate* one; its truth is the *Concept*. Because in finite *cognition* it is not in its infinity,

[p. 119]

it is the *determinate Concept*, merely *of the understanding*. The taking up of the object into this form is the *synthetic method*.

§. 175.

α). The object, brought by cognition into the form of the determinate Concept, so that its *genus* and its universal *determinacy* is posited, is the *definition*.

§. 176.

β). The statement of the second moment of the Concept, of the determinacy of a universal as *particularization*, is the *division*.

§. 177.

γ). In the concrete *singularity* the object is a synthetic relation of *distinct* determinations; — a *theorem*. The identity of these is a *mediated* one. The bringing up of the material that constitutes the intermediate terms is the *construction*, and the mediation itself, from which the necessity of that relation for cognition proceeds, the *proof*.

According to the usual statements of the difference between the synthetic and the analytic method, it appears on the whole as arbitrary which of them one wishes to use. If the concrete, which according to the synthetic method is result, is presupposed, then the abstract determinations can be analyzed out of it as *consequences*, which constituted the *presuppositions* and the *material* for the proof. The algebraic *definitions* of curved lines are theorems in the geometrical course; so too would the Pythagorean theorem, adopted as definition of the right-angled triangle, yield the theorems proved earlier in geometry for its

[p. 120]

purpose, by analysis. The arbitrariness of the choice rests on the fact that the one method as much as the other proceeds from something *externally presupposed*. By the nature of the Concept, analyzing is the first, in that it has first to raise the given concrete material into the form of universal abstractions, which can then, and only then, be put ahead as definitions. — That these methods are unusable for philosophical cognition is evident of itself, since they have a first presupposition, and cognition thereby lowers itself to the understanding, and to a progression along formal identity. — In place of the abuse that has been practiced with the formalism of these methods in philosophy and in the sciences, the abuse with the so-called *construction* has stepped in in recent times. After the representation had been put into circulation by Kant that mathematics *constructs its Concepts*, which here means nothing other than that it has *no Concepts* but presents abstract determinations in *sensuous intuitions*, — then the stating of *sensuous* determinations, picked up from *perception*, bypassing the Concept, and the formalism of classifying philosophical and scientific objects in tabular fashion according to a presupposed schema, otherwise by arbitrary will and discretion, — have been called a *construction of Concepts*. There lies thereby at the basis an obscure representation of the *Idea*, of the *unity of the Concept and of objectivity*. But that play of so-called constructing is far from presenting this *unity*, which only the *Concept* as such is. Because geometry, moreover, has to do with the *sensuous, but abstract intuition* of space,

[p. 121]

it can, unhindered, fix simple determinations of the understanding in it, and for that reason it alone has the synthetic method of finite cognition in its perfection. It runs, however, in the end also into *incommensurabilities* and *irrationalities*, where it, if it wants to go further in determining, is driven out beyond the principle of the understanding. (Here too, as is often the case elsewhere, there occurs in the terminology the inversion that what is called *rational* is *what is of the understanding*, but what is *irrational* is rather a beginning and trace of *rationality*). Other sciences, when they come to the limit of their progression of the understanding, help themselves in an easy way; they break off the consistency of it, and take up what they need, often the opposite of what went before, from outside, from representation, opinion, perception, or wherever else it may be. — The blindness of finite cognition lets it cognize neither that in its progression through definitions, divisions, etc. it is led forward by the *necessity of the determinations of the Concept*, nor where it is at its limit, nor, when it has overstepped it, that it finds itself in a field where the determinations of the understanding no longer hold, which it nevertheless still uses in it in a crude manner.

§. 178.

The *necessity* which finite cognition produces in the *proof* is an external one, only for subjective insight, just as the determinacy in the definition is a *mark*, and the *ground of division* some external consideration, — because this cognition in general holds fast to the *formal* Concept in opposition to *the Concept of the matter*. But the

[p. 122]

necessity as such is *in itself* the Concept, and the truth of the formal and external mediation is the mediation of it with itself, the independent subjectivity. The Idea that is cognition therefore passes over from the determinacy *that is outside* to the *inner* one, *immanent to the subject*, — into the *Idea* of *willing*.

§. 179.

B). The subjective Idea, as the Idea determinate in and for itself, is the *good*. Its drive to realize itself has the inverted relationship to the Idea of the *true*, and no longer aims at taking up the Object and determining itself according to it, but rather at determining the found world according to its *end*.

§. 180.

This *willing* has, on the one hand, the certainty of the *nullity* of the presupposed Object, in that in necessity the validity of immediate being has sublated itself; — on the other hand, however, because the sublating of being through cognition is its first and formal negation, and the end of the good is still a subjective Idea, it presupposes the *independence* of the Object.

§. 181.

The finitude of this will is therefore the *contradiction* that in the contradictory determinations of the objective world the end of the good is carried out just as well as not, that it is posited as an unessential one just as much as an essential one, as an actual and at the same time as only a possible one. This contradiction vanishes in that the subjectivity of the end, which is *finite* not in itself and according to its content, — for it is the *good*, — but only through its opposition to objectivity, sublates itself through the activity

[p. 123]

itself, and thereby the presupposition of cognition, the objective as one that *immediately is*, restores itself just as its opposition to the final end of the good vanishes.

§. 182.

The truth of the good, as an end, is therefore the unity of the theoretical and practical Idea, that the good is achieved in and for itself, — that the objective world is in and for itself the Concept. This life, which has returned to itself out of the difference and finitude of cognition and has become identical with the Concept through the activity of the Concept, is the *speculative or absolute Idea*.

##### c). Absolute Idea.

§. 183.

The Idea as unity of the subjective and the objective Idea is the Concept for which the Concept as such is the object, or for which the Object is the Concept; — an Object in which all determinations have come together. This unity is thereby the *absolute* one, *and all truth*, the Idea that thinks itself.

§. 184.

For itself the *absolute Idea* is, because no passing over nor presupposing, and in general no determinacy which would not be fluid and transparent, is in it, the *pure form* that intuits *its content* as itself. It is *content* to itself, insofar as it is the ideal differentiating of itself from itself, and the one of the differentiated is the identity with itself, in which, however, the totality of the form is contained as determination. This content is the *logical*. As *form* nothing remains to it but the *method* of this content.

[p. 124]

§. 185.

The moments of the speculative method are a) the *beginning*, which is *being* or the *immediate*; for itself, on the simple ground that it is the beginning; from the speculative Idea, however, it is now its self-determining, which, as the absolute negativity or movement of the Concept, *judges* and posits itself as the negative of itself. *Being*, which for the beginning as such appears as *position*, is thus rather *negation*. But because it is the negation of the Concept, which in its otherness is absolutely identical with itself and is the certainty of itself, it is the Concept not yet posited as Concept, or the Concept *in itself*. — This being is therefore, as the still indeterminate Concept, just as much the *universal*.

The *beginning* merely in the abstract sense of immediate being is a beginning that is taken from intuition and perception, — the beginning of the *analytic* method of finite cognition; in the abstract sense of universality, it is the beginning of the synthetic method of that cognition. But since the logical is immediately just as much a universal as a being, just as much something presupposed to itself by the Idea as it is immediately the Idea itself, its beginning is just as much a synthetic as an analytic beginning.

§. 186.

b). The *progression* is the *judgment* of the Idea. The immediate universal is in itself not the simple, but the internally differentiated, or precisely its immediacy and universality constitutes its determinacy. In it, therefore, the *negative* of the beginning, or the first in its *determinacy*, is thereby posited; it is *for one*, the *relation* of the differentiated, *positedness*; — *moment of reflection*,

[p. 125]

which, however, as not external but immanent, is *dialectical*. This progression is just as much *analytic*, in that through the immanent reflection only that is posited which is contained in the immediate Concept; — as *synthetic*, in that in this Concept this difference is not yet posited.

§. 187.

This progression is, in being, an *other* and *passing over* into an other, in essence *shining* in the *opposite*, in the *Concept* the differentness of the *singular* from *universality*, which *continues* itself as such into that which is distinguished from it, and *as identity* is with it. In the *Idea* this middle is already the second negation, the negation of the negation, the living soul of the totality.

§. 188.

3). The *end* is this, that the differentiated is posited as that which it is in the Idea. It is in its own self the negative of the first, and as the identity with it, it is the negativity of itself; thereby the unity in which the two firsts are as ideal and as moments, or as sublated. — In the Idea this end is only still the *vanishing* of the *semblance*, as if the beginning were an immediate, and the end a result; — the cognition that the Idea is the One totality.

§. 139.

The method is in this way not an external form, but the soul and the Concept of the content itself, and is distinguished from it only insofar as the *determinations of the Concept*, as content, are also *in their own selves* the totality of the Concept, which, however, shows itself not adequate to such an element and content, and therefore sublates it.

[p. 126]

§. 190.

Since the content now leads itself back to the Idea through the Concept, the latter presents itself as a *systematic* totality, which is only *One* Idea, whose particular moments are partly *in themselves* the same, partly through the dialectic of the Concept produce the simple *being-for-itself* of the Idea. The difference of form or method and content makes itself vanish herein.

§. 191.

The speculative Idea, which is thus *for itself* the Idea, is thereby the infinite *actuality*, which in this absolute freedom does not merely *pass over* into *life*, nor, as finite cognition, lets the same *shine* within itself, but in the absolute truth of itself *resolves* to *release* the moment of its particularity or of the first determining and otherness, the *immediate Idea*, as its reflection, itself as *nature*, freely *out of itself*.

[p. 127]

B.

### The Philosophy of Nature.

§. 192.

Nature has resulted as the Idea in the form of *otherness*. Since in it the *Idea* is as the negative of itself, or is *external* to itself, nature is not merely external relatively to this Idea, but *externality* constitutes the determination in which it is as nature.

§. 193.

In this externality the determinations of the Concept have the semblance of an *indifferent subsistence* and of *individuation* against one another; the Concept therefore is as something inner. Nature therefore shows in its determinate being no freedom, but *necessity* and *contingency*.

Nature, therefore, is not to be deified according to its determinate existence, by which it is precisely nature; nor are sun, moon, animals, plants, etc., to be regarded and cited as works of God in preference to human deeds and events. — Nature is *in itself*, in the Idea, divine; but in the Idea the determinate kind and manner by which it is nature is sublated. As it *is*, its being does not correspond to its Concept; its existing actuality therefore has no *truth*; its abstract essence is the negative, as the ancients grasped matter in general as the *non-ens*. But because, although in such an element, it is nonetheless the exposition of the Idea, one may well admire the wisdom of God in it; but when *Vanini* said that a straw suffices to cognize the being

[p. 128]

of God, then every representation of spirit, the worst of its imaginings, the play of its most contingent whims, every word, is a more excellent ground of cognition for God’s being than any single natural object. In nature the play of forms has not only its unfettered, unbridled contingency, but every shape for itself lacks the Concept of itself. The highest to which nature drives in its determinate being is *life*, but as merely natural Idea this is given over to the irrationality of externality, and individual vitality is in every moment of its existence entangled with a singularity other to it; whereas in every spiritual expression the moment of free universal relation to itself is contained. — Rightly has nature in general been determined as the *fall* of the Idea from itself, because in the element of externality it has with it the determination of inadequacy to itself. — It is a similar misunderstanding when human works of art are ranked below natural things on the ground that for the former the material must be taken from outside, and that they are not alive. — As if the spiritual form did not contain a higher vitality, and were not more worthy of spirit than the natural, and as if, in everything ethical, that too which one may call matter did not belong wholly to spirit alone. — Nature remains, despite all contingency of its existences, faithful to eternal laws; but surely so too does the realm of self-consciousness; — which is already acknowledged in the belief that a providence guides human events; — or should the determinations of this providence in this field be only contingent and irrational? — But if spiritual contingency, the *arbitrary will*

[p. 129]

goes as far as *evil*, is this then still something infinitely higher than the lawful behavior of the stars or than the innocence of the plant?

§. 194.

Nature is to be regarded as a system of stages, of which one proceeds necessarily from the other and is the proximate truth of the one from which it results, but not in such a way that the one would be generated *naturally* from the other, but rather in the inner Idea that constitutes the ground of nature.

It has been a clumsy representation of older and also more recent philosophy of nature to regard the further formation and the transition of one natural form and sphere into a higher as an externally actual production; which, however, in order to make it *clearer*, one has put back into the *darkness* of the past. It is precisely this externality that is peculiar to nature, to let the differences fall apart and to let them step forth as indifferent existences; and the dialectical Concept that leads the stages onward is the inner, which comes forth only in *spirit*. — The formerly so favored *teleological* consideration did indeed take as its basis the relation to the Concept in general, likewise also to *spirit*, but held only to external purposiveness, — (§ 154) and considered spirit in the sense of the finite spirit, entangled in natural ends; because of the shallowness of such finite ends, for which it showed natural things to be *useful*, it has lost its credit for pointing out the wisdom of God. — The consideration of the *usefulness* of natural things has the truth in it that they are not, in and for themselves, an absolute end; this *negativity*, however, is not external to them, but rather

[p. 130]

the immanent moment of their Idea, which brings about their transience and passing over into another existence, but at the same time into a higher Concept.

§. 195.

Nature is *in itself* a living whole; the motion of its Idea through its progression of stages is more precisely this: *to posit* itself as what it is *in itself*; or, what is the same, to go *into itself* out of its immediacy and externality, which is *death*, in order to be as a *living being*, but further also to sublate this determinacy of the Idea, in which it is only life, and to become spirit, which is its truth.

§. 196.

The Idea as nature is 1) as the *universal, ideal being-outside-itself*, as *space* and *time*; 2) as the *real* outside-one-another, the *particular* or *material* determinate being, — *inorganic nature*; 3) as *living* actuality, *organic* nature. The three sciences can therefore be called *mathematics*, *physics* and *physiology*.

#### Part One.

### Mathematics

§. 197.

(1 The first or immediate determination of nature is the abstract *universality of its being-outside-itself*, — the unmediated indifference of it, *space*. It is the entirely ideal *juxtaposition*, because it is the being-outside-itself, and absolutely *continuous*, because this outside-one-another is still wholly abstract and has no determinate difference within itself.

Many things have been put forward about the nature of space from time immemorial. I mention only the *Kantian*

[p. 131]

determination that, like time, it is a *form* of *sensuous intuition*. Otherwise too it has become customary to take as a basis that space must be regarded only as something subjective in representation. If abstraction is made from what belongs to the Kantian Concept, to subjective idealism and its determinations (see § 5, Remark), there remains the correct determination that space is a mere form, i.e., an *abstraction*, and indeed of immediate externality. — To speak of *points of space*, as if they constituted the positive element of space, is untenable, since, on account of its differencelessness, it is only the possibility, not the positedness, of the negative, and therefore absolutely continuous; the point is on that account rather the negation of space. The question concerning its infinity is likewise decided through this. It is in general pure *quantity* (§ 53 f.), but no longer merely the same as a logical determination, but as being immediate and external. — Nature therefore begins not with quality but with quantity, because its determination is not, like logical being, the absolutely first and immediate, but essentially something *mediated*, being-external and being-other.

§. 198.

Space, as Concept in general (and more precisely as the indifferent *being-outside-one-another*), has its *differences* in it, a) immediately in its indifference as the merely *diverse*, wholly undetermined three *dimensions*.

To deduce the necessity that space has precisely three dimensions is not to be demanded of geometry, which is not a philosophical

[p. 132]

science, and may presuppose its object, space. But elsewhere too no thought is given to the demonstration of this necessity. It rests on the nature of the Concept, whose determinations, however, since they present themselves in this first element of outside-one-another, in abstract quantity, are quite merely superficial, and a wholly empty difference. One therefore also cannot say at all how *height*, *length* and *breadth* differ from one another, because they are only *supposed* to be different, but *are* not yet differences. — The *height* has its closer determination as direction toward the center of the earth; but this is no concern of the nature of space for itself; — abstracted from this, it is just as indifferent what one wants to call height or depth, as what one wants to call length or breadth, which one otherwise too often calls depth.

§. 199.

b) But difference is essentially determinate, qualitative difference. As such it is α) first of all the *negation* of space itself, because this is the immediate, differenceless being-outside-itself; the *point*. β) But the negation is, as negation *of space*; this relation of the point to it is the *line*, the first otherness of the point; γ) but the truth of otherness is the negation of the negation. The line therefore passes over into *plane*, which on the one hand is a determinacy over against line and point, and thus plane in general, but on the other hand is the sublated negation of space, hence restoration of spatial totality, which, however, now has the negative moment in it; — *enclosing surface*, which separates off a *single* whole space.

That the line does not consist of points, nor the plane of lines, follows from their Concept

[p. 133]

since the line is the point as, being *outside itself*, relating itself to space, and sublating itself, the plane likewise is the sublated line that is outside itself. — The point has here been represented as the first and positive, and one has proceeded from it. But it is just as much the other way round: insofar as space is the positive, the plane is the first negation, and the line the second, which, however, according to its truth, as self-relating negation, is the point; the necessity of the transition is the same. — The further figurations of space, which geometry considers, are further *qualitative* delimitations of a spatial abstraction, of the plane, or of a delimited whole space. Few moments of necessity occur therein, such as that, e.g., the triangle is the first rectilinear figure, that all others must be traced back to it or to the square if they are to be determined, and the like. — The principle of these drawings is the identity of the understanding, which determines the figurations to regularity, and thereby grounds and puts into them the relationships which it is now the end of the science to cognize. — In passing it may be remarked that it was a peculiar idea of *Kant’s* to assert that the definition *of straight lines*, that they are the shortest path between two points, is a synthetic proposition; for my *Concept* of the *straight* was said to contain nothing of magnitude, but only a quality. — In this sense every definition is a synthetic proposition; the *definitum*, the *straight line*, is at first only the intuition or representation, and the determination that it is the shortest path between two points first makes up the *Concept* (as it namely appears in such definitions, see § 110). That the Concept is not already present in intuition

[p. 134]

constitutes precisely the difference between the two, and the requirement of a definition. That something appears to mere representing as a quality, whose specific character, however, rests on a quantitative determination, is something very simple, and is also the case, e.g., with the right angle, with the degree, etc.

§. 200.

2) The negativity that relates itself as point to space and develops in it its determinations as line and plane is, however, in the sphere of being-outside-itself just as much for itself and appearing as indifferent toward the quiescent juxtaposition; thus posited for itself, it is *time*.

§. 201.

Time, as the negative unity of being-outside-itself, is likewise an absolutely abstract, ideal being, which, in that it *is*, is *not*, and in that it is *not*, *is*.

Time is, like space, a *pure form* of *sensuousness* or of intuiting, — but just as this difference of objectivity and of a consciousness subjective over against it does not concern the former, so it does not concern time either. If these determinations are applied to space and time, the former is abstract objectivity, the latter, however, abstract subjectivity. Time is the same principle as the I = I of pure self-consciousness; but the same, or the simple Concept, still in its complete externality, the intuited, mere *becoming*, pure being-within-itself as absolutely a coming-outside-itself. — Time is just as *continuous* as space, for it is abstract self-relating negativity, and in this abstraction there is as yet no real difference. — In time, one says, everything *comes to be and passes away*, for it is

[p. 135]

precisely the abstraction of coming-to-be and passing-away itself. If abstraction is made from *everything*, namely from the filling of time, and likewise from the filling of space, then empty time remains, like empty space, — i.e., these abstractions of externality are then posited. — But time itself is this *becoming*, this *abstracting that is*, *Chronos*, who gives birth to everything and destroys his births. — But the real is just as much *identical* with time as different from it. Everything that is *temporal* is transient, namely whatever is only *in* time, i.e., whatever is not, like the Concept, pure negativity in its own self, but has this, to be sure, as its universal essence in itself, yet is not absolutely adequate to it, and therefore relates itself to the latter as to its power. Time itself is *eternal*, for it, not any *one* time, nor now, but time as time, is its Concept. This, however, in its identity with itself, I = I, is in and for itself absolute negativity and freedom; time is therefore not its power, nor is it in time and something temporal, but it is rather the power of time, which is only this negativity as externality. — The natural is therefore subject to time insofar as it is finite; the true, by contrast, the Idea, spirit, is *eternal*. The Concept of eternity must therefore not be grasped as if it were the *sublated* time, in any case not in the sense as if eternity came *after* time; in that way eternity would be made into the future, into a moment of time; nor in the sense that time is purely negated and eternity the mere abstraction from it, but rather time in its Concept is, like the Concept itself in general, the eternal, and for that reason also absolute presence.

[p. 136]

§. 202.

The dimensions of time, the *present*, *future* and *past*, are only becoming and its dissolution into the differences of being as the passing over into nothing, and of nothing as the passing over into being. The immediate vanishing of these into singularity is the present as now, which itself is only this vanishing of being into nothing and of nothing into being.

1) The *finite* present differs from the *eternal* in that the former is as *now*, and therefore its abstract moments, as past and future, are distinguished from it as the concrete unity; eternity, however, as the Concept, contains these moments within itself, and its concrete unity is for that reason not the now, because it is the quiescent identity, concrete being as universal, not that which vanishes into nothing, as becoming. — Moreover, in nature, where time is *now*, it does not come to a subsisting difference of those dimensions; they are of necessity only in subjective representation, in recollection, fear or hope. The *abstract* past and future of time, however, is space, just as sublated space is, first of all, the point, and time.

2) The finite *science* of *space*, *geometry*, is not immediately confronted by any such *science of time*, because the differences of time do not have this *indifference* of being-outside-itself, which constitutes the immediate determinacy of space, and are therefore not capable of figurations, as the latter is. The principle of time acquires this capability, however, by being paralyzed, and its negativity being reduced by the understanding to the *one*. Intuition here, as elsewhere, contains the higher, relative

[p. 137]

truth than the understanding merely for itself, because the latter is only abstract, but the former concrete. — This dead one, now, the highest externality of thought, is capable of external combination, and these combinations, the figures of *arithmetic*, are in turn capable of the determination of the understanding according to likeness and unlikeness, of identifying and of distinguishing. The science that has the one as its principle therefore constitutes the counterpart to geometry. — 3) The name *mathematics*, moreover, was previously used also for the philosophical consideration of space and time, because it lies at least nearest to the same, notwithstanding that mathematics considers in these objects only the *determination of magnitude*, and of them also, as remarked, not *time* itself, but only the one in its figurations and connections. — In the *doctrine of motion*, time too indeed becomes an object of this science; but *applied* mathematics is in general no *immanent* science; precisely because it is the application of pure mathematics to a given material and its determinations taken up from experience. —

4) But one could still further conceive the thought of a *philosophical mathematics*, which, namely, would cognize from Concepts what the usual mathematical science of the understanding derives, without Concepts, from its presupposed determinations according to the method of the understanding. But since mathematics is after all the science of finite determinations of magnitude, which are to remain fixed and be valid in their finitude, not to pass over, it is essentially a science of the understanding; and since it has the capability of being this in a perfect manner, the advantage

[p. 138]

that it has over the other sciences of this kind is rather to be preserved for it, and not to be contaminated either by the admixture of the Concept, which is heterogeneous to it, or by that of empirical ends. It always remains open here that the Concept may establish a more thorough consciousness than has hitherto shown itself, both concerning the guiding principles of the understanding and concerning the order, and its necessity, in the arithmetical operations as well as in the propositions of geometry. — If one wanted to treat the figurations of space or of the one philosophically, they would, for the reason given, lose their peculiar significance; a philosophy of them would become something logical, or also something of another concrete philosophical science, according to whether one gave the Concepts a more concrete significance. — It would, however, be a very superfluous and thankless labor to want to use, for the expression of thoughts, such a refractory and inadequate medium as spatial figures and numbers are, and to treat them forcibly for this purpose; to them the determinate Concept would always be something externally attached. The first simple figures and numbers can be employed at will as symbols, which, however, are a subordinate and meager expression for thought. The first attempts of pure thinking resorted to this makeshift; the Pythagorean system of numbers is the famous example of it. But with richer Concepts these means become wholly insufficient, since their external composition and the contingency of the connection in general are inadequate to the nature of the Concept, and make it entirely ambiguous which of the many relations that are possible in more composite numbers and figures

[p. 139]

are to be held fast. Besides, the fluidity of the Concept evaporates in such an external medium, in which every determination falls into the indifferent outside-one-another. That ambiguity could be removed only through explanation. The essential expression of the thought is then that explanation, and that symbolizing a superfluity without import. — Other mathematical determinations, such as the *infinite*, *relationships of the same*, the *infinitely small*, *factors*, *powers*, etc., have their true Concepts in philosophy itself; it is awkward to want to take them from mathematics for it and to borrow them, where they are taken up conceptlessly, indeed often senselessly, and have rather to expect their rectification and meaning from philosophy. — The truly philosophical science of mathematics as *doctrine of magnitude* would be the science of *measures*, but this already presupposes the real particularity of things, which is present only in *concrete* nature.

§. 203.

3) Space and time constitute in and for themselves the Idea, the former its real or immediately objective, the latter its purely subjective side. Space is in itself the contradiction of the indifferent being-asunder and of the differenceless continuity, hence the pure negativity of itself and the passing over into time; — space makes itself into the singularity of *place*. Likewise time, since its opposed moments held together in one immediately sublate themselves, is the immediate collapse into indifference, into the undifferentiated outside-one-another, or space, so that its place, just therein, immediately, as absolutely indifferent to its determinacy, becomes *an other*.

[p. 140]

This *passing-away* and *regeneration* of space into time and of time into space is *motion*; — a becoming, which, however, itself is just as much immediately the *identical, existent* unity of the two, *matter*.

The transition from ideality to reality, from abstraction to concrete determinate being, here from space and time to the reality that appears as *matter*, is incomprehensible for the understanding, and therefore always makes itself external for it, and as something given. The familiar representation is to let space and time, as *empty*, be *filled* from *outside* with matter, and, on the one hand, in this way to assume material things as indifferent to space and time, and on the other hand at the same time as essentially spatial and temporal. — What is usually said of matter is, α) that it is *composite*; — this refers to its identity with space. Insofar as abstraction is made in it from time and in general from all form, it has been asserted of it that it is eternal and unalterable. This follows in fact immediately; but such a matter is also only an untrue abstraction. β) Matter is *impenetrable* and offers *resistance*, is something tangible, visible, etc. These predicates are nothing other than that matter is, on the one hand, for determinate perception, in general *for an other*, but on the other hand just as much *for itself*. Both are determinations which it has precisely as the *identity* of space and time, of the immediate *outside-one-another* and of *negativity* or of becoming. — The *transition* of *ideality* into *reality* occurs, however, also in a more formal manner in the best-known mechanical phenomena, namely that the

[p. 141]

ideality can take the place of reality and conversely; and it is only the usual thoughtlessness of representation and of the understanding that is to blame for the fact that for them their identity does not emerge from this interchangeability of the two. In the *lever*, e.g., *mass* can be put in the place of *distance* and conversely, and a quantum of the ideal moment produces the same effect as the corresponding real. — In the *magnitude of motion*, likewise, *velocity*, the quantitative relationship of space and time, takes the place of the *mass*, and conversely the same real effect comes forth when the latter is increased and the former proportionally diminished. — A brick by itself does not strike a man dead, but produces this effect only through the velocity attained, i.e., the man is struck dead by *space and time*. — It is the determination of reflection of *force* that here, once it is fixed for the understanding and stands there as an ultimate, therefore hinders it and lets it hold it superfluous to inquire further after the *Concept*. But at least this hovers before it thoughtlessly: that the *effect* of the force is something real, evident to the senses, and that *in the force* there is what is in its *expression*, also that the force attains precisely *this force of its expression* through the relationship of the ideal moments, of space and time. — It belongs, further, also to this conceptless reflection to regard the so-called forces of matter as *implanted*, and as originally *external* to it, so that precisely this identity of time and space, which hovers before one in the determination of reflection of *force* and which in truth constitutes the *essence* of matter, is posited as something *alien* to it and *contingent*.

[p. 142]

#### Part Two.

### The Physics.

§. 204.

Matter holds itself asunder in itself through the moment of its negativity, diversity or abstract *individuation*; it has *repulsion*. Its outside-one-another, however, is just as essential, because these diverse ones are one and the same, the negative unity of this being-for-itself that is outside-one-another, — hence continuous. Matter therefore has *attraction*. The unity of these moments is *gravity*.

Kant has, among other things, also the merit of having made, by his attempt at a so-called *construction* of matter, in his *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science*, the beginning of a *Concept* of matter, after it had previously lain at the basis only as a dead thing of the understanding, and its determinations had been grasped in the relationship of *properties*. — With this attempt Kant awakened the Concept of *philosophy of nature*, which is nothing other than the comprehending of nature, or, what is the same, the cognition of the Concept in nature. In this, however, he accepted the determinations of reflection of *attractive force* and *repulsive force* as ready-made, and again, in determining these, out of which matter was to proceed, presupposed it as something ready-made, — a confusion that is a necessary consequence, because those abstract moments cannot be comprehended without their identity, or rather because the consideration of these opposed determinations dissolves immediately into their identity. I have presented more fully the confusion prevailing in this Kantian

[p. 143]

exposition, in my System of Logic, vol. 1, part 1, p. 119 ff.

§. 205.

Matter, as *heavy*, is at first only 1) the matter *that is within itself*, or *universal* matter; it is the object of *mechanics*. But this Concept must 2) *specify* itself; thus it is *elemental* matter, and object of *elemental physics*. 3) Particular matter, gathering itself together into *singularity*, is *individualized* matter, and object of the physics of the *proper world of bodies*.

A.

#### Mechanics,

§. 206.

Matter has at first, as merely universal, only a quantitative difference, and particularizes itself into different quanta, — *masses*, which in the superficial determination of a whole or one are *bodies*.

§. 207.

The body is 1) as *heavy* matter the solid identity of space and time, but 2) as the first negation it has in it their ideality, *distinguished* from one another and from it, and is *essentially in* space and *in* time, whose *content*, indifferent to this form, it constitutes.

§. 208.

3) The body is, as the space in which time is sublated, *enduring*, and 4) as the time in which the indifferent subsistence of space is sublated, *transient*, and in general a wholly *contingent*

[p. 144]

one, 5) but it is the unity binding the two in their opposition; thus it essentially has *motion*, the appearance of gravity.

Just as the forces have been regarded as merely *implanted* in matter, so it is in particular also *motion* that, even in the physics that is supposed to be scientific, is considered as a determination *external* to the body; so that it is a main axiom of mechanics that the body is absolutely to be put into motion, as into a *state*, only by an *external cause*. On the one hand it is the understanding that holds motion and rest apart as conceptless determinations, and therefore does not grasp their passing over into one another; on the other hand, only the *selfless* bodies of the earth, which are the object of ordinary mechanics, hover before representation. The determinations that occur and hold in the appearance of such bodies are taken as a basis, and the nature of the independent bodies is subsumed under them; whereas in truth the latter are rather the universal ones and the former the absolutely subsumed ones, and in absolute mechanics the Concept presents itself in its truth and in its own peculiar way.

§. 209.

In motion time posits itself spatially as place, but this indifferent spatiality becomes just as immediately *temporal*; the place becomes *an other* (§ 202). This difference of time and space is, as the difference of their absolute unity, of the indifferent *content*, a difference of bodies, which hold themselves apart from one another and just as much seek their unity through their gravity; — universal *gravitation*.

[p. 145]

§. 210.

Gravitation is the true and determinate *Concept* of material corporeality, which is thereby just as essentially divided into *particular* bodies, and has its appearing determinate being, the moment of external *singularity*, in *motion*, which is thereby immediately determined as a relation of *several bodies*.

Universal gravitation must be acknowledged for itself as a profound thought, which constitutes an absolute foundation for mechanics, even though, grasped at first in the sphere of reflection, it drew attention and confidence to itself chiefly through the quantitative determination connected with it, and was to find its confirmation only in the *experience* followed up from the solar system down to the phenomenon of capillary tubes. — Moreover, gravitation immediately contradicts the law of inertia, for by virtue of the former matter strives *out of itself* toward the other. — In the *Concept* of *gravity*, as shown, the two moments themselves, of being-for-itself and of the continuity that sublates being-for-itself, are contained. These moments of the Concept now undergo the fate of being grasped as particular forces, corresponding to attractive and repulsive force, in closer determination as *centripetal* and *centrifugal force*, which, like gravity, are to *act on the bodies*, independently of one another and contingently to collide in a third, the body. Thereby what is profound in the thought of gravity is again reduced to nothing, and so long can Concept and reason not penetrate into the doctrine of absolute motion, as the so highly praised discoveries of *forces* prevail in it.

[p. 146]

— If one attentively follows the quantitative determinations that have been found concerning laws of these two forces, the confusion that arises from that separation soon reveals itself. It becomes still greater, however, when they are mentioned in relation to *gravity*; gravitation, which is also called *attraction*, then appears as the same as the centripetal force, the law for this single force as a law of the whole of gravitation, and the centrifugal force, which at another time again counts as absolutely essential, as something quite superfluous. — In the above syllogism, which contains the immediate *Idea* of gravity, namely gravity itself as the Concept that, through the particularity of the bodies, enters into the external reality of motion, the rational identity and inseparability of these three moments is contained. — The relativity of motion also shows itself in it, in that motion has a meaning absolutely only in the system of *several* bodies standing in relationship to one another, and indeed according to different determination; which different determination will emerge immediately.

§. 211.

The particular bodies, in which gravity is realized, have as determinations of their differentiated nature the moments of their Concept. One of them, then, is the *universal* center of *being-within-itself*. Over against this extreme stands the centerless *singularity* that is outside itself. The *particular* ones, however, are others, which stand in the determination of being-outside-itself and, as at the same time being-within-themselves, are also centers for themselves, and relate themselves to the first as to their essential unity; — a relation that is not a relation of mass to mass, but is qualitative.

[p. 147]

§. 212.

1) The motion of the bodies of relative centrality, in relation to the body of the abstract, universal one, is the *absolutely free motion*, and the syllogism of this system is that the universal central body is concluded together, through the relative ones, with dependent corporeality.

The laws of absolutely free motion were, as is well known, discovered by *Kepler*; — a discovery of immortal fame. *Kepler* has *proved* it in the sense that he found for the empirical data their universal expression. (§ 145) It has since become a common saying that *Newton* first found the proofs of those laws. Hardly ever has a fame passed more unjustly from a first discoverer to another. I merely call attention here to the fact that, at bottom, it is already conceded by mathematicians 1) that the Newtonian formulas can be derived from the Keplerian laws, 2) that the Newtonian proof of the proposition that a body subject to the law of gravitation moves in an *ellipse* around the central body applies to a *conic section* in general; whereas the main proposition that was to be proved consists precisely in this, that the orbit of such a body is *not a circle or any other conic section*, but *alone the ellipse*. The conditions that make the orbit of the body a determinate conic section are traced back to an *empirical* circumstance, namely a particular position of the body at a determinate point in time, and the *contingent* strength of an *impact* that it is supposed originally to have received. 3) that the Newtonian law of the so-called force of

[p. 148]

gravity is likewise shown only from experience through induction. — On closer consideration it turns out that what Kepler expressed in a simple and sublime manner, in the form of *laws of the heavenly motion*, Newton has transformed into the *conceptless reflective form* of *force of gravity*. The whole manner of this so-called proving presents, in general, a confused web, of *lines* of the merely geometrical construction, to which a physical meaning of *independent forces* is given, and of empty concepts of the understanding of an *accelerating force*, of *time-particles*, at the beginning of which those always act anew, of a *force of inertia*, which is to continue their previous effect, etc. — A rational proof concerning the quantitative determinations of free motion can rest solely on the *determinations of the Concept* of space and time, of the moments whose *relationship* is motion.

§. 213.

2) The absolute relationship of the *dependent* bodies, which are merely the extreme of the being-outside-itself of gravity and therefore lack a centrality of their own, to their relative central bodies, is the *resting* of their gravity in them, which, because of the corporeal being-outside-one-another, is only a striving and therefore a *pressure*, directed toward their center lying outside them.

§. 214.

The separation from the immediate connection in which such a body rests is a contingent state, which it, when the external obstacle to its connection has been lifted, sublates through the *fall*

[p. 149]

— a *relatively free* motion, inasmuch as that removal does not belong to the body as a dependent one, but the motion, when the obstacle is removed, is *immanent* to it and an appearance of its own gravity. This motion passes over of itself into rest.

The *attractive force* of the sun, e.g., toward the planets, or of the earth toward the bodies belonging to it and independent, brings with it the skewed representation, as if it were an activity indwelling in the central body, and the bodies located in its sphere behaved only *passively* and *externally* toward it. — So too absolute motion, through the application of determinations of ordinary mechanics, is represented as the dead *conflict* of an independent tangential force and an equally independent force proceeding from the center, by which the body would be pulled passively hither and thither. — The Galilean law of *fall*, that the spaces traversed are related as the *squares* of the elapsed times, shows, in contrast with the abstract uniform velocity of dead mechanism, where the spaces are proportional to the times, the *becoming free* of the *determinations of the Concept* of time and of space, of which the former, as the negative moment or principle of the one, has the determination of the *root*, the latter, however, as being-outside-one-another, and indeed a coming-outside-itself without any other determinacy than that of the root, the determination of the *square*. In this law both moments still remain in this relationship, because the *freedom* of motion in *fall*, since it is *also conditioned*, is only formal; in *absolute motion*, on the other hand, the *relationship* is in its totality, since it is the motion returning into itself. Because the

[p. 150]

law is essentially a relationship, space and time are held in their original difference; the dimensionless time therefore comes only to the formal identity with itself, space, on the other hand, as the positive being-outside-one-another, to the dimension of the Concept. The Keplerian law is therefore a relationship of the *cube of the distances* to the *squares* of the times; — a law that is so great for this very reason, because it so simply and immediately presents the *reason of the matter*, whereas the Newtonian formula, by which it is transformed into a law for the *force of gravity*, *shows* only the distortion and inversion of the *reflection* that stops halfway.

§. 215.

3) In the extreme of the dependent bodies, the universal gravitation which they have toward one another as matters is subject to that which they have toward their common central body. Toward one another, therefore, their motion is external and contingent to them; the cause of it is an *impact* and *pressure*. In this *ordinary mechanical motion* the magnitude of the mass, which has no significance in the fall, and the resistance which it offers through a particular constitution, are a determining moment. Because it contradicts the essential relationship of the dependent body, namely that to its central body, it sublates itself through itself into *rest*. This necessity of the Concept, however, appears in this sphere of externality as an external obstacle or as friction.

The *law of inertia* is taken first of all from the nature of the motion of dependent bodies, to which *motion*, since they do not have the difference of gravity in themselves, is external.

[p. 151]

But precisely for this reason rest is immanent to them, namely the identity with the center lying outside them. Their motion therefore passes essentially into rest, but not into absolute rest, but into pressure, as striving toward their center, which striving, if it is to be regarded as a *striving moving*, is at least the alteration of that external motion into this striving, which constitutes the nature of the body. — The single obstacle or the universal one, friction, is indeed an external one, but a *necessary* one; it is the appearance of that transition posited by the Concept of the dependent body. — Precisely this also takes place with regard to the *pendulum*, of whose motion it is customary to say that it would continue without ceasing if friction could be removed. — Taken by itself, the law of inertia expresses nothing but the understanding's holding fast to the abstractions of rest and motion, namely that rest is only rest, motion only motion; the transition of these abstractions into one another, which is the Concept, is for the understanding something external. — This law of *inertia*, the *impact*, the *attracting* and other determinations have been transferred inadmissibly from ordinary mechanics into the absolute, in which motion is rather in its free Concept.

§. 216.

The difference between central bodies and dependent ones is in the being-within-itself of gravity itself, whose identical nature is its existence. The dependent one, as the being-outside-itself of the self-identical gravity, has the beginning of real difference; it has only a negative center of gravity, a *point of gravity* of its *mass*;

[p. 152]

the determinacy of its motion is not in and for itself, but recedes into a *factor*, to which the mass is the other, so that their magnitude can be interchanged and the motion remains the same.

§. 217.

This *externality* of being-determined makes up the peculiar determinacy of matter; thereby it does not stop at the quantitative difference, but this is essentially more qualitative, so that the determinacy of matter makes up its being.

The empty abstraction of a formless matter admits a merely quantitative difference of it, and regards the further determinacy as a form not essential to it. Already the attractive and repulsive forces are supposed to *act externally* upon it. Since it is the *Concept* positing itself outside itself, it is so *identical* with the determinate form that this makes up its peculiar nature.

B.

#### Elemental Physics.

§. 218.

Gravity, as the *essence* of matter that is within itself, merely inner identity, passes over, since its Concept is the essential *externality*, into the *manifestation* of the essence. As such it is the *totality* of the determinations of reflection, but the same thrown asunder, each appearing as a *particularly qualified* matter, which, as not yet determined to *singularity*, is a shapeless *element*.
