For the determination of an element, *chemical simplicity* has in recent times been arbitrarily assumed, which has nothing to do with the Concept

[p. 153]

of a *physical element*, which is still a real matter, a totality of it that is in itself.

##### a). The Elemental Bodies

§. 219.

1) The first elemental matter is its *pure identity*, not as inner, but as *existent in determinate being*, i.e., the relation to itself determined as *independent* against the other determinations of the totality. This existing *self* of matter is *light*.

§. 220.

As the abstract *self* of matter, light is the *absolutely light*, and, as matter, *infinite*, but, as material ideality, *inseparable* and *simple being-outside-itself*.

In the Oriental intuition, the substantial unity of the spiritual and the natural, the pure selfhood of consciousness, thinking identical with itself as the abstraction of the *true and good*, is one with light. — If the representation that has been called *realistic* denies that ideality is *present* in nature, it is to be referred only to light, to this pure manifesting, which is nothing but *manifesting*. — Heavy matter is *separable* into masses, because it is the *concrete* identity, quantity; but in the quite *abstract* ideality of light there is no such difference; a restriction of it in its infinite spreading does not sublate its absolute connection. The representation of discrete simple *rays of light* and *particles*, and

[p. 154]

*bundles* of the same, of which a light restricted in its spreading is supposed to consist, belongs to the remaining barbarism of concepts that has become dominant in physics especially since *Newton*. — The inseparability of light, in its infinite extension, a real being-outside-one-another that remains identical with itself, can least of all be declared *incomprehensible* by the understanding, since its own principle is rather this abstract identity. — If astronomers have come to speak of heavenly appearances which, while they are being perceived by us, are said to have already occurred 500 years ago and more, one can in this, on the one hand, believe empirical appearances of the *propagation of light*, which hold in one sphere, to be transferred to another, where they have no significance, but on the other hand see a past become a present in the ideal manner of recollection. — According to the representation that from *each point* of a visible surface rays are sent out *in all directions*, and hence a *material hemisphere* of infinite dimension is formed from each, all these infinitely many hemispheres interpenetrate. Instead, however, of a condensed, confused mass having to arise through this between the eye and the object, and of the visibility to be explained having to produce, by virtue of this explanation, invisibility rather, this whole representation itself reduces itself rather to nullity, just as the representation of a concrete body that is supposed to consist of many matters, so that the others are located in the pores of the one, in which, conversely, the others themselves are lodged and circulate, — sublates, through this all-sided interpenetration, the assumption of the discrete materiality of the stuffs, and rather establishes a

[p. 155]

wholly ideal relationship. — The *self-like* nature of light, insofar as the natural things are enlivened by it, *individualized* and their opening-up is strengthened and held together, first comes to light in the individualization of matter, in that the at first abstract identity is only as the return and sublation of particularity, as the negative unity of singularity.

§. 221.

Light relates itself, as universal identity, first of all in this determination of *diversity* or of the understanding of the moments of the totality, to concrete matter as something external and other, as to a *dark*; this contact and external *clouding* of the one by the other is *color*.

According to the well-known *Newtonian* theory, white, i.e., colorless light, *consists* of *five* or of *seven* colors; — for the theory itself does not know this exactly. — On the *barbarism*, first of all, of the representation that with light too recourse has been had to the worst form of reflection, *composition*, and that the *bright* is here supposed even to consist of seven *darknesses*, just as one could let clear water consist of seven earths, one can *not* express oneself strongly *enough*; as well as, further, on the *clumsiness*, *insipidity*, indeed even *dishonesty* of the Newtonian observing and experimenting; — then on the equally bad constitution of the *concluding*, *inferring* and *proving* from those impure empirical data; — further on the *blindness* of the *parroting* now carried on for nearly a century and a half, as well as on the *ignorance* of these parrots who defend that *simple-minded* representation,

[p. 156]

finally, in particular, about the thoughtlessness with which a number of the immediate consequences of that theory (e.g., the impossibility of achromatic telescopes) have been given up and yet the theory itself is maintained; — lastly, about the blindness of the *prejudice* that it rests on something *mathematical*, and as if the *measurements*, in part themselves false and one-sided, as well as the quantitative determinations brought into the inferences, furnished any ground for the theory and the nature of the matter itself. — A principal reason why the *Goethean* illumination of this darkness in light, as clear as it is *thorough* and *learned*, has not met with a more effective reception is, without doubt, this: because the thoughtlessness and simple-mindedness, which one ought to admit, are altogether too great. — Instead of these absurd representations having diminished, they have in the most recent times, following *Malus's* discoveries, been further increased by a *polarization* of light, and even by the *quadrangularity* of the sun's rays, by a *left-rotating motion* of the red and a *right-rotating* one of the blue rays. Such *instances of simple-mindedness* justify themselves by the privilege of physics to the so-called *hypotheses*; but one permits oneself no instances of simple-mindedness in jest, much less should one put forward such things for hypotheses that are not even supposed to be a jest.

§. 222.

Light makes up the *determinate being* or the *physical* meaning of the body of abstract centrality, which as *light-body* is real.

[p. 157]

§. 223.

This abstract identity has its *real* opposition outside it; as an elemental moment of reflection it falls apart within itself, and is as a *duality*, α) of corporeal *diversity*, of material being-for-itself, of *rigidity*, β) of *counter-positing* as such, which, however, as free and not held by individuality, merely collapsed within itself, is *dissolution* and *neutrality*; the former the *lunar*, the latter the *cometary* body.

These two bodies also have, in the system of gravity, as *relative central bodies*, their more determinate meaning, which has the same Concept at its ground as their physical one does, and which can be noted here. — They do not rotate about their axis. The *body of rigidity*, as of formal being-for-itself, which is the independence involved in opposition and therefore not individuality, is for this reason *serving* and the *satellite* of another, in which it has its *axis*. The *body of dissolution*, the opposite of rigidity, is in its behavior *erratic*, and, in its eccentric orbit as in its physical determinate being, presents contingency; so that it has been conjectured of these bodies that the proximity of a great planet could alter their orbit; — they show themselves as a superficial concretion, which may just as contingently disperse again. — The *moon* has no atmosphere, and thereby lacks the meteorological process. It shows only high mountains and craters, and the inflammation of this rigidity within itself; the shape of a crystal, which *Heim* (one of the few ingenious geognosts) has pointed out as the original one of the merely rigid earth. — The *comet* appears

[p. 158]

as a formal process, a restless mass of vapor; none has shown anything rigid, a *nucleus*. Against the representation of the ancients, that comets are mere meteors, astronomers in the most recent times, however, no longer act so aloof and superior as formerly. As yet only the return of a single one has been observed; others were expected according to calculation, but did not come. — The conjecture put forward by astronomers also points to this, that they could in time give up the hitherto formal view of the appearance of the comets, contingent in relation to the whole of the system and emerging athwart and across it, and grasp the thought that the other bodies of the same *defend* themselves against them, i.e., behave as necessary organic moments, so that they would also put themselves in a position to offer better grounds of consolation than hitherto against the dangers feared from the comet. —

§. 224.

3) The opposition gone back into itself is the *earth* or the *planet* in general, the body of *individual* totality, in which rigidity is *opened up* into separation into real differences, and this dissolution is held together by the *self-like point of unity*.

One is accustomed to hold the sun and the stars to be more excellent natures than the planets, because the first elevation of reflection above sensuous perception takes the *abstract* for the highest as against the singular, which is not yet comprehended. — The name *wandering stars* has arisen for the bodies of individuality from the immediate view of their motion; in and for itself, however, this is, as axial rotation about itself and at the same time motion

[p. 159]

around a central body the most concrete, expression of vitality, and for that reason just as much more excellent than the rest of the center of the system as than the serving and erratic motion of the lunar and cometary bodies. Likewise the light-nature of the central body is the *abstract* identity, whose truth, as that of thinking, is in the concrete Idea, in individuality. — As for the series of the planets, astronomy has discovered, concerning their nearest determinacy, the *distance*, as yet no actual law, much less anything rational. What I attempted on this in an earlier dissertation I can no longer regard as satisfactory. — Likewise the attempts in the philosophy of nature to show the rationality of the series in the physical constitution can so far be regarded only as beginnings of finding the points of view that matter. — What is irrational is to lay the thought of contingency at the ground of this, and, e.g., in *Kepler's* thought of grasping the arrangement of the solar system according to the laws of musical harmony, to see only an *aberration of the imagination*, and not to esteem highly the deep belief that *reason is in this system*; — a belief that was the sole ground of his brilliant discoveries. The wholly clumsy and erroneous application of the numerical relationships of tones which *Newton* made to *colors* has, on the other hand, retained renown and credence.

##### b). The Elements.

§. 225.

The body of individuality has the determinations of the elemental totality, which are immediately free

[p. 160]

self-subsisting bodies, as subjected moments in it; thus they constitute its universal *physical elements*.

§. 226.

1) The element of undifferentiated simplicity is no longer the positive identity with itself, the self-manifestation, which *light* as such is; this makes up the *own*, inner self of the individual body, but it is only *negative universality*, as the selfless moment of *another*. This identity is therefore the unsuspected but creeping and consuming power of the individual and organic process; the all-receiving and transparent, but just as much elastic, all-penetrating fluidity, *air*.

§. 227.

2) The elements of opposition are α) being-for-itself, but not the *indifferent* one of rigidity, but that posited in individuality as a moment and hence the material selfhood, light as identical with heat, *fire*. It is the materialized *time*, the absolutely restless and consuming, into which the self-consumption of the subsisting body just as much turns over as, conversely, coming externally to it, it destroys it, — a consuming that just as much consumes itself.

§. 228.

β) The other element is the neutral, the opposition gone together within itself, which, however, without singularity, hence without rigidity and determination within itself, a thoroughgoing equilibrium, dissolves every determinacy mechanically posited in it, receives limitedness of shape only from outside, and, without the restlessness of the process in itself, is absolutely the possibility of the same and the dissolvability; *water*.

[p. 161]

§. 229.

3) The element, however, of developed difference and of the individual determination of the same, is the at first still indeterminate *earthiness*, in general.

##### c). The Elemental Process.

§. 230.

The individual identity, under which the differing elements, and their diversity against one another and against their unity, are bound, is a dialectic that makes up the physical life of the earth, the *meteorological process*; the elements, as dependent moments, have in it their subsistence only to the same extent as they are produced in it.

Just as the determinations of ordinary mechanics and of the independent bodies are applied to absolute mechanics and the free central bodies, so the finite physics of the individuated individual bodies is taken for the same as the free, independent physics of the earth-process is. It is held to be precisely the triumph of science to recognize again and demonstrate in the universal process of the earth the same determinations that show themselves in the external and dependent processes of individuated physical corporeality. The demonstration of this likeness is effected by the determinations being made, through abstraction from their peculiar differences and conditions, into superficial universalities, such as attraction, — forces and laws, in which the particular, the concrete Concept and the conditions are lacking, and were then partly regarded as an external stuff entering in, partly also, by analogy, added by invention.

[p. 162]

— A principal difference is made by the fixed representation of the substantial, unalterable *diversity* of the elements, which has once been fixed by the understanding from the processes of the individuated stuffs; where higher transitions show themselves in these, e.g., where in the crystal water becomes solid, light, heat disappears, etc., the obstinacy of formal thinking against the Concept provides itself with an aid through nebulous determinations, in part through merely senseless expressions, of *dissolution*, *becoming bound and latent*, and the like. To this belongs essentially the transformation of all relationships in the appearances into *stuffs* and *matters*, in part *imponderable*, whereby every physical determinate being is made into the already mentioned *chaos* of matters and their going out and in in the invented pores of each other, and not only all Concept but also representation gives out.

§. 231.

The process of the earth is continually kindled by its universal *self*, the activity of *light*, its original relationship to the sun. The *one moment* of this process is the *diremption* of its substantial identity, the tension into the moments of independent opposition, into rigidity and selfless neutrality, whereby the earth goes toward dissolution, to become, on the one hand, a crystal, a moon, on the other a body of water, a comet, and the moments seek to realize their connection with their independent roots.

§. 232.

The *other moment* is that the being-for-itself, toward which the moments of counter-positing go,

[p. 163]

sublates itself as negativity driven to the extreme; — the *self-kindling consumption* of the attempted differentiated subsistence of the moments, whereby their substantial identity restores itself, and the earth has become, for itself, *fruitful individuality*.

The *thunderstorm* is the complete appearance of this process; the other meteorological phenomena are beginnings or moments and immature realizations of it. In it, however, physics, since it confines its view to the conditions of the external process, has been able to come to terms neither with the formation of rain, (notwithstanding *de Luc's* observations and the consequences drawn from them by him, and urged among the Germans against the theory of dissolution by the ingenious *Lichtenberg*, and at least kept in memory), nor with lightning, nor with thunder either; just as little with other meteorological phenomena, in particular the *atmospherilia*, in which the process proceeds as far as the beginning of a terrestrial nucleus.

§. 233.

The *Concept* of matter, gravity, lays out its moments in the elemental nature, at first in the shape of independent realities. The earth, at first the *abstract* ground of individuality, posits itself in its process as the negative unity of the abstract elements going asunder, and hence as *real* ground and *actuality* of individualization, in which the elements now present themselves as united together in a concrete point of unity.

[p. 164]

C.

#### Individual Physics.

§. 234.

The individual body is the matter that has been concluded together to *singularity* out of the *universality* of gravity through the *particularity* of the elements. Thus it is now determinate in and for itself, and has through singularity a peculiar *form*, which makes up the *unity* of the particularization of a body. — This individuality is a) immediate or resting, *shape*; b) its particularization into the diversity of properties and into the tension of the differences; c) process, in which the shape dissolves itself just as much as it comes forth in its being-determinate in and for itself.

##### a). The Shape.

§. 235.

The individuality of matter in its immediate determinate being is the *immanent form*, which gives to the matter of the body, which at first has only a superficial one, and then *one* particular determinacy as its essence, its own determinate difference; — the *shape*, the specific manner of the *inner cohesion* of the matter and of its *external* delimitation in space; — the individuality of *mechanism*.

The specification of matter into an *element* is still shapeless, because it is as yet only particularity. — With the form of shape, and of individuality in general, the representation of an *externally mechanical* manner and of *composition* is above all to be removed. It is of no help to call to one's aid the external division and an external attaching of the parts for the understanding of the determinacy

[p. 165]

of shape; what is essential always remains the peculiar distinction that comes to light in these parts, and makes up a determinate *self-like* unity of their relation.

§. 236.

The abstract specification is the *specific gravity or density* of matter, the relationship of the weight of the mass to the volume, whereby the material selfhood tears itself loose from the abstract universal relationship to the central body, and ceases to be the uniform filling of space, and opposes to its abstract being-outside-one-another a specific being-within-itself.

The varying density of matter is, as is well known, explained by the assumption of *pores*; — to *explain* means in general to trace an appearance back to assumed, familiar *determinations of the understanding*, and with none is the representation more familiar than with *composition*, *parts* and their *smallness*, and *emptiness*, and hence nothing is clearer than to use, for the comprehension of the condensation of matter, the invention of *pores*, — of empty interspaces, of which physics speaks as of something present, but which it does not show, notwithstanding that it at the same time professes to rely solely on experience and observation. What goes beyond these and is only *assumed* is a matter of *thinking*. It does not occur to physics, however, that it has thoughts; which is true in a double sense, and here in the third, that the pores are mere *inventions*. — An immediate example of a peculiar specification of gravity is the appearance that an iron bar poised in equilibrium on its point of support,

[p. 166]

as it is *magnetized*, loses its equilibrium and now shows itself heavier at the one pole than at the other. — The propositions that physics presupposes in its way of representing density are: 1) that an equal number of equally large material parts are equally heavy; — thus one remains stuck at the formal identity of gravity. 2) The measure of the number of the parts is the weight, but 3) also space, so that what is of equal weight also takes up equal space; if therefore 4) equal weights nevertheless appear in a different volume, then, by the assumption of pores, the equality of the space that is filled is maintained. — Kant has already opposed *intensity* to the quantitative determination of *number*, and put in the place of *more* parts in equal space the equal number but of a stronger *degree of space-filling*, and thereby given the origin to a so-called *dynamical physics*. — At least the determination of the *intensive* quantum would have as much right as that of the *extensive*; but this difference (§ 56) is empty and null in itself. The *intensive* determination of magnitude, however, has this advantage here, that it points to measure and indicates, first of all, a *being-within-itself* which, in its determination of the Concept, is *immanent form-determinacy*, which is there as quantum in general; whose differences, however, as extensive or intensive, — and dynamical physics goes no further, — express no reality.

§. 237.

Density is as yet only a *simple* determinacy. The simple determinacy, however, is essentially as the *one* of corporeality, but here absolutely as immanent

[p. 167]

form-determinacy. Thus it constitutes the principle of *brittleness*, of the configuration that keeps itself in punctuality.

The small particles mentioned earlier, molecules of matter, are an external determination of reflection; the real significance of the determination of the one is that it is immanent form of configuration.

§. 236.

The brittle, as the *subjective* that is for itself, must however open itself up to the difference of the Concept, the point pass over into the line and, on it, oppose itself in extremes, which are held by the middle, their point of indifference. This syllogism constitutes the principle of *configuration* in its developed determinacy, and in this strictness is *magnetism*.

Magnetism is one of the determinations that had to present themselves above all when thought surmised itself in determinate nature, and conceived the idea of a *philosophy of nature*. For the magnet presents, in a simple, naive manner, the nature of the Concept. The poles are not particular things; they do not have the sensuous, mechanical reality, but an ideal one; the point of indifference, in which they have their substance, is the unity in which they are only as determinations of the Concept, and polarity is an opposition only of such moments. The appearances that allow magnetism to be regarded as something merely particular are only ever the same determinations again and again, not different properties that could give data for a description. — That the single magnetic needle directs itself toward the north and thereby, at one and the same time, toward the south, is appearance of the universal *earth-magnetism*; the same, that of

[p. 168]

two such empirical magnets the like-named poles repel one another and the unlike-named attract one another; which is precisely magnetism itself, that the like, the indifferent, dirempts itself and opposes itself into extremes, and the unlike, the different, posits its indifference; one has even called the *unlike-named* poles *friendly*, the *like-named*, however, *hostile*. — That all bodies, however, are magnetic has a skewed double meaning; the correct one is that every real shape, not merely brittle shape, contains this Concept; the incorrect one, however, that all bodies also have in themselves this principle in its strict abstraction, as it is as magnetism. To want to show a form of the Concept as present in nature by claiming that it should exist universally in the determinacy in which it is as an abstraction would be an unphilosophical thought. Nature is rather the Idea in the element of being-outside-one-another, so that it, just as the understanding does, holds the moments of the Concept fast *scattered* and presents them in reality, but in the higher organic things unites the highest *concretion* of the different forms of the Concept in one.

§. 239.

Over against magnetism, which as linear spatiality and ideal opposition of the extremes is the abstract Concept of shape, stands the abstract totality of the same, the *sphere*, the shape of real shapelessness, of fluid indeterminacy and of the indifferent displaceability of the parts.

§. 240.

Between the two properly shapeless extremes, punctual brittleness and the fluidity that forms itself into a sphere, falls the *real shape* in the unification

[p. 169]

of those extremes, the magnetism that has passed over into total corporeality, *cohesion*.

§. 241.

Common cohesion concerns merely the single moment of the *quantitative strength* of the coherence of the parts of a body. Concrete cohesion is immanent form and determinacy of this coherence, and comprises the external *crystallizations* and the *fracture shapes or core shapes*, the internal crystallization that shows itself going right through.

§. 242.

Through external crystallization the individual body is closed off as a *singular* against *others*, and is capable of a *mechanical process* with them. As internally formed, it specifies this process against its behavior as a mere universal mass; in *elasticity*, hardness, softness, toughness, ductility, shatterability, it thus obtains its individual determinacy in the resistance to external force.

§. 243.

But just as density is the *simple* determinacy only at first, as the relationship of volume to mass, so cohesion is this *simplicity* as the *self-like* of individuality. The self-preservation of the body in the shaking by mechanical force is therefore also an emergence of its individual pure *ideality*, of its peculiar motion within itself through its whole cohesion; the specific determination of its ideal being-asunder within itself through its self-like time. This trembling, which the body lets appear as its own and indeed *specified ideality*, in face of real force and external alteration, is sound.

[p. 170]

What lacks cohesion, the brittle and the fluid, is soundless, and in its resistance, which is only an external shaking, makes only a *noise*.

§. 244.

This individuality, since it is here as yet only the immediate one, can be sublated by mechanical force. *Friction*, which brings the difference of corporeality, held outside one another by cohesion, together into the negativity of *one moment of time*, makes break forth a beginning or self-completing self-consumption of the body, which presents its *specific* nature, in the relationship of the *internal* alteration and sublation of its cohesion, through *heat capacity*.

##### b). The Particularization of the Differences.

§. 245.

Configuration, the individualization of mechanism or of gravity, passes over into elemental particularization. The individual body has the totality of the elements in it; as subject of them it contains them *firstly* as *properties* or *predicates*; but *secondly*, held as yet only in *immediate* individuality, they are also as *matters* indifferent to one another; *thirdly*, they are relationships to the unbound elements and processes of the individual body with them.

In the case of the old, universal thought that every body consists of the four elements, or of the more recent *Paracelsian* one that it consists of mercury or fluidity, sulfur or oil, and salt, and of many other thoughts of this kind, the refutation has been easy *in the first place*, in that one

[p. 171]

wanted to understand by those names the individual empirical stuffs that are first of all designated by such names. It is not to be mistaken, however, that they contained, and were meant to express, far more essentially the determinations of the Concept, and it is therefore rather the violence that is to be admired with which thought, in such sensuous things, cognized and held fast only its own determination and the universal significance. *Secondly*, such a grasping and determining, since it has reason as its source, which lets itself neither be led astray by the sensuous play of appearance and its confusedness, nor even be brought into forgetfulness of itself, is infinitely elevated above the thoughtless searching out and the chaotic reciting of the *properties* of bodies. Here it still counts as a merit and a distinction to have set out after something *particular*, instead of tracing it back to the universal and the Concept, and cognizing the latter therein.

§. 246.

The body individualizes a) the external self of *light*, at its darkness, into a specific clouding of the same, into *color*; b) *air*, as the abstract, selfless universality, into the simplicity of its specific process, or in *smell* the specific individuality of the body is rather in its simplicity, itself only as process; c) *water*, the abstract neutrality, it individualizes into the determinate neutrality of saltiness, acidity, and so on, into *taste*.

§. 247.

Through this their universal terrestrial totality the bodies are first of all in a superficial process against the elements, for their individual nature consists

[p. 172]

in being closed off against them. But as singular ones they also stand in relationship to one another, and indeed, apart from mechanical behavior, as particular individualities.

§. 248.

First of all, as *independent* ones against one another, they *maintain* themselves, and as, in the mechanical relationship, they make their self known in ideal motion, as a vibrating within itself, as sound, so they now step forth against one another in real selfhood, in the *electrical* relationship.

§. 249.

The *being-for-itself* that they manifest in contact is posited through the difference of the other in each, and is therefore not free, but an opposed *tension*, in which, however, not the nature of the body enters in its entire determinacy, but only the reality of its abstract self, a *light*, and indeed an opposed one, produces itself. The sublation of the diremption, the other moment of this process, has an indifferent light as its product, which, as incorporeal, vanishes immediately, and outside this abstract physical appearance therefore has, chiefly only, the mechanical effect of the shaking.

As is well known, the earlier difference, bound to a determinate sensuous existence, between *glass*- and *resin-electricity* has been idealized, through completed empirical science, into the *difference of thought* between *positive* and *negative* electricity; — a remarkable example of how empirical science, which at first wants to grasp and hold fast the universal in *sensuous* form, sublates itself. — If much has been said in recent times about the *polarization of light*, then

[p. 173]

this expression would have been reserved with greater right for electricity than for *Malus’s* phenomena, in which transparent media, reflecting surfaces and the different positions of these toward one another, as well as a determinate angle of incidence of the light, are so many other different circumstances that show no difference *in the light* itself, but only in its shining. — The conditions under which positive and negative electricity emerge, the smoother or duller surface, e.g., a breath, and so on, prove the superficiality of the electrical process and how little the concrete physical nature of the body enters into it. In just the same way the faint coloring of the two electrical lights, smell, taste, show only the beginning of a corporeality at the abstract self of light, in which the process remains. The negativity that is the sublation of the opposed tension is chiefly a *shock*; — the self that posits itself as identical with itself remains, even as such, within the *ideal* sphere of space and time and *mechanism*. Light has hardly a beginning of materializing itself into heat, and the *ignition* that can arise from the so-called discharge is (*Berthollet Statique Chimique Ière Partie, Sect. III. not. XI.*) more a direct *effect* of the shaking than the consequence of a realization of light into fire. — *Galvanism* is the electrical process made more *permanent*; it has permanence as the contact of two different, non-brittle bodies, which, *for the sake of their fluid nature* (the so-called electrical conductivity of the metals), give immediate determinate being, against one another, to their *whole* difference, and, for the sake of their *solidity* and of the

[p. 174]

superficiality of this relation, maintain themselves and their *tension* against one another. The galvanic process comes about only through this particular peculiarity of bodies of a more concrete and more corporeal nature, and makes the transition to the chemical process.

§. 250.

The individuality of the body is the negative unity of the Concept, which is absolutely not an immediate and unmoved universal, but one that posits itself only through the mediation of the process; the body is therefore product, and its shape a presupposition by which, rather, the end into which it passes over is presupposed. — *Particularization*, however, does not remain standing at the merely inert diversity and opposition, only as tension and difference of pure selfhood, but, since the particular properties are only the reality of this simple Concept, the body of their soul, of *light*, the *whole* corporeality enters into the tension and the process, which is the *formation* of the individual body, *individuation*; — the *chemical process*.

##### c) The Process of Individuation.

§. 251.

The *chemical process* has its products as its presupposition, and therefore begins 1) from their immediacy. *Immediate* according to the Concept is the particularized body, insofar as its properties or matters have been unified into *simple determination* and have become equal to the simplicity of specified gravity, of density. *Metality* is this compactness, through which particularity is fluid, and capable of positing itself, in undivided wholeness and universality, into

[p. 175]

the determinate difference against another.

§. 252.

The middle, through which the *Concept*, which is in itself the *unity* of both and the *essence* of each, joins together these compact differences with its *reality*, — or posits the difference of each of the two as one with the difference of the other, and thereby becomes *real* as totality of its Concept, is first of all, over against the immediate solidity of the extremes, the *abstract neutrality*, the element of *water*. The process itself is the diremption of it into opposed moments through that presupposed difference of the extremes, which therein sublate their abstraction and complete themselves into the unity of their Concept.

§. 253.

The moments into which water dirempts, or, what is the same, the forms under which it is posited, are abstract, because water itself is only a physical *element*, not an individual physical body; — the *chemical elements* of opposition, *oxygen* and *hydrogen*. The metals, however, which have integrated themselves through this, likewise obtain from that abstract middle only an abstract integration; a reality which is as yet only a *being-posited* of difference in them; *oxides*.

Nearest to metality lies the stage of being, as calx, only an oxide, because of the inner indifference of its compact nature; but the impotence of nature to hold fast the determinate Concept lets individual metals also go over so far to opposition that their oxides stand at once on the side of the *acids*. — As is well known, chemistry has come so far as, also not merely in potash

[p. 176]

and soda, even in ammonium, but also in strontian, baryta, indeed in the earths, to present the metallic base, at least in amalgams, and thereby to cognize these bodies as oxides. — Moreover, the chemical elements are such abstractions that in the gaseous form, in which they present themselves for themselves, they interpenetrate one another like light, and their materiality and impenetrability, notwithstanding ponderability, shows itself here heightened to immateriality. Furthermore, oxygen and hydrogen have a determination so little independent of the individuality of the bodies that oxygen determines bases just as well to oxides and to the alkaline side in general as to the opposite one, that of acid, just as, conversely, in hydrothionic acid the determination to acid shows itself as hydrogenization.

§. 254.

Over against the *compact* indifference of particularized corporeality stands physical *brittleness*, the being-gathered-together of particularity into the self-like unity (ore, as unification of sulfur and metal, presents the totality). This brittleness is the *real possibility* of ignition, to which the actuality of the self-consuming being-for-itself, *fire*, is still something external. It mediates the *internal difference* of the combustible body, through the physical element of abstract negativity, *air*, with *being-posited* or reality, and inspirits it into *acid*. Air, however, is thereby dirempted into this its negative principle, oxygen, and into the dead positive residuum, into *nitrogen*.

§. 255.

The chemical elements, the abstraction of indifference, *nitrogen*, and the two of opposition,

[p. 177]

of the difference that is for itself, *oxygen*, the burning, and of the indifference that belongs to opposition or is for itself, *hydrogen*, the combustible, have the abstraction of their *individual* element in *carbon*.

§. 256.

2) The two products of the abstract processes, *acid* and the *calcareous* or *alkaline*, are no longer in mere diversity, but in proper and (concentrated acid and potash inspirited to causticity) therefore incapable of subsisting for themselves, and only the unrest of sublating themselves and positing themselves as identical with their opposites. This unity, in which their Concept is realized, is the neutral, the *salt*.

§. 257.

3) In the salt the shaped and concrete body is as product of its process. The behavior of various such ones toward one another concerns in part the closer particularization of the bodies, from which the so-called *elective affinities* arise. In general, however, these processes, since the extremes entering into them are not abstract bodies, are for themselves more real ones. As to what is peculiar to them, they are diremptions of the neutral into the abstractions from whose process they were produced, *regressions* to the oxides and acids, and further also, immediately and in more abstract forms, to the indifferent bases, which in this way present themselves as products.

In empirical chemistry it is chiefly a matter of the *particularity of the products*, which are then ordered according to superficial abstract determinations. In this order there appear metals, oxygen, hydrogen, etc., earths, sulfur, phosphorus as *simple* chemical

[p. 178]

bodies alongside one another; just as chaotically abstract and more real processes are placed on the same level. If a scientific form is to enter into this medley, every product is to be determined according to the stage of the process from which it essentially emerges and which gives it its peculiar significance; and it is just as essential to distinguish the stages of abstraction or reality of the process. *Animal* and *vegetable* substances belong in any case to an entirely different order, and their nature can so little be comprehended from the chemical process that they are rather destroyed by it, and only the path of their death is grasped therein. These substances, however, should serve most of all to counteract the metaphysics that is dominant in chemistry as in physics, namely the thoughts, or rather wild representations, of the *unalterability* of the *stuffs*, of their *composition* and of *consisting* of them. But we see it generally admitted that the chemical stuffs lose in union the *properties* they show in separation, and yet see the representation hold good that without the properties they are the same things as they are with them, and as things with these properties are not first products of the process. — An important step toward the simplification of the particularities in the elective affinities has been made through the law found by *Richter* and *Guyton Morveau*, that *neutral compounds undergo no alteration in respect of the state of saturation when they are mixed through solution and the acids exchange their bases with one another*. Connected with this is the scale of the quantities of acids

[p. 179]

and alkalis, according to which each single acid has a particular relationship, for its saturation, to that alkaline one, yet in such a way that for every other acid, whose quantitative unity is different only from that of the rest, the alkalis now have among themselves the same relationship to their saturation as to the rest of the acids, so that the acids too show among themselves a constant relationship toward every different alkaline one. — Since the chemical process, moreover, has its determination in the Concept, the empirical conditions of a particular form of it, as with electricity, are not such *firm* sensuous determinations and not such *abstract* moments as one imagines, e.g., in the case of elective affinity; *Berthollet*, in his celebrated work *Statique chimique*, has compiled and investigated the circumstances that bring about an alteration in the results of chemical action — results that are supposed to flow only from conditions of affinity, which one regards as constant and firmly determined laws; he says that the superficiality that science obtains through these explanations is regarded chiefly as progress on its part.

§. 258.

The chemical process is indeed, in general, life, whereby the individual body is sublated and produced in its immediacy, so that the Concept no longer remains inner necessity, but comes to *appearance*. But it comes also only to this, not to *objectivity*. — This process is a finite and transitory one, because the single body is immediate individuality, and so a limited particularity, and the process thereby has immediate and contingent conditions. In the neutral

[p. 180]

the difference and the fire are extinguished; it does not kindle itself within itself to disunion; just as the different exists at first in indifferent independence, does not stand for itself in relation with one another, nor inspirit itself.

The chemical phenomena — that, e.g., in the process an oxide is reduced to a lower degree of oxidation, at which it can combine with the acting acid, and a part of it, on the other hand, is more strongly oxidized — induce chemists to apply the determination of *purposiveness* in the explanation; — in which, thereby, the self-determination of the Concept in realization lies.

§. 259.

In the process the body shows, both in its coming-to-be and in its passing-away, the transience of its immediate individuality, and presents itself as a moment of the *universal* one; in the latter the Concept has its reality corresponding to it, a concrete universality that has come to be out of particularization, which thus contains within itself the conditions and moments of the total syllogism that fall asunder in the immediate process of chemism; — the *organism*.

#### Part Three.

### Organic Physics.

§. 260.

The real totality of the individual body, in that it has made its particularity into a product and has just as much sublated it, has thereby raised itself into the first ideality of nature, so that, however, it has become a *filled* one, and essentially *self-like* and *subjective*. The *Idea* has thus come to existence, at first to the immediate one, to *life*. This is a) as shape,

[p. 181]

the universal image of life; the *geological* organism; b) as particular or formal subjectivity, the *vegetable* one, c) as singular concrete subjectivity, *animal* nature.

A.

#### Geological Nature.

§. 261.

The universal system of individual bodies is the *earth*, which in the chemical process has, at first, its abstract individuality in particularization, but, as totality of the same, is the infinite relation to itself, a universal process kindling itself, — the immediate subject and product of the same. But as the immediate totality that the subjective totality has presupposed to itself, the earth-body is only the *shape* of the organism.

§. 262.

The members of this organism therefore do not contain the universality of the process within themselves; they are the particular individuals, and make up a *system*, whose formations present themselves as members of the unfolding of an underlying Idea, whose *formative process* is a *past* one.

§. 263.

The powers of this process, which nature leaves behind beyond the earth as independent entities, are the connection and the position of the earth in the solar system, its solar, lunar and cometary life, the inclination of its axis to the orbit, and the magnetic axis. In closer relation to these axes and their polarization stands the distribution of sea and land, whose continuous extension in the north,

[p. 182]

the division and pointed narrowing of the parts toward the south, the further separation into an old and a new world, and the subsequent distribution of the former into the parts of the world, differing from one another, and from the new world, through their physical, organic and anthropological character, to which a still younger and more immature one attaches itself; — the mountain ranges, etc.

§. 264.

The physical organization indicates a graded progression from the *granitic* mountain core, which presents a triad of determinations within itself, of which the other formations are partly transitions and modifications, in which its totality remains the existing foundation, only as one more unequal and more formless within itself, partly a stepping-asunder of its moments into more determinate difference and into more abstract mineral moments, the metals and the oryctognostic objects in general, until they lose themselves in mechanical layerings and in alluvial deposits lacking immanent configuration.

§. 265.

This crystal of life, the dead-lying organism of the earth, which has its *Concept* in the sidereal connection, but its process as a presupposed past, is the immediate subject of the meteorological process, the subject which is, as this organized whole, in its complete determinacy. In this objective subject the previously elemental process is now the objective and individual one, — the sublation of that immediacy, whereby the *universal* individuality now becomes *for itself* and life becomes the *living being*, i.e., *actual*. The first actual vitality that the fertile earth brings forth is *vegetable* nature.

[p. 183]

B.

#### Vegetable Nature.

§. 266.

The universality of life and its singularity are immediately *identical* in *immediate vitality*. The process of articulation and self-preservation of the vegetable subject is therefore a coming-outside-itself, and a falling apart into several individuals, for which the one whole individual is more the mere soil than their subjective unity. Furthermore, for that reason the *difference of the organic parts* is only a superficial metamorphosis, and the one can easily pass over into the function of the other.

§. 267.

The process of configuration and of reproduction of the single individual coincides in this way with the process of the genus; and because the self-like universality, the subjective one of individuality, is not separated from the real particularization but is merely sunk into it, the plant has no motion from its place, nor an interrupting intussusception, but a continuously flowing nourishment; it does not relate itself to an individualized inorganic, but to the universal elements; nor is it capable of feeling and of animal heat.

§. 268.

Insofar, however, as life is essentially the Concept, which realizes itself only through self-disunion and re-uniting, the processes of the plant also fall asunder. 1) Its inner *process of configuration* is, however, partly, as positive, only immediate transformation of the inflows of nourishment into the specific nature of the plant species. Partly this process is, as mediation, for the sake of its

[p. 184]

essential simplicity, the *disunion*, on the one hand into the *abstract universal* of the individuality inseparable within itself, as into the *negative* of vitality, lignification; on the other hand, however, as the side of *singularity* and *vitality*, immediately the process that specifies itself *outward*.

§. 269.

2) This is the unfolding of the members as organs of the different elemental relationships; the disunion partly into the relationship to the earth and into that which mediates it, the air and water process. Since the plant does not hold back its self, in inner subjective universality, against external singularity, it is, by the light, from which it takes the specific confirmation and *individualization* of its self, just as much torn outward, and knots and branches itself into a multiplicity of individual being.

§. 270.

But because the reproduction of the vegetable individual as a singular one is not the subjective return into itself, a *feeling of self*, but, inwardly, lignification, the production of the plant’s self therewith goes outward. It gives birth to its light out in the *blossom*, in which the neutral, green color is determined to a specific clouding, or else the light is produced pure of the dark, as white color.

§. 271.

In that the plant thus offers up its self in sacrifice, this *externalization* is at the same time the Concept realized through the process, the plant that has produced itself as a whole, but has therein stepped over against itself. This highest point is therefore the beginning of a sexual difference and the indication of the process of the genus.

[p. 185]

§. 272.

3) The process of the genus, as distinguished from the process of configuration and of reproduction of the individual, is in the actuality of vegetable nature a superfluity, because those processes are immediately also a falling apart into many individuals. But in the Concept it is, as the subjectivity that has gone together with itself, the *universality*, in which the plant sublates the *immediate singularity* of its organic life, and thereby grounds the transition into the higher organism.

C.

#### The Animal Organism.

§. 273.

Organic individuality is *subjectivity* only insofar as its singularity is not merely immediate actuality, but just as much sublated, and is as a concrete moment of universality, and the organism in its process outward retains the self-like sun inwardly. This is *animal* nature, which, in the actuality and externality of singularity, is, just as much, on the other hand, immediately singularity reflected into itself, subjective universality that is within itself.

§. 274.

The animal has contingent *self-motion*, because its subjectivity, like light and fire, an ideality wrested from gravity, — is a free time, which, as at the same time withdrawn from real externality, determines itself, according to inner contingency, even to place. Connected with this is that the animal has *voice*, in that its subjectivity, as being in and for itself, is the dominion of the abstract ideality of time and space, and presents its

[p. 186]

self-motion as the ideal, inner individuality of a free trembling within itself; — animal *heat*, as the continuing process of dissolution of cohesion in the continuing preservation of shape; — *interrupted intussusception*, — but above all *feeling*, as the individuality that is immediately universal to itself in determinacy and distinguishes itself from it as actual.

§. 275.

The animal organism is, as living universality, the Concept, which courses through its three determinations, each of which is the same *total identity* of the substantial unity and at the same time, for itself as determination of form, the *passing over* into the others, so that from the same the totality results; only as this self-reproducing, not as something that is, is the living being.

§. 276.

It is therefore α) its simple, *universal being-within-itself* in its externality, whereby the actual determinacy is received *immediately* as particularity into the *universal*, and this thereby is undivided identity of the subject with itself in that; — *sensibility*; — β) *particularity*, as excitability from without and the reaction, coming from the receiving subject, against it outward. — *irritability*; — γ) the unity of these moments, the *negative* return through the relationship of externality to itself, and thereby generation and positing of itself as a *singular one*, — *reproduction*; inwardly the reality and foundation of the former moments, and *articulation* and *armament* outwardly.

§. 277.

These three moments of the *Concept* have their reality in the three systems, the *nerve*-, *blood*-

[p. 187]

and *digestive system*, of which the first turns itself in the systems of the bones and of the sense organs, the second in the lung and the muscles, outward on two sides; the digestive system, however, as glandular system with skin and cellular tissue, is an *immediate*, vegetative reproduction, but in the system of the viscera proper the *mediating* one. The animal is thereby divided *for itself* into the centers of three systems, (*insectum*) head, chest and abdomen, whereas the extremities, for mechanical motion and grasping, make up the moment of the singularity that posits itself as distinguished outwardly.

§. 278.

The Idea of the living being is the exhibited *unity* of the Concept with its reality; but it is, as *opposition of that subjectivity and objectivity*, essentially only as *process*, — as motion of the abstract relation of the living being to itself, which dirempts itself into particularity, and as return into itself is the negative unity of subjectivity and totality. Each of these moments, however, as a concrete moment of vitality, is itself process, and the whole is the *unity of three processes*.

§. 279.

1) The *abstract* process of living singularity is the *process of configuration within itself*, in which the organism makes its own members into its inorganic nature, into means, feeds off itself, and produces itself, i.e., precisely this totality of articulation itself, so that each member is reciprocally end and means, maintains itself out of the others and against them; — the process that has the simple self-feeling as its result.

[p. 188]

§. 280.

2) The self-feeling of *singularity* is, in its negative return into itself, immediately *excluding* and tensing itself against an inorganic nature as against a *real and external* one. Inasmuch as α) the animal organization is *immediately* reflected into itself in this external relation, this ideal behavior is the *theoretical* process, and indeed the *determinate feeling*, — which differentiates itself into the *multiplicity of senses* of inorganic nature.

§. 281.

The senses and the theoretical processes are therefore 1) the sense of the mechanical sphere, — of *gravity*, of cohesion and its alteration, of heat, *feeling* as such; 2) the senses of *opposition*, of particular *airiness*, and of the likewise realized *neutrality*, of *water*, and of the oppositions of its dissolution; — *smell* and *taste*. 3) The sense of the pure essential, but external *identity*, of the side, not belonging to the heavy material, of *fire*, of *light* and of *color*; — and 4) the sense for the presentation of the *subjective* reality, or of the independent inner ideality of the body standing opposite, the *sense* of *hearing*.

The *triplicity* of the moments of the Concept passes over here into a *quintuplicity* as regards number because the moment of particularity or of opposition is itself a triplicity in its totality, and the animal organism is the reduction of the inorganic nature that has fallen asunder into the infinite unity of subjectivity, but in this at the same time its developed totality, whose moments, because it is still *natural* subjectivity, exist particularly. *Universality*, as the still inward concrete, gravity with its individualized

[p. 189]

determinations, has therefore its particular sense in *touch*, the underlying, universal sense, which for that reason is also better called *feeling* in general. *Particularity* is the opposition, and this is the identity and the opposition itself; to it therefore belongs the sense of *light*, as of the abstract, but for that very reason itself *determinate* identity that makes up one side of the opposition; further, the two senses of the opposition itself as such, of *air* and of *water*, of both, as of the rest, in their embodied specification and individualization. To the sense of *singularity* belongs the subjectivity that makes itself known as pure subjectivity being within itself, *sound*.

§. 282.

β) The *real process* with the inorganic nature likewise begins with *feeling*, namely with the feeling of real externality, and thereby of the *negation* of the subject, which is at the same time the positive relation to itself and the *certainty* of this relation against this its negation, — with the feeling of *lack* and the *drive* to sublate it, — which is the condition of *being excited* from outside.

Only a living being feels *lack*; for only it is in nature the *Concept*, which is the unity *of itself and of its determinate opposite*; thereby it is *subject*. Where there is a *limitation*, it is a negation only *for a third*, an external reflection; *lack*, however, it is insofar as in *one* just as much the *being-beyond* is present, the *contradiction* as such is posited. Such a one, which is capable of having and enduring the contradiction of itself within itself, is the *subject*; this constitutes its *infinity*.

[p. 190]

— When *finite* reason is spoken of, it proves precisely thereby that it is infinite, in that it determines itself as *finite*; for negation is finitude, lack only for that which is the *being-sublated* of it, the *infinite* relation to itself. — Thoughtlessness, however, stays at the abstraction of the *limitation*, and in life, where the *Concept* itself enters into *existence*, it likewise does not grasp it, but holds to determinations of representation, such as *drive*, *instinct*, *need*, etc. — That for the organism the determination of *being excited* by *external potencies* has taken the place of the *acting-upon* by *external causes* is an important step in the true representation of it. — Idealism begins in this: that in general nothing can have a positive relation to the living being whose possibility this would not be in and for itself, i.e., which would not be determined through the Concept, thus absolutely immanent to the subject. But as unphilosophical as any scientific brew of determinations of reflection is the introduction of such formal and material relationships in the *theory of excitation*, as have long passed for philosophical; e.g., the wholly abstract opposition of *receptivity* and *capacity for effect*, which as factors are supposed to stand to one another in inverse ratio of magnitude, whereby all difference to be grasped in the organism has fallen into the *formalism* of merely *quantitative* diversity, *heightening* and *diminution*, *strengthening* and *weakening*, i.e., into the highest possible *conceptlessness*. A theory of medicine that is built on these barren determinations of the understanding is completed with half a dozen

[p. 191]

propositions, and it is no wonder if it found rapid diffusion and many adherents. The occasion for this aberration of philosophy, with which nature had only just begun to make friends, lay in the fundamental error that, after the absolute had been determined as the absolute indifference of the subjective and the objective, all determination was now supposed to be only a *quantitative* difference. The absolute form, the *Concept*, and vitality have rather the qualitative difference, consuming itself within itself, alone as its soul. Because this true infinite negativity had not been cognized, it was thought that the absolute identity of life, as in Spinoza the attributes and modes occur in an external understanding, could not be held fast without making the difference into a merely external one of reflection; whereby life lacks the *springing point* of selfhood, the principle of self-motion, diremption of itself, singularity in general. — To be held entirely unphilosophical and crudely sensuous, further, is the procedure that wanted to give *formal* determinations a *real* meaning, and in place of determinations of the Concept even put *carbon* and *nitrogen*, oxygen and hydrogen, and determined the previously intensive difference, now, as the *more* or *less* of the one and the other stuff, but the effective and positive relationship of the external stimuli as an *adding* of a lacking stuff; — e.g., in an *asthenia*, — a nervous fever, *nitrogen* is said to have the upper hand in the organism, because the brain and the nerve in general are supposed to be the *potentiated* nitrogen, since *chemical* analysis shows it as the *principal constituent* of these organic formations; the addition of *carbon* is said to be hereby

[p. 192]

indicated, in order to restore the equilibrium of these *stuffs*, health. The remedies that have proved effective against nervous fever in an empirical manner are, for this very reason, regarded as belonging to the side of *carbon*, and such a superficial putting together and opining is passed off as *construction* and *proving*. — The crudity consists in this, that the external *caput mortuum*, the dead stuff, into which chemistry has killed a deadened life for the second time, is taken for the *essence* of a living organ, indeed for its *Concept*. This latter then grounds the so highly convenient formalism of using such sensuous materials as the chemical stuffs, further relationships that belong to the sphere of inorganic nature, such as the north and south polarity of magnetism, or the differences of this itself and of the electrical moment, instead of determinations of the Concept, and of comprehending and developing the natural universe in such a way that onto the spheres and differences that it presents, such a ready-made schema of north- and south-, west- and east-polarity, or whatever else it may be, is externally attached. A great multiplicity of forms is possible here, since it remains optional to adopt for the schema the determinations of the totality, as they appear in the *chemical* sphere, e.g., oxygen, hydrogen, etc., and to transfer them to magnetism, mechanism, vegetation, animality, etc., or else to take magnetism, electricity, the masculine and the feminine, contraction and expansion, etc., in general to reach for oppositions of any other sphere and to use them in the rest.

[p. 193]

§. 283.

Need and excitation are directed to the relationship of the universal and particular *mechanism*, (sleeping and waking), to the process with *air*, (respiratory and cutaneous process), with *water*, (thirst) and with individualized *earth*, namely particular formations of it (hunger, § 275). Life, the subject of these moments of the totality, tenses itself within itself as Concept and into the moments as a reality external to it, and is the continuing conflict in which it overcomes this externality. Because the animal, as essentially a singular, can do this only in the singular, this objectification of itself is not adequate to its Concept, and therefore continually goes back out of satisfaction into the state of need.

§. 284.

The *mechanical mastering* of the external Object is only the beginning of the uniting of it with the animal living being. Since this is thereby a subject, the simple negativity of the punctual one, *assimilation* can be of neither a mechanical nor, just as little, of a chemical nature, since in these processes the stuffs as well as the conditions and the activity remain external to one another, and lack the living absolute unity.

§. 285.

It is, first, because the living being is the *universal* power over its external nature opposed to it, the *immediate* going-together of what is taken up inwardly with animality; an infection with this and *simple transformation* (§ 278). Second, since the power of the living being is the relation to itself through mediation, assimilation is *digestion*; — opposition of the subject against

[p. 194]

its immediate assimilating, so that the former, as negative, excites itself against it, and appears as the process of opposition, process of the animal *water* (of the gastric and pancreatic juice, of animal lymph in general) and of the animal *fire* (of the *bile*, in which the *being-turned-back-into-itself* of the organism, from its concentration, which it has in the spleen, is determined to *being-for-itself* and to active consumption).

§. 286.

This animal excitation is at first turned against the *external* potency, which, however, through the infection (§ 277) has been placed immediately on the side of the organism. But that excitation, as the opposition and the being-for-itself of the process *against* the *universality* and *simple* relation of the living being to itself, likewise has the determination of *externality*. Both together, appearing at first on the side of the *subject* as *means*, therefore actually constitute the Object and the negative against the organism, which it has to overcome and digest.

§. 287.

This inversion of the view is the reflection of the organism into itself, the negation of its own negativity or outwardly directed activity. As natural being, the singularity that it attains herein closes together with its universality as *disjunctive* in such a way that it, on the one hand, excretes from itself the first negation, the externality of the Object and its own activity, and, on the other hand, as immediately identical with this, has reproduced for itself its negation in this means; in that the process going outward is thus transformed out of itself and has passed over into the first formal elements of reproduction.

[p. 195]

The main moment in digestion is the *immediate* effect of life, as the *power* over its inorganic Object, which it opposes to itself, and presupposes as its exciting stimulus only and insofar as it is *in itself* identical with it. This effect is *infection* and immediate transformation. *Spallanzani’s* and other experiments, and recent physiology, have proved, also in an empirical manner, and shown in accordance with the Concept, this immediacy, with which the living being as *universal*, without further mediation, through its mere contact and taking up of the foodstuff into its heat and sphere in general, continues itself into the same, against the mediation represented as a mechanical, fictitious *sorting out and separating off* of already *homogeneous*, usable parts, as well as against the mediation represented as a *chemical* process. The investigations, on the other hand, of the mediating actions have not yielded *more determinate* moments of this transformation (such as a series of *fermentations* presents itself, e.g., in vegetable stuffs); on the contrary they have shown, e.g., that already from the stomach onward much passes over into the mass of the juices without having had to go through the remaining stages of mediation, that the pancreatic juice is nothing further than saliva, and the pancreas can very well be dispensed with, etc. The last product, the chyle, which the *thoracic duct* takes up and *pours* into the blood, is the same lymph that each single viscus and organ excretes, that the skin and the lymphatic system everywhere obtain in the immediate process of transformation, and which is everywhere already prepared. The lowly animal organizations, which anyway are nothing but a lymph coagulated into a membranous point or little tube — a simple intestinal canal — do not go

[p. 196]

beyond this immediate transformation. The mediated digestive process, in the higher animal organizations, is, with regard to its *peculiar product*, just such a *superfluity* as, in plants, their seed-production mediated through so-called sexual difference. — The *faeces* show, especially in children, in whom the increase of matter nevertheless stands out most, frequently the greatest part of the foodstuffs unchanged, mixed chiefly with animal stuffs, the *bile*, phosphorus and the like, and as the principal effect of the organism, to overcome and remove its own productions. — The syllogism of the organism is therefore not the syllogism of *external purposiveness*, because it does not stop at directing its activity and form against the external Object, but makes this process itself, which because of its externality is on the point of becoming mechanical and chemical, into the Object, and, since it is nature, in the coming together of itself therein with itself, is disjunctive activity, which removes this process from itself, abstracts from its *anger* against the Object, this one-sided subjectivity, and thereby becomes *for itself* what it is in itself, — identity of its Concept and its reality, — thus finds the end and product of its activity as that which it already is from the beginning and originally. Hereby *satisfaction* is *rational*; the process going into external difference turns over into the process of the organism with itself, and the result is not the mere production of a means, but of the end.

[p. 197]

§. 288.

Through the process with the external nature the animal gives to the certainty of itself, to its subjective Concept, truth, objectivity, as *singular* individual; and is *production* of itself as much as self-preservation, or *reproduction* as production of its first Concept. Thereby the latter goes together with itself, and is as *concrete universal*, *genus*. The *disjunction* of the singularity that finds itself in the genus is the *sexual difference*, the relation of the subject to an Object that is itself such a subject.

§. 289.

This relation is *drive*; the *singular* as such is not adequate to its genus; nor does this adequacy fall into an external reflection; the individual, in its limitation of the genus, is at the same time the genus’s identical relation to itself in one unity; it thus has the *feeling* of this *lack* and is in natural difference of sex.

§. 290.

3) The *process of the genus* has, as chemism does in the inorganic, the universal Concept, as the essence of the individuals, as the universal extreme; the tension of the same against the inadequacy of their singular actuality drives them, each only in the other of its genus to have its self-feeling and to integrate itself through union with it; through this mediation the concrete universal is closed together with itself and gives itself singular actuality.

§. 291.

This product is the *negative identity* which is the *genus that has become*, a sexless

[p. 198]

life; but according to the *natural* side only *in itself* this genus and falling asunder against the individuals who have perished in it, and therefore itself a *singular*, which has in itself the determination of such difference and transience. But at the same time in this new life, in which singularity is sublated, the same subjectivity is *positively* preserved, and in this its return into itself the *genus as such* has stepped *for itself* into reality, and has become something higher than nature.

§. 292.

The *different formations and orders of the animals* have as their ground the universal *type of the animal*, determined through the Concept, which nature presents partly in the various stages of its development, from the simplest organization up to the most perfect, in which it is the tool of spirit, partly under the various circumstances and conditions of elemental nature.

The Concept of the animal has the *Concept* itself as its essence, because it is the actuality of the Idea of life. The nature of its *universality* makes it possible that it has a simpler and more developed determinate being, as well as one more or less corresponding to it. From determinate being itself, therefore, the Concept cannot be grasped in its determinacy. The classes in which it appears developed and completely presented in its moments appear as a particular *determinate being* against the rest, and also in those it can have a *bad* determinate being. But whether determinate being is *bad*, to this judgment the Concept is already presupposed. If, as is usual, determinate being was presupposed in order to cognize from it the nature of the animal and its essential determinations, or the essential organs of a class, then it comes

[p. 199]

on this empirical path to no firm determination, and all particular properties show themselves also in such a way that they can be lacking; e.g., the acephala have been cited as an instance that the human being could live even without a brain. — In *zoology*, as in the natural sciences in general, it has been more a matter of finding secure and simple *marks* for subjective cognition. Only since this end of so-called artificial systems has been lost sight of more in the cognition of animals has a larger view opened up, and among the empirical sciences there is hardly one that in recent times has gained such great expansions — not preeminently in the mass of observations, for no science has lacked that, but in respect of its material having worked itself toward rationality — as zoology, through its auxiliary science, *comparative anatomy*. Partly, in the individual formations, the *habitus*, as a connection determining the construction of all parts, has been made the main matter, so that the great founder of this science, *Cuvier*, could boast of being able to cognize the essential nature of the whole animal from a single bone. Partly the universal type of the animal has been followed through the various formations, however imperfect and disparate they appear, and in the scarcely beginning indication, as well as in the intermixture of organs and functions, their significance has been recognized, and by this very means it has been raised above and out of particularity into its universality. A main aspect of this consideration is the cognition of how nature forms this organism onto, and fits it snugly to, the particular element into which it throws it, to climate, circle of nourishment, in general to the world in

[p. 200]

which it is absorbed (which can also be a single plant genus or other animal genus). — It is the *immediacy* of the Idea of life that the Concept, although it alone is that which is determined in and for itself, does not as such *exist in life*, its determinate being therefore submits itself to the manifold conditions and circumstances of external nature, and can appear in the most meager forms, and the *fertility* of the earth lets it sprout *everywhere*. The animal world can therefore, almost less than the other spheres of nature, present a rational system of organization independent in itself, adhere to the *forms* that would be determined by the Concept, and preserve them against the imperfection and intermixture of conditions from mingling, stunting and transitions. — This weakness of the Concept, which in the animal does not exist in its firm, independent *freedom*, also subjects the existing genus entirely to the changes of the external universal life of nature, whose alternation it lives through along with it, and which in its singular existence is a continual violence against the single animal. Animal life therefore shows itself in general as a *sick* one; as also its feeling, as an *insecure*, *anxious*, and *unhappy* one.

§. 293.

The single organism can, because of the externality of its determinate being, also become non-corresponding to its determination. It finds itself in the state of *disease*, insofar as one of its systems or organs, excited into conflict with an inorganic potency, establishes itself for itself and persists in its particular activity against the activity of the whole, whose fluidity and process passing through all the moments is thereby inhibited.

[p. 201]

§. 294.

The peculiar appearance of disease is therefore that the identity of the whole organic Concept presents itself as *successive* course of the vital motion through its different moments, sensibility, irritability and reproduction, as *fever*, which, against the *isolated* activity, as the course of the totality, is just as much the attempt at and the beginning of *healing*.

§. 295.

The remedy excites the organism to remove the inorganic potency with which the activity of the single organ or system is entangled and thereby isolated, — but essentially to sublate the excitation in which the formal activity of the *whole* is fixed, and to restore fluidity into the whole. The remedy effects this by being a stimulus, but one still harder to assimilate and to overcome, against which the organism is compelled to summon up its whole force. In that it thus directs itself against something external, it has stepped out of the limitedness that had become identical with it, in which it had been caught up.

Medicines must in general be regarded as something *indigestible*. But the determination of indigestibility is only relative, though not in the indeterminate sense in which it is accustomed to be taken, according to which easily digestible means what weaker constitutions can tolerate. Such easily digestible matter is, for the more vigorous individuality, rather indigestible. The true *relativity*, that of the *Concept*, which has its actuality in life, consists, expressed in the quantitative regard that holds here, — in a *homogeneity* all the higher the *higher and more independent* the opposition is; the highest *qualitative*

[p. 202]

form of it in the living being has shown itself to be the relationship of the sexes, in which independent individualities are as identical. — For the lower animal formations, which have come to no *difference* within themselves, the *neutral* that is without individuality, water, is the digestible, as it is for the plant; — for children the digestible is partly the wholly *homogeneous* animal lymph, mother’s milk, something already digested, or rather only immediately and in general transformed into animality and not further differentiated within it itself; — partly, of different substances, those that have matured least toward individuality. Substances of this kind are, on the other hand, indigestible for natures that have grown strong.
